Characterizations of uniformly differentiable co-horizontal intrinsic graphs in Carnot groups Thanks: G.A., D.D.D., S.D. and E.L.D. are partially supported by the Academy of Finland (grant 288501 ‘ Geometry of subRiemannian groups ’ and by grant 322898 ‘ Sub-Riemannian Geometry via Metric-geometry and Lie-group Theory ’) and by the European Research Council (ERC Starting Grant 713998 GeoMeG ‘ Geometry of Metric Groups ’).
Gioacchino Antonelli, Daniela Di Donato, Sebastiano Don and Enrico Le Donne

Abstract
. In arbitrary Carnot groups we study intrinsic graphs of maps with horizontal target. These graphs are C H 1 C^{1}_{\rm H} regular exactly when the map is uniformly intrinsically differentiable. Our first main result characterizes the uniformly intrinsic differentiability by means of Hölder properties along the projections of left-invariant vector fields on the graph. We strengthen the result in step-2 Carnot groups for intrinsic real-valued maps by only requiring horizontal regularity. We remark that such a refinement is not possible already in the easiest step-3 group. As a by-product of independent interest, in every Carnot group we prove an area-formula for uniformly intrinsically differentiable real-valued maps. We also explicitly write the area element in terms of the intrinsic derivatives of the map.

53C17, 22E25, 28A75, 49N60, 49Q15, 26A16.

1.
Introduction

1.1.
An historical account of the notion of
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surface in Carnot groups

In these last twenty years there has been an increasing interest in a fine study of parametrized intrinsically regular surfaces in sub-Riemannian settings. The search for a good such notion was motivated by a negative result obtained in
[
AK99
]
. Indeed, in the reference the authors show that the sub-Riemannian Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{1}
is not
k
-rectifiable in Federer’s sense
[
Fed69
]
, for every
k\geq 2
.

A notion of intrinsic
C^{1}
regular surface was firstly introduced and studied in
[
FSSC01
]
, and then in
[
FSSC03a
]
in arbitrary Carnot groups
\mathbb{G}
. Initially, the authors only took
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-hypersurfaces into account. A first step toward a general definition of
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surfaces in arbitrary codimensions was done in
[
FSSC07
, Definition 3.1, Definition 3.2]
in the setting of Heisenberg groups
\mathbb{H}^{n}
. Then a general notion of
(\mathbb{G},\mathbb{M})
regular surface, where
\mathbb{G}
and
\mathbb{M}
are Carnot groups, was proposed by Magnani in
[
Mag06
, Definition 3.5]
. According to the latter definition, a
(\mathbb{G},\mathbb{M})
regular surface is locally the zero level set of an
\mathbb{M}
-valued
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-function defined on an open subset of
\mathbb{G}
and whose intrinsic Pansu differential
\de_{\rm P}\!f
is surjective.

A first natural question one could try to answer is whether it is possible to (locally) write a
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surface as an intrinsic graph of a function. An intrinsic graph in a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
is the set of points of the form
p\cdot\varphi(p)
, given a function
\varphi\colon U\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
, where
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
are homogeneous and complementary subgroups, namely
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{W}\cdot\mathbb{L}
, and
\mathbb{W}\cap\mathbb{L}=\{e\}
.
The answer to this question is affirmative for
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-hypersurfaces. Moreover the graphing function is intrinsically Lipschitz according to the definition of
[
FSSC06
,
FS16
]
, while it is in general neither Euclidean Lipschitz nor Lipschitz with respect to any sub-Riemannian distance, see
[
FSSC06
, Example 3.3 & Proposition 3.4]
.

A more general implicit function theorem was proved by Magnani in
[
Mag13
, Theorem 1.4]
. This theorem holds for arbitrary
(\mathbb{G},\mathbb{M})
regular surfaces with the additional property that
{\rm Ker}(\de_{\rm P}\!f(x))
has a complementary subgroup in
\mathbb{G}
, where
x
is the point around which we want to parametrize the surface. From
[
Mag13
, Eq. (1.8)]
it follows that this parametrization is intrinsically Lipschitz. The validity of the implicit function theorem leads the way to a very general definition of
(\mathbb{G},\mathbb{M})
regular sets for
\mathbb{G}
, where
\mathbb{M}
is just a homogeneous group, given in
[
Mag13
, Definition 10.2]
. We will not deal with objects at this level of generality, but we refer the interested reader to
[
Mag13
, Sections 10,11,12]
. The class of intrinsically regular surfaces is also studied in
[
JNGV20
]
, where area and coarea formulae are proved. For an alternative proof of the implicit function theorem, one can also see
[
JNGV20
, Section 2.5]
.

We will mainly deal with co-horizontal
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surfaces, that have been studied in
[
Koz15
,
DD18
,
Cor19
]
, see
Section

2.3
.

Definition 1.1

(Co-horizontal
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surface)
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a Carnot group, and let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. We say that
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{G}
is a
co-horizontal
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surface of codimension
k
if, for any
p\in\Sigma
, there exist a neighborhood
U
of
p
and a map
f\in C^{1}_{\rm H}(U;\mathbb{R}^{k})
such that

\Sigma\cap U=\{g\in U:f(g)=0\},

and the Pansu differential
\de_{\rm P}\!f(p)\colon\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
is surjective.

If, morevoer, the subgroup
\mathrm{Ker}(\de_{\rm P}\!f(p))
admits a complementary subgroup, that is horizontal, we say that
\Sigma
is a
co-horizontal
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surface with complemented tangents
. We call
\mathrm{Ker}(\de_{\rm P}\!f(p))
the
homogeneous tangent space to
\Sigma
at
p
. If the previous holds, the homogeneous subgroup at
p
is independent of the choice of
f
, see
[
Mag13
, Theorem 1.7]
.

Uniform intrinsic differentiability of the parametrizing function
. A fine study on the regularity of the parametrizing function of a
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surface has been initiated in
[
ASCV06
]
in the setting of Heisenberg groups
\mathbb{H}^{n}
, for the class of
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-hypersurfaces. For this study in arbitrary
{\rm CC}
-spaces, see also
[
CM06
]
. In
[
ASCV06
]
, the authors introduced the notion of uniform intrinsic differentiability. In this paper we abbreviate “intrinsically differentiable” and “uniformly intrinsically differentiable” with ID and UID, respectively.

From the analytic viewpoint, the notion of (U)ID is defined in a translation invariant way, mimicking the Euclidean notion of derivative. For the sake of exposition, we here recall the definition of ID at the identity. Then, building on this definition, one can define the notion of (U)ID at any point by means of translations, see
Section

2.3
, and
Section

2.3
. In the following,
\|\cdot\|
is a homogeneous norm on
\mathbb{G}
.

Definition 1.2

(Intrinsic differentiability)
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a Carnot group, with identity
e
, and a splitting
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{W}\cdot\mathbb{L}
. Let
e\in U\subseteq\mathbb{W}
be a relatively open subset, and
\varphi\colon U\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
a function with
\varphi(e)=e
.

We say that
{\varphi}
is
intrinsically differentiable at
e
if there exists an intrinsically linear map
\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi_{e}\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
such that

\lim_{\varrho\to 0}\left(\sup\left\{\frac{\|\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi_{e}(b)^{-1}\cdot{\varphi}(b)\|}{\|b\|}:b\in U,0<\|b\|<\varrho\right\}\right)=0,

where we say that a function is
intrinsically linear
if its intrinsic graph is a homogeneous subgroup. The function
\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi_{e}
is called
intrinsic differential
of
\varphi
at
e
.

Building upon the implicit function theorem, the authors in
[
ASCV06
]
prove that in
\mathbb{H}^{n}
the graphing map
\varphi
for a
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-hypersurface is UID. The idea behind this implication is the following: a function
f\in C^{1}_{\rm H}
not only has continuous derivatives, but also its horizontal gradient
\nabla_{\rm H}f
uniformly approximates
f
at first order, see
[
Mag13
, Theorem 1.2]
, and
[
JNGV20
, Proposition 2.4]
. This notion is often referred to as strict differentiability. This fact has a strong analogy with the Euclidean setting. Indeed, in the Euclidean framework, a function
f
with continuous partial derivatives is Fréchet-differentiable, and the proof relies on a use of a mean value inequality, that is exactly what one finds in
[
Mag13
, Theorem 1.2]
, and
[
ASCV06
, Lemma 4.2]
. Eventually, the uniform differentiability of
f
translates into the uniform intrinsic differentiability of
\varphi
.

The fact that the graphing function is UID was proved in the case of co-horizontal
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surfaces in
\mathbb{H}^{n}

[
AS09
]
, and more in general for co-horizontal
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surfaces with complemented tangents in any Carnot groups, in
[
DD18
]
. The inverse implication, i.e., the fact that the graph of a UID function is a
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surface, was firstly shown to be true in
[
ASCV06
,
AS09
]
in the setting of
\mathbb{H}^{n}
, and lately generalized in
[
DD18
]
for arbitrary Carnot groups
\mathbb{G}
to functions with horizontal target, see our
Section

2.3
for a precise statement. Notice that the lack of generality in the statement, namely, the fact that one restricts the target to be horizontal, is due to the fact that a generalized version of Whitney’s extension theorem is not known to be true.

1.2.
Main theorems

Definitions & statements
. For the notation we refer to
Section

2
. Given an arbitrary Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
of step
s
, with layers
V_{i}
, with
1\leq i\leq s
, we fix a splitting
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{W}\cdot\mathbb{L}
, where
\mathbb{L}
is horizontal. We denote by
\|\cdot\|
a (fixed) homogeneous norm on
\mathbb{G}
.

We first aim at characterizing the uniform intrinsic differentiability of a map
{\varphi}\colon U\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
defined on a relatively open subset
U\subseteq\mathbb{W}
. This is done by means of a correct Lipschitz/Hölder property of
\varphi
along the integral curves of projected vector fields on
U\subseteq\mathbb{W}
, that we define here. See also
Section

3.1
.

Definition 1.3

(Projected vector fields)
.

Let
U
be a relatively open subset of
\mathbb{W}
. Given a continuous function
{\varphi}\colon U\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
, for every
W\in\mathrm{Lie}(\mathbb{W})
, we take
D^{\varphi}_{W}
as the continuous vector field on
U
defined by

(1)

D^{\varphi}_{W}(p)\coloneqq\left(\de\!\pi_{\mathbb{W}}\right)_{p\cdot\varphi(p)}W_{p\cdot\varphi(p)}.\qquad\forall p\in U,

where
\pi_{\mathbb{W}}
is the projection on
\mathbb{W}
associated to the splitting
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{W}\cdot\mathbb{L}
. Notice that if
\varphi
is
C^{1}
, then
D^{\varphi}
is a
C^{1}
vector field.

We define the notion of broad* regularity, mimicking the definition in
[
ASCV06
,
BSC10b
]
, and we then introduce the notion of vertically broad* hölder regularity, see
Section

3.4
, and
Section

4.1
, respectively.

Definition 1.4

(Broad* regularity)
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a Carnot group, with splitting
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{W}\cdot\mathbb{L}
, and
\mathbb{L}
horizontal. Let
U
be a relatively open subset in
\mathbb{W}
,
\varphi\colon U\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
be a continuous function, and let
\omega\colon U\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathrm{Lin}({\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})\cap V_{1};\mathbb{L})
be a continuous function with values in the space of linear maps.

We say that
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega

in the broad* sense on
U
if the following holds: for every
a_{0}\in U
, there exist a neighborhood
U_{a_{0}}\Subset U
of
a_{0}
and
T>0
such that, for every
a\in U_{a_{0}}
and every
W\in{\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})\cap V_{1}
with
\|W\|\leq 1
, there exists
an integral curve
\gamma\colon[-T,T]\to U
of
D^{\varphi}_{W}
such that
\gamma(0)=a
and

\varphi(\gamma(s))^{-1}\cdot\varphi(\gamma(t))=\int_{s}^{t}\omega(\gamma(r))(W)\de\!r,\qquad\forall t,s\in[-T,T].

Definition 1.5

(Vertically broad* hölder regularity)
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a Carnot group, with splitting
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{W}\cdot\mathbb{L}
, and
\mathbb{L}
horizontal. Let
U
be a relatively open subset in
\mathbb{W}
, and let
\varphi\colon U\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
be a continuous function.

We say that
\varphi
is
vertically broad* hölder on
U
if the following holds: for every
a_{0}\in U
there exist a neighborhood
U_{a_{0}}\Subset U
of
a_{0}
and
T>0
such that for every
a\in U_{a_{0}}
and every
W\in{\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})\cap V_{d}
, with
d>1
and
\|W\|\leq 1
, there exists
an integral curve
\gamma\colon[-T,T]\to U
of
D_{W}^{\varphi}
such that
\gamma(0)=a
and

\lim_{\varrho\to 0}\left(\sup\left\{\frac{\|\varphi(\gamma(s))^{-1}\cdot\varphi(\gamma(t))\|}{|t-s|^{1/d}}:t,s\in[-T,T],|t-s|\leq\varrho\right\}\right)=0,

and the limit is uniform on
a\in{U}_{a_{0}}
and on
W\in{\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})\cap V_{d}
, with
d>1
, and
\|W\|\leq 1
.

We next state our first main result in a free-coordinate fashion. We refer the reader to
Theorem

4.17
for a coordinate-dependent, though equivalent, statement. We remark that the equivalence (a)
\Leftrightarrow
(b) of the forthcoming
Theorem

1.6
is not in the statement of
Theorem

4.17
, but it is an outcome of
Section

2.3
, and the implicit function theorem
[
Mag13
, Theorem 1.4]
.

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a Carnot group with splitting
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{W}\cdot\mathbb{L}
, and
\mathbb{L}
horizontal. Let
U
be a relatively open subset in
\mathbb{W}
, and let
\varphi\colon U\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
be a continuous function. The following facts are equivalent.

(a)

{\rm graph}(\varphi)
is a co-horizontal
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surface with homogeneous tangent spaces complemented by
\mathbb{L}
(see
Section

1.1
).

(b)

\varphi
is uniformly intrinsically differentiable on
U
(see
Section

1.1
).

(c)

\varphi
is
vertically broad* hölder
on
U
(see
Section

1.2
), and there exists a continuous function
\omega\colon U\to\mathrm{Lin}({\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})\cap V_{1};\mathbb{L})
, such that, for every
a\in U
, there exist
\delta>0
and a family of functions
\{\varphi_{\varepsilon}\in C^{1}(B(a,\delta);\mathbb{L}):\varepsilon\in(0,1)\}
such that, for every
W\in{\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})\cap V_{1}
, one has

(2)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(p)=\varphi(p)\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}(D_{W}^{\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\varphi_{\varepsilon})(p)=\omega(p)(W)\quad\mbox{uniformly in $p\in B(a,\delta)$}.

(d)

\varphi
is
vertically broad* hölder
on
U
(see
Section

1.2
) and there exists a continuous function
\omega\colon U\to\mathrm{Lin}({\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})\cap V_{1};\mathbb{L})
such that
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
in the broad* sense on
U
(see
Section

1.2
).

Our second main result is an improvement of
Theorem

1.6
for step-2 Carnot groups, in the case
\mathbb{L}
is one-dimensional. Again, we here give a coordinate-free statement. We refer the reader to
Theorem

6.17
for a coordinate-dependent, though equivalent, statement. We stress that in the forthcoming theorem we are removing the
vertically broad* hölder
condition of
Theorem

1.6
, and we work with
\mathbb{L}
that is
one-dimensional
. We also stress that in general in the statement of
Theorem

1.6
one cannot remove the vertically broad* hölder condition, see
Section

4.4
, and
[
Koz15
, Example 4.5.1]
for a counterexample in the easiest step-3 group, namely the Engel group.

Theorem 1.7
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a Carnot group of step
2
with a splitting
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{W}\cdot\mathbb{L}
, and
\mathbb{L}

one-dimensional horizontal
. Let
U
be a relatively open subset in
\mathbb{W}
, and let
\varphi\colon U\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
be a continuous function. The following facts are equivalent.

(a)

{\rm graph}(\varphi)
is a
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-hypersurface with homogeneous tangent spaces complemented by
\mathbb{L}
(see
Section

1.1
).

(b)

\varphi
is uniformly intrinsically differentiable on
U
(see
Section

1.1
).

(c)

There exists a continuous map
\omega\colon U\to\mathrm{Lin}({\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})\cap V_{1};\mathbb{L})
, such that, for every
a\in U
, there exist
\delta>0
and a family of functions
\{\varphi_{\varepsilon}\in C^{1}(B(a,\delta);\mathbb{L}):\varepsilon\in(0,1)\}
such that, for every
W\in{\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})\cap V_{1}
, one has

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(p)=\varphi(p)\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}(D_{W}^{\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\varphi_{\varepsilon})(p)=\omega(p)(W)\qquad\mbox{uniformly in $p\in B(a,\delta)$}.

(d)

There exists a continuous function
\omega\colon U\to\mathrm{Lin}({\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})\cap V_{1};\mathbb{L})
such that
D^{\varphi}{\varphi}=\omega
in the broad* sense on
U
(see
Section

1.2
).

The last main result that we state is an area-formula for uniformly differentiable intrinsic real-valued maps, which we believe has its independent interest. We here give a coordinate-free statement. We refer the reader to
Section

4.2
, and
Section

4.2
for a coordinate-dependent statement.

Proposition 1.8
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a Carnot group of homogeneous dimension
Q
with a splitting
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{W}\cdot\mathbb{L}
, with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and one-dimensional. Let
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
denote a scalar product on the first layer, and let
d
be a homogeneous and left-invariant distance on
\mathbb{G}
. Consider
U
a relatively open subset in
\mathbb{W}
, and a uniformly intrinsically differentiable function
\varphi\colon U\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
.

Then, the subgraph
E_{\varphi}
of
\varphi
has locally finite
\mathbb{G}
-perimeter
1
1

1

The
\mathbb{G}
-perimeter is defined with respect to the scalar product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
.
in
U\cdot\mathbb{L}
and its
\mathbb{G}
-perimeter measure
|\partial E_{\varphi}|_{\mathbb{G}}
is given by

(3)

|\partial E_{\varphi}|_{\mathbb{G}}(V)=\int_{\Phi^{-1}(V)}\sqrt{1+|\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi|^{2}}\,\de\!\mathscr{S}^{Q-1}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits\mathbb{W},\qquad\mbox{for every Borel set $V\subseteq U\cdot\mathbb{L}$},

where
\Phi
denotes the graph map of
\varphi
,
\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi
is the intrinsic differential of
\varphi
, and
\mathscr{S}^{Q-1}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits\mathbb{W}
is the spherical Hausdorff measure of dimension
Q-1
restricted to
\mathbb{W}
.

Moreover, the reduced boundary of
E_{\varphi}
coincides with
{\rm graph}(\varphi)
, it is a
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-hypersurface, and there exists a positive measurable function
\beta
on
{\rm graph}(\varphi)
, only depending on the tangent to the hypersurface and on the homogeneous distance
d
, such that

(4)

\int_{V}\beta\de\!\mathscr{S}^{Q-1}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits{\rm graph}(\varphi)=\int_{\Phi^{-1}(V)}\sqrt{1+|\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi|^{2}}\,\de\!\mathscr{S}^{Q-1}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits\mathbb{W},\quad\mbox{for every Borel set $V\subseteq U\cdot\mathbb{L}$}.

Comments on the statements
. We point out that in
Section

1.2
, and
Section

1.2
we give coordinate-free definitions of the broad* conditions, while in
Section

3.4
, and
Section

4.1
, we will give apparently weaker definitions, choosing an adapted basis. Nevertheless the broad* condition and the vertically broad* hölder condition, when coupled together, are independent on the choice of the basis, see
Section

3.4
, and
Section

4.1
.

We comment on the statement of
Theorem

1.6
, and we refer the reader to the introduction of
Section

4
for a more detailed discussion. We notice that (a)
\Leftrightarrow
(d) is

[
Koz15
, Theorem 4.3.1]
.
In
[
Koz15
]
the proof of this latter fact is heavily based on the characterization of co-horizontal
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surfaces in terms of uniform convergence to Hausdorff tangents, see
[
Koz15
, Theorem 3.1.12]
. We give a self-contained different proof of this equivalence, with analytic flavor. Namely, we first show (b)
\Leftrightarrow
(d) in
Theorem

1.6
, whose proof is based on ideas coming from
[
ASCV06
]
and
[
DD18
]
, and thus, as a corollary, we eventually recover
[
Koz15
, Theorem 4.3.1]
by using the latter equivalence and (a)
\Leftrightarrow
(b).

The approximating condition in (
2
) of item (c) of
Theorem

1.6
could be interpreted as a weak formulation of the equality
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
on
U
, and it is the one that was first proposed and studied in
[
ASCV06
]
, see
Section

4.3
for a detailed discussion about this condition. Indeed, in
[
ASCV06
]
, in the case
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{H}^{n}
and
\mathbb{L}
one-dimensional, the equivalence (b)
\Leftrightarrow
(c) of
Theorem

1.6
has been proved, even in the stronger version obtained by removing the vertically broad* hölder regularity, see
[
ASCV06
, Theorem 5.1]
. We stress that the fact that
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
holds in the sense of distributions on
U
, that is part of
[
ASCV06
, Theorem 5.1]
, follows in general from the argument of item (c) of our
Section

4.2
.

We comment on the statement of
Theorem

1.7
, and we refer the reader to the introduction of
Section

6
for a more detailed discussion on the idea behind the proof. First, we notice that the main difference between this statement and the one in
Theorem

1.6
is in the fact that, in all the equivalences, we are able to drop the vertically broad* hölder regularity on
\varphi
. The equivalence (b)
\Leftrightarrow
(c) of
Theorem

1.7
results in being a generalization of
[
ASCV06
, Theorem 5.1]
to all step-2 Carnot groups.

The equivalence (b)
\Leftrightarrow
(d) generalizes the result in
[
BSC10b
, Theorem 1.2]
, if one also takes item (d) of
Section

4.2
into account, in order to explicitly write the intrinsic normal of
{\rm graph}(\varphi)
in terms of the intrinsic derivatives of
\varphi
.

We comment on the statement of
Section

1.2
. First of all, let us notice that (
4
) simply comes from (
3
) and the general area formula in
[
Mag17
]
, see
Section

4.2
for details. Let us notice that for some particular choices of the homogeneous distance on
\mathbb{G}
, i.e., when it is vertically symmetric, the function
\beta
is constant, thus simplifying (
4
), see again
Section

4.2
.

We also mention that in orthonormal coordinates we can explicitly compute the intrinsic normal, see item (d) of
Section

4.2
, thus generalizing the formulas already proved in Heisenberg groups and in Carnot groups of step 2 in
[
ASCV06
,
DD18
]
.

We finally remark that a formula in the spirit of (
4
) has been recently obtained in
[
CM20
]
for parametrized co-horizontal
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surfaces with complemented tangents of arbitrary codimensions in
\mathbb{H}^{n}
(see
[
CM20
, Theorem 4.2]
), building on an upper blow-up thorem, see
[
CM20
, Theorem 1.1]
. Very recently, in
[
JNGV20
, Theorem 1.1]
, a general area formula for
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surfaces has been proved in great generality. In
[
JNGV20
]
the area element is left implicit, depending only on the tangent to the surface and on the homogeneous distance on the group. We stress that, with our formula (
4
), we explicitly write the area element in terms of the intrinsic derivatives of
\varphi
, in the case the target is one-dimensional. For an area-formula for intrinsically Lipschitz functions in
\mathbb{H}^{n}
, one can also see
[
CMPSC14
]
.

Geometric characterizations of intrinsic differentiability
. The notion of (U)ID has a geometric meaning. Indeed, given
U\subseteq\mathbb{W}
open, a function
\varphi\colon U\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
is ID at
w_{0}\in U
if and only if the Hausdorff tangent to
{\rm graph}({\varphi})
at
w_{0}\cdot{\varphi}(w_{0})
is a homogeneous subgroup that is complementary to
\mathbb{L}
, see
Section

2.3
.

We stress that, at least in the case
\mathbb{L}
is horizontal, the previous convergence to the tangent is uniform if and only if
\varphi
is UID, see
[
Koz15
, Theorem 3.1.1]
, and
[
Koz15
, Theorem 3.1.12]
. The proof of these statements are rather involved and based on the so-called four cones Theorem, see
[
BK14
]
. We remark that we will not use this particular uniformity result throughout the paper.

We point out that the mere existence of Hausdorff tangents for
C^{1}_{\rm H}
regular surfaces - without any information on the uniformity of convergence - has been proved in great generality also in
[
Mag13
, Theorem 1.7]
, and in
[
JNGV20
, Lemma 2.14]
.

Now a natural question can be raised.
Is it true that an ID function
\varphi
with continuous intrinsic gradient
\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi
is UID?
Taking the geometric interpretation into account, the question can be reformulated, at least in the category of co-horizontal
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surfaces:
is it true that, if a co-horizontal
{\rm graph}({\varphi})
has continuously varying Hausdorff tangents, then it is a co-horizontal
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surface
? If true, this would be the counterpart of an already known result in the Euclidean setting that goes back to the beginning of twentieth century. We refer the reader to
[
BNG14
, Proposition 2.1]
and references therein for an historical account of the problem.

The answer to the previous question is affirmative in Heisenberg groups
\mathbb{H}^{n}
, see
[
SC16
, Theorem 4.95]
, and
[
Cor19
, Theorem 1.4]
. In this paper we obtain a new result in this direction. We prove that the answer is affirmative also for hypersurfaces in every step-2 Carnot group, see (b)
\Rightarrow
(a) in
Theorem

6.17
, thus generalizing
[
SC16
, Theorem 4.95]
.

We give also a partial affirmative answer for arbitrary Carnot groups, by requiring the additional hypothesis of the vertically broad* hölder condition on
\varphi
, see
Section

4.1
. This weaker implication might not be so satisfactory. Indeed, the intrinsic differentiability (even if it is not uniform) by itself already implies a
1/d
-little Hölder continuity on integral curves of the vector fields
D^{\varphi}_{W}
, with
W\in{\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})\cap V_{d}
. Nevertheless, this little Hölder continuity, a priori, might not be uniform, see
Section

3.3
,
Section

4.1
, and
Section

2.2
.

1.3.
Structure of the paper

In
Section

2
we introduce the common terminology and notation we use throughout the paper. We introduce Carnot groups, little Hölder functions, intrinsic submanifolds, intrinsically Lipschitz functions, (uniformly) intrinsically differentiable functions and we describe their basic properties and relations.

In
Section

3
we introduce the projected vector fields and we study their basic properties: in particular, we show some invariance properties that will be crucial in the proof of the main theorems. We also show how the (uniformly) intrinsic differentiability affects metric properties along integral curves of the projected vector fields.

In
Section

4
we prove the main results
Theorem

1.6
and
Section

1.2
we discussed above.

In
Section

5
we construct examples and apply our results also to obtain different proofs of particular cases of theorems already contained in the literature. We refer the reader to the beginning of
Section

5
for a more detailed discussion.

In
Section

6
we give the proof of
Theorem

1.7
.

Acknowledgments
. We are grateful to Katrin Fässler for several discussions around the topic of the paper. We warmly thank Raul Serapioni for several stimulating discussions and for having shared and discussed with us the examples in
Section

2.2
and
Section

5.2
. We also thank Sebastiano Nicolussi Golo for stimulating discussions, in particular regarding the last part of
Section

2.3
. We finally thank Francesco Serra Cassano and Davide Vittone for having discussed with us the content of
Section

4.3
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Carnot groups

A Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
is a connected and simply connected Lie group, whose Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
is stratified. Namely, there exist subspaces
V_{1},\dots,V_{s}
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
such that

\mathfrak{g}=V_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus V_{s},\qquad[V_{1},V_{j}]=V_{j+1}\quad\forall j=1,\dots,s-1,\qquad[V_{1},V_{s}]=\{0\}.

The integer
s
is called
step
of the group
\mathbb{G}
, while
m\coloneqq\dim(V_{1})
is called
rank
of
\mathbb{G}
. We call
n\coloneqq\mathrm{dim}\mathbb{G}
the topological dimension of
\mathbb{G}
. We denote by
e
the identity element of
\mathbb{G}
.

It is well known that the exponential map
\exp\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathbb{G}
is a diffeomorphism. We call

(5)

\mathbb{V}_{1}\coloneqq\exp(V_{1}),

the
horizontal bundle
of
\mathbb{G}
.
We write

(6)

\pi_{\mathbb{V}_{1}}\coloneqq\exp\circ\pi_{V_{1}}\circ\exp^{-1},

to denote the
projection on the horizontal bundle
\mathbb{V}_{1}
, where
\pi_{V_{1}}
is the linear projection in
\mathfrak{g}
onto
V_{1}
.

Every Carnot group has a one-parameter family of
dilations
that we denote by
\{\delta_{\lambda}:\lambda>0\}
. These dilations act on
\mathfrak{g}
as

(\delta_{\lambda})_{|_{V_{i}}}=\lambda^{i}(\mathrm{id})_{|_{V_{i}}},\qquad\forall\lambda>0,\quad\forall 1\leq i\leq s,

and are extended linearly. We will indicate with
\delta_{\lambda}
both the dilations on
\mathfrak{g}
and the group automorphisms corresponding to them via the exponential map.

We fix a scalar product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
in
V_{1}
, that can be extended left-invariantly on the horizontal bundle
\mathbb{V}_{1}=\exp(V_{1})
, and a homogeneous norm
\|\cdot\|
on
\mathbb{G}
. We recall that
\|\cdot\|
is a
homogeneous norm
on
\mathbb{G}
if

(7)

\begin{split}\|g\|&=0\qquad\mbox{if and only if $g=0$},\\
\|\delta_{\lambda}g\|&=\lambda\|g\|,\qquad\forall\lambda>0,\quad\forall g\in\mathbb{G},\\
\|g\|&=\|g^{-1}\|,\qquad\forall g\in\mathbb{G}.\end{split}

Sometimes we will also call the homogeneous norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{G}}
. We also fix on
\mathbb{G}
a left-invariant
\delta_{\lambda}
-homogeneous distance
d
and we denote by
B(g,r)
(respectively
\overline{B(g,r)}
) the open (respectively closed) balls of center
g\in\mathbb{G}
and radius
r>0
according to this distance. We next give the definition of complementary subgroups.

Definition 2.1

(Complementary subgroups)
.

Given a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
, we say that two subgroups
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
are
complementary subgroups
in
\mathbb{G}
if they are
homogeneous
, i.e., closed under the action of
\delta_{\lambda}
for every
\lambda>0
,
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{W}\cdot\mathbb{L}
and
\mathbb{W}\cap\mathbb{L}=\{e\}
.

We say that the subgroup
\mathbb{L}
is
horizontal and
k
-dimensional
, if there exist linearly independent
X_{1},\dots
,
X_{k}\in V_{1}
such that
\mathbb{L}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\})
.
Given
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
two complementary subgroups, we denote the
projection maps
from
\mathbb{G}
onto
\mathbb{W}
and onto
\mathbb{L}
by
\pi_{\mathbb{W}}
and
\pi_{\mathbb{L}}
, respectively. Defining
g_{\mathbb{W}}\coloneqq\pi_{\mathbb{W}}g
and
g_{\mathbb{L}}\coloneqq\pi_{\mathbb{L}}g
for any
g\in\mathbb{G}
, one has

(8)

g=(\pi_{\mathbb{W}}g)\cdot(\pi_{\mathbb{L}}g)=g_{\mathbb{W}}\cdot g_{\mathbb{L}},

and, whenever
\mathbb{W}
is normal (for example this is true when
\mathbb{L}
is horizontal), we have

(g\cdot h)_{\mathbb{L}}=g_{\mathbb{L}}\cdot h_{\mathbb{L}},\qquad(g\cdot h)_{\mathbb{W}}=g_{\mathbb{W}}\cdot\left(g_{\mathbb{L}}\cdot h_{\mathbb{W}}\cdot\left(g_{\mathbb{L}}\right)^{-1}\right),\qquad\forall g,h\in\mathbb{G}.

Remark 2.2
.

If
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
are complementary subgroups of
\mathbb{G}
and
\mathbb{L}
is one-dimensional, then it is easy to see that
\mathbb{L}
is horizontal. For the sake of clarity, we will always write
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and one-dimensional even if one-dimensional is technically sufficient.

Let us set
m_{0}\coloneqq 0
and
m_{j}\coloneqq\dim{V_{j}}
for any
j=1,\dots,s
. We recall that
m=m_{1}
. Let us define
n_{0}\coloneqq 0
, and
n_{j}\coloneqq\sum_{\ell=1}^{j}m_{\ell}
. The ordered set
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
is an
adapted basis
for
\mathfrak{g}
if the following facts hold.

(i)

The vector fields
X_{n_{j}+1},\dots,X_{n_{j+1}}
are chosen among the iterated commutators of order
j
of the vector fields
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
, for every
j=0,\dots,s-1
.

(ii)

The set
\{X_{n_{j}+1},\dots,X_{n_{j+1}}\}
is a basis for
V_{j+1}
for every
j=0,\dots,s-1
.

If we fix an adapted basis
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
, and
\ell\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, we define the
holonomic degree of
\ell
to be the unique
j^{*}\in\{1,\dots,s\}
such that
n_{j^{*}-1}+1\leq\ell\leq n_{j^{*}}
. We denote
\deg\ell\coloneqq j^{*}
and we also say that
j^{*}
is the
holonomic degree of
X_{\ell}
, i.e.,
\deg(X_{\ell})\coloneqq j^{*}
.

Definition 2.3

(Exponential coordinates)
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a Carnot group of dimension
n
and let
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
be an adapted basis of its Lie algebra. We define the
exponential coordinates of the first kind
associated with
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
by the map
F\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{G}
defined by

F(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\coloneqq\exp\left(x_{1}X_{1}+\ldots+x_{n}X_{n}\right).

It is well known that
F
is a diffeomorphism from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to
\mathbb{G}
. We will often need to consider maps in exponential coordinates. To avoid inconvenient notation we will use the following conventions.

•

If
\widetilde{U}\subset\mathbb{G}
, then
U\coloneqq F^{-1}(\widetilde{U})
.

•

If
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then
\widetilde{U}\coloneqq F(U)
.

•

If
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
are complementary subgroups of
\mathbb{G}
and
\mathbb{L}
is horizontal and
k
-dimensional, we may assume that
\mathbb{L}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\})
and
\mathbb{W}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{k+1},\dots,X_{n}\})
for an adapted basis
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
. Therefore
F
is one-to-one from
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\{0_{\mathbb{R}^{n-k}}\}
onto
\mathbb{L}
and also from
\{0_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}\}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-k}
onto
\mathbb{W}
.

•

If
\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}
and
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\to\mathbb{L}
is a function, then
\varphi\colon U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n-k}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
denotes the composition of
\widetilde{\varphi}
with
F
, namely
\varphi\coloneqq F^{-1}\circ\widetilde{\varphi}\circ F
.

•

If
\varphi\colon U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n-k}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
is a function, then we denote by
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
the map defined by
\widetilde{\varphi}\coloneqq F\circ\varphi\circ F^{-1}
.

•

If
p\in\mathbb{G}
and
j=1,\dots,s
, then
p^{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{j}}
is the vector of the coordinates of
p
in the
j^{\rm th}
layer, namely
p^{j}\coloneqq(F^{-1}(p)_{n_{j-1}+1},\dots,F^{-1}(p)_{n_{j}})
.

•

If
p\in\mathbb{G}
and
j=1,\dots,s
, then
\|p^{j}\|_{m_{j}}
denotes the Euclidean norm of
p^{j}
in
\mathbb{R}^{m_{j}}
.

It is well known that all the homogeneous norms on
\mathbb{G}
are bi-Lipschitz equivalent. Thus, when it will be convenient in the proofs, we work with the
anisotropic norm
that in exponential coordinates reads as

\|(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\|_{\mathbb{G}}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}|x_{\ell}|^{1/\mathrm{deg}\ell}.

We remark that a slight variation of the previous homogeneous norm gives rise to a homogeneous norm that induces a left-invariant homogeneous distance, see
[
FSSC03a
, Theorem 5.1]
.

We recall that the
homogeneous degree
of the monomial
x_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdot\dots\cdot x_{n}^{a_{n}}
in exponential coordinates, is
\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}a_{\ell}\cdot\deg\ell
.

For the expression of the operation on the group
\mathbb{G}
in exponential coordinates we refer to
[
FSSC03a
, Proposition 2.1]
. In the following result we point out a useful estimate for the norm of the conjugate.

Proposition 2.4

(
[
FS16
, Lemma 3.12]
)
.

There exist
\mathcal{P}=(\mathcal{P}^{1},\dots,\mathcal{P}^{s})\colon\mathbb{G}\times\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{m_{s}}
such that, for every
p,q\in\mathbb{G}
, one has

(9)

F^{-1}(p^{-1}qp)=F^{-1}(q)+\mathcal{P}(p,q),

where
\mathcal{P}^{1}=0
and, for each
i=2,\dots s
,
\mathcal{P}^{i}
is a vector valued
\delta_{\lambda}
-homogeneous polynomial of degree
i
. Moreover, for any bounded set
B\subset\mathbb{G}
, there exists
C\coloneqq C(B,\mathbb{G})>0
such that

|\mathcal{P}^{i}(p,q)|\leq C\bigl(\|q^{1}\|_{m_{1}}+\dots+\|q^{i-1}\|_{m_{i-1}}\bigl),

for every
p,q\in B
and every
i=2,\dots,s
.

Remark 2.5
.

With a little abuse of notation, (
9
) will be always written as

(10)

p^{-1}qp=q+\mathcal{P}(p,q),

where the identification of
\mathbb{G}
with
\mathbb{R}^{n}
has to be understood via exponential coordinates. Notice, however, that if one chooses a different diffeomorphism between
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\mathbb{G}
, such as exponential coordinates of the second kind or of mixed type, the polynomial
\mathcal{P}
, the constant
C
and the components
p^{j}
have to be changed accordingly.

2.2.
Little Hölder continuous functions

We introduce and discuss the notion of
\alpha
-little Hölder continuous function.

Definition 2.6

(little Hölder functions,
[
Lun95
]
)
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open set. We denote by
h^{\alpha}(U;\mathbb{R}^{k})
the set of all
\alpha
-little Hölder continuous
functions of order
0<\alpha<1
, i.e., the set of maps
\varphi\in C(U;\mathbb{R}^{k})
satisfying

(11)

\lim_{r\to 0}\left(\sup\Biggl\{\,\frac{|\varphi(b^{\prime})-\varphi(b)|}{|b^{\prime}-b|^{\alpha}}:\,b,b^{\prime}\in U\,,\,0<|b^{\prime}-b|<r\,\Biggl\}\right)=0.

We also define
h^{\alpha}_{{\rm loc}}(U;\mathbb{R}^{k})
the set of all functions
\varphi\in C(U;\mathbb{R}^{k})
such that
\varphi\in h^{\alpha}(U^{\prime};\mathbb{R}^{k})
for any open set
U^{\prime}\Subset U
.

The following example is, in some sense, “pathological”. As it will be clear during the paper, it gives a flavor of the difference between intrinsically differentiable functions and uniformly intrinsically differentiable functions, see
Section

4.1
. We thank R. Serapioni for having shared this example with us.

Example 2.7
.

We are going to construct a real-valued function
\varphi\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\varphi\in h^{1/2}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})
,
\varphi\notin h^{1/2}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R})
, but still it holds

(12)

\lim_{x\to 0}\frac{|\varphi(x)-\varphi(0)|}{|x|^{1/2}}=0.

Let us first notice that, for
n\geq 2
, the intervals
I_{n}\coloneqq[1/n-1/n^{3},1/n+1/n^{3}]
are mutually disjoint. Let us define, for
n\geq 2
, the functions
\varphi_{n}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
as

(13)

\varphi_{n}(x)\coloneqq\begin{cases}n^{3}\left|x-\frac{1}{n}\right|&if $x\in I_{n}$,\\
1&otherwise.\end{cases}

Notice that for each
n\geq 2
, the function
\varphi_{n}
is globally Lipschitz. Define
\varphi\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
as

\varphi(x)\coloneqq|x|\cdot\prod_{n=2}^{+\infty}\varphi_{n}(x).

Notice that, being
I_{n}
pairwise disjoint for
n\geq 2
, the infinite product is well-defined, since, if
x\notin I_{n}
, then
\varphi_{n}(x)=1
. Moreover, being each
\varphi_{n}
globally Lipschitz, we get that
\varphi\in{\rm Lip}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})
and thus
\varphi\in h^{1/2}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})
. We now prove that
\varphi\notin h^{1/2}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R})
. In particular this will follow from the fact that, for every compact neighborhood
U
of the origin,
\varphi\notin h^{1/2}(U)
.

Indeed, by definition of
\varphi
, we get the following equalities

(14)

\frac{\left|\varphi\left(\frac{1}{n}+\frac{1}{n^{3}}\right)-\varphi\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)\right|}{\left(\frac{1}{n^{3}}\right)^{1/2}}=\frac{\frac{1}{n}+\frac{1}{n^{3}}}{\frac{1}{n^{3/2}}}=\frac{n^{2}+1}{n^{3/2}},\qquad\forall n\geq 2.

Thus, if
U
is an arbitrary compact neighborhood of 0, we get that, for every
n
large enough, one has
[1/n,1/n+1/n^{3}]\subset U
, and thus (
14
) implies that (
11
) cannot hold, because
1/n^{3}=\left(1/n+1/n^{3}\right)-1/n\to 0
but
(n^{2}+1)/(n^{3/2})\to+\infty
as
n\to+\infty
. Thus
\varphi\not\in h^{1/2}(U)
.

Finally, by definition of
\varphi
, we get that, for
x\neq 0
,

\frac{|\varphi(x)-\varphi(0)|}{|x|^{1/2}}=|x|^{1/2}\cdot\prod_{n=2}^{+\infty}\varphi_{n}(x),

and thus (
12
) holds, because
\prod_{n=2}^{+\infty}\varphi_{n}
is bounded.

We remark that, by a little modification of this example, one can replace
1/2
with any
0<\alpha<1
.

2.3.
Intrinsic surfaces, Intrinsically Lipschitz and Intrinsically differentiable functions

In this section we recall the notion of intrinsic graph of a function, and see what happens to the defining map if we translate the graph. Then we recall the definitions of intrinsically Lipschitz and intrinsically differentiable maps. Finally we discuss the notion of co-horizontal
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surface.

Definition 2.8

(Intrinsic graph of a function)
.

Given
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
two complementary subgroups in
\mathbb{G}
, and
\widetilde{\varphi}:\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
a function, we denote

\widetilde{\Phi}(\widetilde{U})=\mathrm{graph}(\widetilde{\varphi})\coloneqq\{\widetilde{\Phi}(w):=w\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}(w):w\in\widetilde{U}\}.

Definition 2.9

(Intrinsic translation of a function)
.

Given
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
two complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
and a map
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
, we define, for every
q\in\mathbb{G}
,

\widetilde{U}_{q}\coloneqq\{a\in\mathbb{W}:\pi_{\mathbb{W}}(q^{-1}\cdot a)\in\widetilde{U}\},

and
\widetilde{\varphi}_{q}\colon\widetilde{U}_{q}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
by setting

(15)

\widetilde{\varphi}_{q}(a)\coloneqq\left(\pi_{\mathbb{L}}(q^{-1}\cdot a)\right)^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}\left(\pi_{\mathbb{W}}(q^{-1}\cdot a)\right).

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be two complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
and let
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
be a function. Then, for every
q\in\mathbb{G}
, the following facts hold.

(a)

{\rm graph}(\widetilde{\varphi}_{q})=q\cdot{\rm graph}(\widetilde{\varphi})
;

(b)

(\widetilde{\varphi}_{q})_{q^{-1}}=\widetilde{\varphi}
;

(c)

If
\mathbb{W}
is normal, then
\widetilde{U}_{q}=q_{\mathbb{W}}\cdot\left(q_{\mathbb{L}}\cdot\widetilde{U}\cdot q_{\mathbb{L}}^{-1}\right)
and

\widetilde{\varphi}_{q}(a)=q_{\mathbb{L}}\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}(q_{\mathbb{L}}^{-1}\cdot q_{\mathbb{W}}^{-1}\cdot a\cdot q_{\mathbb{L}}),

for any
a\in\widetilde{U}_{q}
;

(d)

If
q=\widetilde{\varphi}(a)^{-1}\cdot a^{-1}
for some
a\in\widetilde{U}
, then

\widetilde{\varphi}_{q}(e)=e.

Proof.

The proof of (a), directly follows from (
15
), which yields

(16)

a\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}_{q}(a)=q\cdot\pi_{\mathbb{W}}(q^{-1}\cdot a)\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}\left(\pi_{\mathbb{W}}(q^{-1}\cdot a)\right),\qquad\forall a\in\widetilde{U}_{q}.

To prove (b), it is enough to apply twice (
15
).
For the proof of (c), decompose
q=q_{\mathbb{W}}\cdot q_{\mathbb{L}}
. Then, for every
a\in\widetilde{U}_{q}
,

q^{-1}\cdot a=(q_{\mathbb{L}}^{-1}\cdot q_{\mathbb{W}}^{-1}\cdot a\cdot q_{\mathbb{L}})\cdot q_{\mathbb{L}}^{-1},

and whenever
\mathbb{W}
is normal one gets

(17)

\pi_{\mathbb{L}}(q^{-1}\cdot a)=q_{\mathbb{L}}^{-1},\qquad\pi_{\mathbb{W}}(q^{-1}\cdot a)=q_{\mathbb{L}}^{-1}\cdot q_{\mathbb{W}}^{-1}\cdot a\cdot q_{\mathbb{L}}.

As a consequence we get
\widetilde{U}_{q}=q_{\mathbb{W}}\cdot\left(q_{\mathbb{L}}\cdot\widetilde{U}\cdot q_{\mathbb{L}}^{-1}\right)
and, using again (
15
), we obtain (c).

To prove (d), it is enough to evaluate (
16
) in
a=e
and
q=\widetilde{\varphi}(a)^{-1}\cdot a^{-1}
.
∎

We introduce the notion of intrinsically Lipschitz function and state some properties. See
[
FS16
, Section 3]
.

Definition 2.11

(Intrinsic Cone)
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be two complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
. The
intrinsic cone

C_{\mathbb{W},\mathbb{L}}(q,\alpha)
of basis
\mathbb{W}
and axis
\mathbb{L}
, centered at
q
and of opening
\alpha\geq 0
, is defined by

C_{\mathbb{W},\mathbb{L}}(q,\alpha)\coloneqq q\cdot\{p\in\mathbb{G}:\|p_{\mathbb{W}}\|\leq\alpha\|p_{\mathbb{L}}\|\}.

Definition 2.12

(Intrinsically Lipschitz function)
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
. We say that a function
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
is
intrinsically
L
-Lipschitz
in
\widetilde{U}
, with
L>0
, if

C_{\mathbb{W},\mathbb{L}}(p,L^{-1})\cap\mbox{graph}(\widetilde{\varphi})=\{p\},\qquad\forall p\in\mbox{graph}(\widetilde{\varphi}).

Proposition 2.13

(
[
FS16
, Theorem 3.2 & Proposition 3.3]
)
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be two complementary subgroups in a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
and let
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
be a function. Then the following facts are equivalent.

(a)

\widetilde{\varphi}
is intrinsically
L
-Lipschitz in
\widetilde{U}
;

(b)

\|\pi_{\mathbb{L}}\left(p^{-1}\cdot q\right)\|\leq L\|\pi_{\mathbb{W}}\left(p^{-1}\cdot q\right)\|
for every
p,q\in{\rm graph}(\widetilde{\varphi})
;

(c)

for any
a\in\widetilde{U}
, setting
q\coloneqq\widetilde{\varphi}(a)^{-1}\cdot a^{-1}
, one has
\|\widetilde{\varphi}_{q}(b)\|\leq L\|b\|
for every
b\in\widetilde{U}_{q}
, where
\widetilde{\varphi}_{q}
and
\widetilde{U}_{q}
are defined in
Section

2.3
.

Moreover, for every
a\in\widetilde{U}
, setting
q\coloneqq\widetilde{\varphi}(a)^{-1}\cdot a^{-1}
, one has that
\widetilde{\varphi}
is intrinsically
L
-Lipschitz in
\widetilde{U}
if and only if
\widetilde{\varphi}_{q}
is intrinsically
L
-Lipschitz in
\widetilde{U}_{q}
.

We now define the notion of intrinsically linear function, intrinsically differentiable function and uniformly intrinsically differentiable function. General properties are studied in
[
FMS14
]
, see for example
[
FMS14
, Proposition 3.1.3 & Proposition 3.1.6]
. For the forthcoming definitions and properties of intrinsically differentiable functions we follow also
[
DD18
]
.

The notion of intrinsic differentiability was first given in
[
FSSC06
, Definition 4.4]
and then first studied in
[
ASCV06
]
, see
[
ASCV06
, Definition 1.1]
. In this last reference the notion of intrinsic differentiability is given in terms of the graph distance.
We here give a slightly different definition of intrinsic differentiability that is indeed equivalent to ours, by
[
SC16
, Proposition 4.76]
, when
\mathbb{W}
is a normal subgroup, that will always be in our case.

Definition 2.14

(Intrinsically linear function)
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be complementary subgroups in
\mathbb{G}
. Then
\ell:\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
is
intrinsically linear
if
\mathrm{graph}(\ell)
is a homogeneous subgroup of
\mathbb{G}
.

Definition 2.15

(Pansu differentiability)
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
and
\mathbb{G}^{\prime}
be two Carnot groups endowed with left-invariant homogeneous distances
d_{\mathbb{G}}
and
d_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime}}
and let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{G}
be an open set. A function
f\colon\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{G}^{\prime}
is said to be
Pansu differentiable
at a point
p\in\Omega
if there exists a Carnot homomorphism
L\colon\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{G}^{\prime}
, i.e., a group homomorphism that commutes with the dilations
\delta_{\lambda}
, such that

\lim_{x\to p}\frac{d_{\mathbb{G}^{\prime}}(f(p)^{-1}f(x),L(p^{-1}x))}{d_{\mathbb{G}}(x,p)}=0.

The map
L
is uniquely determined, whenever it exists, and it is called the
Pansu differential
of
f
at
p
and it is denoted by
\de_{\rm P}\!f(p)
.

Definition 2.16

(
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-function)
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{G}
be an open subset of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
. A map
f\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
is said to be
of class
C^{1}_{\rm H}
if it is Pansu differentiable and the Pansu differential
\de_{\rm P}\!f\colon\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
is continuous. We denote by
C^{1}_{\rm H}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k})
the set of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-valued functions of class
C^{1}_{\rm H}
in
\Omega
.

Definition 2.17

((Uniformly) intrinsic differentiability)
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
and let
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
be a function with
\widetilde{U}
open in
\mathbb{W}
. For
a_{0}\in\widetilde{U}
, let
p_{0}\coloneqq\widetilde{\varphi}(a_{0})^{-1}\cdot a_{0}^{-1}
and denote by
\widetilde{\varphi}_{p_{0}}\colon\widetilde{U}_{p_{0}}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
the shifted function defined in
Section

2.3
.

We say that
\widetilde{\varphi}
is
intrinsically differentiable
at
a_{0}
if the shifted function
\widetilde{\varphi}_{p_{0}}

is
intrinsically differentiable
at
e
, i.e., if there is an intrinsically linear map
\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi_{a_{0}}\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
such that

(18)

\lim_{r\to 0}\left(\sup\left\{\frac{\|\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi_{a_{0}}(b)^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}_{p_{0}}(b)\|}{\|b\|}:b\in\widetilde{U}_{p_{0}},0<\|b\|<r\right\}\right)=0.

The function
\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi_{a_{0}}
, sometimes denoted also by
\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi(a_{0})
, is called
intrinsic differential
of
\widetilde{\varphi}
at
a_{0}
, and we say that
\widetilde{\varphi}
is
intrinsically differentiable
if it is intrinsically differentiable at any point
a_{0}\in\widetilde{U}
. We also denote by
{\rm ID}(\widetilde{U},\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
the set of intrinsically differentiable functions
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
.

We say that
\widetilde{\varphi}
is
uniformly intrinsically differentiable
at
a_{0}
if, setting
p_{a}\coloneqq\widetilde{\varphi}(a)^{-1}\cdot a^{-1}
for any
a\in\widetilde{U}
, we have

(19)

\lim_{r\to 0}\left(\sup\left\{\frac{\|\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi_{a_{0}}(b)^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}_{p_{a}}(b)\|}{\|b\|}:a\in\widetilde{U}\cap B(a_{0},r),b\in\widetilde{U}_{p_{a}}\cap B(a_{0},r),a\neq b\right\}\right)=0.

We say that
\widetilde{\varphi}
is
uniformly intrinsically differentiable
on
\widetilde{U}
if it is uniformly intrinsically differentiable at any
a_{0}\in\widetilde{U}
. We finally denote by
{\rm UID}(\widetilde{U},\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
the set of uniformly intrinsically differentiable functions
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
.

Remark 2.18

(Intrinsic difference quotients)
.

In the papers
[
FSSC06
,
Ser17
]
, the authors introduce and study the following two notions, giving characterizations for intrinsically Lipschitz continuity, see
[
Ser17
, Proposition 3.11 & Theorem 3.21]
. For a continuous function
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
, defined on
\widetilde{U}
open, the
intrinsic difference quotients
of
\widetilde{\varphi}
at the point
w\in\widetilde{U}
in the direction
Y\in{\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})
at time
t>0
, are defined as

\nabla_{Y}\widetilde{\varphi}(w,t)\coloneqq\delta_{1/t}\widetilde{\varphi}_{p}(\delta_{t}\exp Y),

for every
t>0
, where
p\coloneqq\widetilde{\varphi}(w)^{-1}\cdot w^{-1}
, and whenever
\delta_{t}\exp Y
is in
\widetilde{U}_{p}
. The
intrinsic directional derivative
of
\widetilde{\varphi}
at
w\in\widetilde{U}
in the direction
Y\in{\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})
is defined by

D_{Y}\widetilde{\varphi}(w)\coloneqq\lim_{t\to 0}\nabla_{Y}\widetilde{\varphi}(w,t),

whenever the limit exists. In analogy with Euclidean Calculus, we notice that, if
\widetilde{\varphi}\in{\rm ID}(\widetilde{U},\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
, then it admits intrinsic directional derivatives at any
w\in\widetilde{U}
along any
Y\in{\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})
, and, moreover, one has
D_{Y}\widetilde{\varphi}(w)=\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi(w)(\exp Y)
, for any
w\in\widetilde{U}
and every
Y\in\mathrm{Lie}(\mathbb{W})
. Indeed, this is a consequence of the following identity

\|\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi(w)(\exp Y)^{-1}\delta_{1/t}\widetilde{\varphi}_{p}(\delta_{t}\exp Y)\|=\frac{\|\left(\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi(w)(\delta_{t}\exp Y)\right)^{-1}\widetilde{\varphi}_{p}(\delta_{t}\exp Y)\|}{t},

that simply comes from the fact both the norm
\|\cdot\|
and
\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi
are
\delta_{\lambda}
-homogeneous. Then from the previous equality and (
18
) with
a_{0}=w
, and
b=\delta_{t}\exp Y
, we get the sought claim taking
t\to 0
.

Proposition 2.19

(
[
DD18
, Proposition 3.4]
)
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be two complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and
k
-dimensional and let
\ell\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
be an intrinsically linear function. Then
\ell
only depends on the horizontal components of the elements in
\mathbb{W}
, namely on
\mathbb{W}_{1}\coloneqq\mathbb{W}\cap\mathbb{V}_{1}
, where
\mathbb{V}_{1}=\exp(V_{1})
. In particular, if
\pi_{\mathbb{V}_{1}}
denotes the projection from
\mathbb{G}
to
\mathbb{V}_{1}
, see (
6
), one has

\ell(a)=\ell(\pi_{\mathbb{V}_{1}}a),\qquad\forall a\in\mathbb{W}.

As a consequence,
\exp^{-1}\circ\ell\circ\exp_{|_{{\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})\cap V_{1}}}\colon{\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})\cap V_{1}\to{\rm Lie}(\mathbb{L})
is linear, and there exists a constant
C\coloneqq C(\ell)>0
such that

(20)

\|\ell(a)\|\leq C\|\pi_{\mathbb{V}_{1}}a\|,\qquad\forall a\in\mathbb{W}.

Definition 2.20

(Intrinsic gradient)
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be two complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and
k
-dimensional, let
\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}
be open, and let
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\to\mathbb{L}
be intrinsically differentiable at
a_{0}\in\widetilde{U}
. By
Section

2.3
, the map
\exp^{-1}\circ(\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi_{a_{0}})\circ\exp_{|_{{\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})\cap V_{1}}}
is linear and thus there exists a linear map
\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi_{a_{0}}\in\mathrm{Lin}({\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})\cap V_{1};{\rm Lie}(\mathbb{L}))
such that

\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi_{a_{0}}(\exp W)=\exp\left(\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi_{a_{0}}(W)\right),\qquad\forall W\in{\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})\cap V_{1}.

Remark 2.21

(Intrinsic gradient in exponential coordinates)
.

Assume
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
is an adapted basis of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
such that
\mathbb{L}=\mbox{span}\{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\}
and
\mathbb{W}=\mbox{span}\{X_{k+1},\dots,X_{n}\}
and identify
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
with
\mathbb{R}^{n-k}
and
\mathbb{R}^{k}
, respectively, through exponential coordinates as explained in
Section

2.1
.
Then, by
Section

2.3
, with a little abuse of notation, we get a
k\times(m-k)
matrix
\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi_{a_{0}}
such that, in coordinates, one has

\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi_{a_{0}}(a)=\left(\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi_{a_{0}}(a_{k+1},\dots,a_{m})^{\rm T},0,\dots,0\right),\qquad\forall a=(a_{k+1},\dots,a_{n})\in\mathbb{W}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{n-k}.

The following proposition gives us a more explicit way to write the definition of functions in
{\rm ID}(\widetilde{U},\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
and in
{\rm UID}(\widetilde{U},\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
, whenever
\mathbb{L}
is horizontal.

Proposition 2.22

(
[
DD18
, Proposition 3.5]
)
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be complementary subgroups in a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
, with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal, and let
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
with
\widetilde{U}
open in
\mathbb{W}
. Then the following facts hold.

(a)

\widetilde{\varphi}
is intrinsically differentiable at
\widetilde{a}_{0}\in\widetilde{U}
if and only if

(21)

\lim_{r\to 0}\left(\sup\left\{\frac{|\varphi(b)-\varphi(a_{0})-\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi_{a_{0}}(a_{0}^{-1}\cdot b)|}{\|\widetilde{\varphi}(a_{0})^{-1}a_{0}^{-1}\cdot b\,\widetilde{\varphi}(a_{0})\|}:b\in U,0<\|a_{0}^{-1}\cdot b\|<r\right\}\right)=0.

(b)

\widetilde{\varphi}
is uniformly intrinsically differentiable at
\widetilde{a}_{0}
if and only if

(22)

\lim_{r\to 0}\left(\sup\left\{\frac{|\varphi(b)-\varphi(a)-\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi_{a_{0}}(a^{-1}\cdot b)|}{\|\widetilde{\varphi}(a)^{-1}a^{-1}b\,\widetilde{\varphi}(a)\|}:a,b\in B(a_{0},r)\cap U,a\neq b\right\}\right)=0.

Remark 2.23
.

We notice that in (
21
) and (
22
) there is a little abuse of notation, for the sake of simplicity. First we are identifying
\mathbb{L}
with
\mathbb{R}^{k}
in order to write the differences in the numerators, and moreover we write
\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi_{a_{0}}(a_{0}^{-1}\cdot b)
but we mean
\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi_{a_{0}}(\pi_{V_{1}}\exp^{-1}(\widetilde{a}_{0}^{-1}\cdot\exp b))
.

Remark 2.24

(Intrinsic differentiability & tangent subgroups)
.

Let us collect the following observations about
Section

2.3
.

(i) If
\widetilde{\varphi}
is intrinsically differentiable at
a_{0}\in\widetilde{U}
, there is a unique intrinsically linear function
\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi_{a_{0}}
satisfying
(
18
)
. Moreover
\widetilde{\varphi}
is continuous at
a_{0}
, see
[
FMS14
, Theorem 3.2.8 and Proposition 3.2.3]
.

(ii) The notion of intrinsic differentiability is invariant under group translations. More precisely, let
a,b
be in
\widetilde{U}
and let
p\coloneqq\widetilde{\varphi}(a)^{-1}\cdot a^{-1}
and
q\coloneqq\widetilde{\varphi}(b)^{-1}\cdot b^{-1}
. Then
\widetilde{\varphi}
is intrinsically differentiable at
a
if and only if
\widetilde{\varphi}_{q^{-1}p}=(\widetilde{\varphi}_{p})_{q^{-1}}
is intrinsically differentiable at
b
, see (
[
FMS14
, Remark 3.2.2]
).

(iii) The analytic definition of intrinsic differentiability has an equivalent geometric formulation. Indeed, the intrinsic differentiability at one point is equivalent to the existence of a tangent subgroup to the graph, see
[
FSSC11
, Theorem 4.15]
for the proof in the case of Heisenberg groups
\mathbb{H}^{n}
. If we have
\widetilde{\varphi}:\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
, and
w_{0}\in\widetilde{U}
, we say that a homogeneous subgroup
\mathbb{T}
of
\mathbb{G}
is a
tangent subgroup
to
\mathrm{graph}\,(\widetilde{\varphi})
at
w_{0}\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}(w_{0})
if the following facts hold.

(i)

\mathbb{T}
is a complementary subgroup of
\mathbb{L}
;

(ii)

In any compact subset of
\mathbb{G}
, the limit

\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\delta_{\lambda}\left((w_{0}\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}(w_{0}))^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{graph}\,(\widetilde{\varphi})\right)=\mathbb{T}

holds in the sense of Hausdorff convergence.

In the introduction of
[
FMS14
]
the authors say that
\widetilde{\varphi}
is intrinsically differentiable at
w_{0}
if and only if
\mathrm{graph}\,(\widetilde{\varphi})
has a tangent subgroup
\mathbb{T}
in
w_{0}\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}(w_{0})
and in this case
\mathbb{T}=\mathrm{graph}\,(\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi_{w_{0}})
. The complete proof can be given building on
[
FMS14
, Theorem 3.2.8]
, that shows one part of the statement, and generalizing
[
FSSC11
, Theorem 4.15]
, that holds verbatim in the context of arbitrary Carnot groups. We thank Sebastiano Nicolussi Golo for having shared with us some notes containing a detailed proof of the previously discussed statement.

Proposition 2.25

(
[
DD18
, Proposition 3.7]
)
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and
k
-dimensional, let
\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}
be open and let
\widetilde{\varphi}\in{\rm UID}(\widetilde{U},\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
. Then the following facts hold.

(a)

\widetilde{\varphi}
is intrinsically Lipschitz continuous on every relatively compact subset of
\widetilde{U}
.

(b)

the function
a\mapsto\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi_{a}
is continuous from
\widetilde{U}
to the space of matrices
\mathbb{R}^{k\times(m-k)}
. Here
\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi
is the intrinsic gradient, see
Section

2.3
.

Definition 2.26

(
\nabla_{\mathbb{W}},\nabla_{\mathbb{L}}
)
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be two complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
, with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and
k
-dimensional and let
f\in C^{1}_{\rm H}(\widetilde{U};\mathbb{R}^{k})
.
Consider an adapted basis
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
such that
\mathbb{L}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\})
and

\mathbb{W}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{k+1},\dots,X_{n}\})
. Then, we define
\nabla_{\mathbb{L}}f
and
\nabla_{\mathbb{W}}f
by setting

\nabla_{\mathbb{L}}f\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}X_{1}f^{(1)}&\dots&X_{k}f^{(1)}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
X_{1}f^{(k)}&\dots&X_{k}f^{(k)}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\nabla_{\mathbb{W}}f\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}X_{k+1}f^{(1)}&\dots&X_{m}f^{(1)}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
X_{k+1}f^{(k)}&\dots&X_{m}f^{(k)}\end{pmatrix}.

In particular, one has that, in exponential coordinates,
\nabla_{\rm H}f=\left(\nabla_{\mathbb{L}}f\;|\;\nabla_{\mathbb{W}}f\right)
.

We recall the notion of co-horizontal
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surface of arbitrary codimension, see
[
Koz15
, Definition 3.3.4]
.
We stress that we changed the terminology with respect to
[
Koz15
, Definition 3.3.4]
. What he calls co-Abelian surface, for us is a co-horizontal surface
. For a very general definition of
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surface, we refer the reader to
[
Mag06
, Definition 3.1]
,
[
Mag13
, Definition 10.2]
and to
[
JNGV20
, Section 2.5]
.

Definition 2.27

(co-horizontal
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surface)
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a Carnot group of rank
m
and let
1\leq k\leq m
. We say that
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{G}
is a
co-horizontal
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surface of codimension
k
if, for any
p\in\Sigma
, there exist a neighborhood
\widetilde{U}
of
p
and a map
f\in C^{1}_{\rm H}(\widetilde{U};\mathbb{R}^{k})
such that

(23)

\Sigma\cap\widetilde{U}=\{g\in\widetilde{U}:f(g)=0\},

and the Pansu differential
\de_{\rm P}\!f(p)\colon\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
of
f
is surjective.

We say that
\Sigma
is a codimension
k

co-horizontal
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surface with complemented tangents
if, in addition, given a representation around
p
as in (
23
), the homogeneous subgroup
\mathrm{Ker}(\de_{\rm P}\!f(p))
admits a horizontal complement (of dimension
k
).
In this case, we call
{\rm Ker}(\de_{\rm P}\!f(p))
the
homogeneous tangent space to
\Sigma
at
p
. This homogeneous subgroup at
p
is independent of the choice of
f
, see
[
Mag13
, Theorem 1.7]
.

We remark that, if
\Sigma\subseteq\mathbb{G}
is a co-horizontal
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surface with complemented tangents, then one can use the implicit function Theorem, see
[
FSSC03b
, Theorem 2.1]
for the one-codimensional case, and see
[
Mag13
, Theorem 1.4]
for the more general statement, to locally represent the surface as a graph of a function
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\coloneqq\mathrm{Ker}(\de_{\rm P}\!f(p))\to\mathbb{L}
, with
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
complementary subgroups.

The following proposition follows from
[
DD18
, Theorem 4.1 & Theorem 4.6]
and relates level sets of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-valued
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-functions, and ultimately co-horizontal
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surfaces with complemented tangents, with uniformly intrinsically differentiable functions.

Proposition 2.28

(
[
DD18
, Theorem 4.1 & Theorem 4.6]
)
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be two complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
, with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and
k
-dimensional, take
\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}
open and
\widetilde{\varphi}\in\mathrm{UID}(\widetilde{U},\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
. Then, for every
a\in\widetilde{U}
, there exist a neighborhood
\widetilde{V}
of
a\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}(a)
in
\mathbb{G}
, and
f\in C^{1}_{\rm H}(\widetilde{V};\mathbb{R}^{k})
, such that

\widetilde{\Phi}(\widetilde{U})\cap\widetilde{V}=\{g\in\widetilde{V}:f(g)=0\},

and, for every
g\in\widetilde{V}
, the Pansu differential
\de_{\rm P}\!f(g)_{|_{\mathbb{L}}}\colon\mathbb{L}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
is bijective. As a consequence
\mathrm{graph}\,(\widetilde{\varphi})
is a co-horizontal
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surface of codimension
k
, with tangents complemented by
\mathbb{L}
. Moreover, if
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
is an adapted basis of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
such that
\mathbb{L}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\})
and
\mathbb{W}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{k+1},\dots,X_{n}\})
, then
\det\nabla_{\mathbb{L}}f\neq 0
and, in exponential coordinates, one has

(24)

\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi(a)=-\left(\nabla_{\mathbb{L}}f(\widetilde{\Phi}(a))\right)^{-1}\nabla_{\mathbb{W}}f(\widetilde{\Phi}(a)),\qquad\forall a\in\widetilde{U}.

For the definition of
\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi,\nabla_{\mathbb{W}}
and
\nabla_{\mathbb{L}}
we refer to
Section

2.3
and
Section

2.3
.

On the other hand, if
1\leq k\leq m
and
\Sigma
is a codimension
k
co-horizontal
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surface with complemented tangents, then, for every
p\in\Sigma
, there exist two complementary subgroups
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
of
\mathbb{G}
with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and
k
-dimensional, a neighborhood
\widetilde{V}\subseteq\mathbb{G}
of
p
and
\widetilde{\varphi}\in{\rm UID}(\widetilde{U},\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
, with
\widetilde{U}=\pi_{\mathbb{W}}(\widetilde{V})
, such that

\Sigma\cap\widetilde{V}=\mathrm{graph}\,(\widetilde{\varphi}).

Remark 2.29
.

Notice that, in the setting of
Section

2.3
, in the case
k=1
, one may assume
X_{1}f\neq 0
on
\widetilde{V}
, and, in coordinates, formula (
24
) reads as

(25)

\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi(a)=-\left(\frac{X_{2}f}{X_{1}f},\dots,\frac{X_{m}f}{X_{1}f}\right)\circ\Phi(a),\qquad\forall a\in\widetilde{U}.

Remark 2.30

(Tangent subgroups to
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surfaces)
.

From the previous
Section

2.3
and
Section

2.3
it directly follows that every co-horizontal
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surface with complemented tangents has Hausdorff tangent everywhere. For a proof of this property in a more general context one can see
[
Mag13
, Theorem 1.7]
, or
[
JNGV20
, Lemma 2.14, point (iii)]
.
This convergence is moreover locally uniform: we will not use this information, but this comes from
[
Koz15
, Theorem 3.1.1]
.

3.
Intrinsic projected vector fields on subgroups

In this section we mainly deal with complementary subgroups
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
along with a continuous map
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
, where
\widetilde{U}
is open in
\mathbb{W}
.

In
Section

3.1
we shall define, for some
W\in{\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})
, the projected vector field
D^{\varphi}_{W}
on
\mathbb{W}
by taking the projection on
\mathbb{W}
of
W
restricted the graph
\widetilde{\Phi}(\widetilde{U})
of
\widetilde{\varphi}
(see
Section

3.1
), and we discuss some basic properties of these vector fields. We give explicit formulas for these vector fields in Heisenberg groups
\mathbb{H}^{n}
, in Carnot groups of step 2, and in the Engel group
\mathbb{E}
(see
Section

3.1
,
Section

3.1
, and
Section

3.1
, respectively). In
Section

3.1
we show an explicit expression of such vector fields in exponential coordinates.
The definition of the projected vector fields appeared first in
[
Koz15
]
, see
Section

3.1
. In
[
Koz15
]
the author gives equivalent conditions for
\widetilde{\Phi}(\widetilde{U})
to be an intrinsically Lipschitz graph (respectively a co-horizontal
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surfaces with complemented tangents) in terms of Hölder properties of the integral curves of the vector fields
D^{\varphi}_{W}
, see
[
Koz15
, Theorem 4.2.16]
(respectively
[
Koz15
, Theorem 4.3.1]
). Within our context we recover these results by using invariance properties of such vector fields, see the introduction to
Section

4
.

In
Section

3.2
we prove some invariance properties of the projected vector fields with respect to the intrinsic translations (see
Section

2.3
) of
\widetilde{\varphi}
. In particular we write how the vector field
D^{\varphi_{q}}
changes with respect to
D^{\varphi}
and how the integral curves of
D^{\varphi_{q}}
change with respect to the integral curves of
D^{\varphi}
, see
Section

3.2
and
Section

3.2
when
\mathbb{L}
is horizontal, and
Section

3.2
for the general case in which
\mathbb{W}
is normal. These invariance properties will be crucial for the proof of the results in
Section

4
.

In
Section

3.3
we recall that if
\widetilde{\varphi}
is intrinsically Lipschitz, then
\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}
is
1/j
-Hölder whenever
\widetilde{\gamma}
is an integral curve of
D^{\varphi}_{W}
with
\deg W=j
, see
Section

3.3
. We stress that this property was already known from
[
Koz15
, Theorem 4.2.16]
. We improve this result when
\widetilde{\varphi}
is more regular. Namely if
\widetilde{\varphi}
is intrinsically differentiable, then
\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}
is Euclidean differentiable whenever
\widetilde{\gamma}
is an integral curve of
D^{\varphi}_{W}
with
\deg W=1
, while if
\deg W>1
we obtain a
pointwise
little Hölder continuity of
\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}
, see
Section

3.3
. This pointwise little Hölder continuity improves to a
uniform
little Hölder continuity if
\widetilde{\varphi}
is
uniformly
intrinsically differentiable, see
Section

3.3
, and
Section

3.3
for a more refined conclusion.

In
Section

3.4
we recall the notion of broad* solution to the system
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
, with a continuous datum
\omega
. The study of the relation between being a broad* solution to the system
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
with a continuous
\omega
and the intrinsic regularity of
{\rm graph}(\widetilde{\varphi})
was first initiated, in
\mathbb{H}^{n}
for
\mathbb{L}
one-dimensional in
[
ASCV06
, Section 5]
, and then continued in
[
BSC10a
,
BSC10b
,
BCSC14
]
. For the case
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{H}^{n}
and
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and
k
-dimensional see also
[
Cor19
]
and for the general case of Carnot groups of step 2 and
\mathbb{L}
one-dimensional, see
[
DD18
,
DD19
]
. In
Section

3.4
we give a sufficient condition for the map
\varphi
to be a broad* solution to the system
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
, with
\omega
continuous. This condition is the intrinsic differentiability plus the continuity of the intrinsic gradient.

3.1.
Definition of
D^{\varphi}
and main properties

In this subsection we define the projected vector fields
D^{\varphi}_{W}
and state some of their properties.

Definition 3.1

(Projected vector fields)
.

Given two complementary subgroups
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
in a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
, and a continuous function
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
defined on an open set
\widetilde{U}
of
\mathbb{W}
, we define, for every
W\in\mathrm{Lie}(\mathbb{W})
, the continuous
projected vector field

D^{\varphi}_{W}
, by setting

(26)

(D^{\varphi}_{W})_{|_{w}}(f)\coloneqq W_{|_{w\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}(w)}}(f\circ\pi_{\mathbb{W}}),

for all
w\in\widetilde{U}
and all
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{W})
. When
W
is an element
X_{j}
of an adapted basis
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
we also denote
D^{\varphi}_{j}\coloneqq D^{\varphi}_{X_{j}}
.

Remark 3.2
.

The
Section

3.1
is well posed since the projection
\pi_{\mathbb{W}}
is polynomial and hence
C^{\infty}
for every arbitrary splitting. Notice that if
\widetilde{\varphi}
is
C^{\infty}
then
D^{\varphi}_{W}
is a vector field with
C^{\infty}
coefficients.

Section

3.1
has been given in
[
Koz15
, Definition 4.2.12]
and it has been studied in the case
\mathbb{W}
is a homogeneous normal subgroup and, more specifically, when
\mathbb{L}
is horizontal and
k
-dimensional. We refer to the discussion in the introduction of
Section

3
.

From now on
\mathbb{W}
denotes a homogeneous normal subgroup of
\mathbb{G}
.

Remark 3.3
.

Notice that (
26
) is equivalent to

(27)

(D^{\varphi}_{W})_{|_{w}}=\de(\pi_{\mathbb{W}})_{\widetilde{\Phi}(w)}(W_{|_{\widetilde{\Phi}(w)}}),

that is,
D^{\varphi}_{W}
is the push-forward of the vector field
W
towards the map
(\pi_{\mathbb{W}})_{|_{\widetilde{\Phi}(\widetilde{U})}}\colon\widetilde{\Phi}(\widetilde{U})\to\widetilde{U}
.
Thus, as already observed in
[
Koz15
, Equation 4.4]
, one has

(28)

\displaystyle(D^{\varphi}_{W})_{|_{w}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\de}{\de t}_{|_{t=0}}\pi_{\mathbb{W}}(\widetilde{\Phi}(w)\cdot\exp(tW))=\frac{\de}{\de t}_{|_{t=0}}w\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}(w)\cdot\exp(tW)\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}(w)^{-1}=

\displaystyle=\frac{\de}{\de t}_{|_{t=0}}L_{w}\circ L_{\widetilde{\varphi}(w)}\circ R_{\widetilde{\varphi}(w)^{-1}}(\exp(tW))=

\displaystyle=\de(L_{w})_{e}\circ\de(L_{\widetilde{\varphi}(w)})_{\widetilde{\varphi}(w)^{-1}}\circ\de(R_{\widetilde{\varphi}(w)^{-1}})_{e}(W_{|_{e}})=\de(L_{w})_{e}\circ\mathrm{Ad}_{\widetilde{\varphi}(w)}(W_{|_{e}}).

For the previous computation, we used the definition of the differential and, in the second equality, the fact that

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathbb{W}}(\widetilde{\Phi}(w)\cdot\exp(tW))

\displaystyle=\pi_{\mathbb{W}}(w\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}(w)\cdot\exp(tW)\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}(w)^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}(w))=

\displaystyle=w\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}(w)\cdot\exp(tW)\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}(w)^{-1},

for every
w\in\mathbb{W}
and
W\in\mathrm{Lie}(\mathbb{W})
, where the last equality holds since
\mathbb{W}
is normal.

Example 3.4

(Projected vector fields on Heisenberg groups)
.

Consider the Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{n}
, with an adapted basis
(X_{1},\dots,X_{2n+1})
of its Lie algebra such that the only nonvanishing relations are
[X_{i},X_{n+i}]=X_{2n+1}
, for every
1\leq i\leq n
. Fix
1\leq k\leq n
, identify
\mathbb{H}^{n}
with
\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
by means of exponential coordinates associated with
(X_{1},\dots,X_{2n+1})
and define
\mathbb{W}\coloneqq\{x_{1}=\dots=x_{k}=0\}
, and
\mathbb{L}\coloneqq\{x_{k+1}=\dots=x_{2n+1}=0\}
. Then, for a continuous
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
, with
\widetilde{U}
open, one can compute in exponential coordinates

(29)

\begin{split}(D^{\varphi}_{X_{j}})_{|_{w}}&=(X_{j})_{|_{w}},\qquad k+1\leq j\leq n\vee k+n+1\leq j\leq 2n,\\
(D^{\varphi}_{X_{n+i}})_{|_{w}}&=(\partial_{x_{n+i}})_{|_{w}}+\varphi^{(i)}(w)(\partial_{x_{2n+1}})_{|_{w}},\quad i=1,\dots,k.\end{split}

(30)

(D^{\varphi}_{X_{2n+1}})_{|_{w}}=(\partial_{x_{2n+1}})_{|_{w}}.

for every
w\in U
, where
\varphi
denotes the composition of
\widetilde{\varphi}
with the exponential coordinates and
\varphi^{(i)}
is its
i
-th component of
\varphi
. Notice that we do not have the first condition in case
k=n
.

Remark 3.5
.

For the computations of
Section

3.1
, we refer to
[
Koz15
, Section 4.4.2]
, where the constant is slightly different from ours because of the fact that the author considers the model of
\mathbb{H}^{n}
with relations
[X_{i},X_{n+i}]=-4X_{2n+1}
, for every
1\leq i\leq n
. The expression of the projected vector fields in case
\mathbb{L}
is
k
-dimensional is also in
[
Cor19
, Definition 3.6]
.

It is by now well known, from the papers
[
ASCV06
]
,
[
BSC10a
]
,
[
BSC10b
]
, and
[
BCSC14
]
, that in every Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{n}
, in case
\mathbb{L}
is one-dimensional, the intrinsic regularity of
\mathrm{graph}(\widetilde{\varphi})
depends on the regularity of the vector field
D^{\varphi}
applied to
\varphi
, i.e.,
D^{\varphi}\varphi\coloneqq(D^{\varphi}_{X_{2}}\varphi,\dots,D^{\varphi}_{X_{2n}}\varphi)
, which has to be considered in the sense of distributions. For the full results we refer to
[
SC16
, Theorems 4.90 & 4.92]
. In particular
\mathrm{graph}(\widetilde{\varphi})
is an intrinsically Lipschitz graph (respectively a
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-hypersurface) if and only if
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
, in the distributional sense, for some
\omega\in L^{\infty}(U)
(respectively
\omega\in C(U)
).

A step towards obtaining analogous results in
\mathbb{H}^{n}
, in case
\mathbb{L}
has higher dimension and
\omega
is continuous, has been recently done by Corni in
[
Cor19
]
. In particular, the author proves that, if
\mathbb{L}
is horizontal
k
-dimensional, the set
\mathrm{graph}(\widetilde{\varphi})
is a co-horizontal
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surface if and only if
\varphi
is a broad* solution to
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
for some
\omega\in C(U)
. We shall recall the definition of broad* in
Section

3.4
.

Example 3.6

(Projected vector fields on Carnot groups of step 2)
.

Consider a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
of rank
m
and step 2 with an adapted basis
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
. For
1\leq s,\ell\leq m
and
m+1\leq i\leq n
, let us define the
structure constants

c_{\ell s}^{i}
by means of the relation
[X_{\ell},X_{s}]\eqqcolon\sum_{i=m+1}^{n}c_{\ell s}^{i}X_{i}
. Identify
\mathbb{G}
with
\mathbb{R}^{n}
by means of exponential coordinates and take
\mathbb{W}\coloneqq\{x_{1}=0\}
, and
\mathbb{L}\coloneqq\{x_{2}=\dots=x_{n}=0\}
. Then, if
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
is continuous, with
\widetilde{U}
open, by explicit computations one has in exponential coordinates

(31)

(D^{\varphi}_{X_{j}})_{|_{w}}=(X_{j})_{|_{w}}+\sum_{i=m+1}^{n}c_{1j}^{i}\varphi(w)(\partial_{x_{i}})_{|_{w}},\qquad\mbox{ for }j=2,\dots,m;

(32)

(D^{\varphi}_{X_{j}})_{|_{w}}=(X_{j})_{|_{w}}=(\partial_{x_{j}})_{|_{w}},\qquad\mbox{ for }j=m+1,\dots,n,

for every
w\in U
, where
\varphi
denotes the composition of
\widetilde{\varphi}
with the exponential coordinates.

Remark 3.7
.

For the expression of the projected vector fields in
Section

3.1
we refer also to
[
DD18
, Definition 5.2]
. In the papers
[
DD18
]
and
[
DD19
]
the author started to generalize the results already proved in the Heisenberg groups
\mathbb{H}^{n}
(see
Section

3.1
) to Carnot groups of step 2, in case
\mathbb{L}
is one-dimensional.

In particular, in
[
DD18
]
, in the setting of Carnot groups of step 2, the author deals with the characterization of maps
\widetilde{\varphi}
such that
\mathrm{graph}(\widetilde{\varphi})
is a
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-hypersurface. In
[
DD18
, Theorem 5.8]
, the author recovers partially the result in
[
ASCV06
, Theorem 5.1]
, thus making the first step through the complete characterization in step 2 Carnot groups analogous to the one discussed in
Section

3.1
for the Heisenberg group. We stress that in this paper of ours we generalize
[
ASCV06
, Theorem 5.1]
to all step-2 Carnot groups, thus improving also
[
DD18
, Theorem 5.8]
, see
Theorem

6.17
.

In
[
DD19
, Theorem 7.1 & 7.2]
, in the setting of Carnot groups of step 2, with
\mathbb{L}
one-dimensional, the author recovers
[
BCSC14
, Theorem 1.1]
with an additional assumption:
\mathrm{graph}(\widetilde{\varphi})
is intrinsically Lipschitz if and only if
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
in the sense of distribution for some
\omega\in L^{\infty}(U)

and
\varphi
is locally
1/2
-Hölder along the vertical coordinates
. We expect that the techniques of
Section

6
can be used to drop the previous additional assumption on the locally 1/2-Hölder continuity along the vertical coordinates. This will be subject of further investigations.

Example 3.8

(Projected vector fields on Engel group)
.

Consider the Engel group
\mathbb{E}
, which is the Carnot group of topological dimension
4
whose Lie algebra
\mathfrak{e}
admits an adapted basis
(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},X_{4})
such that

\mathfrak{e}\coloneqq\mathrm{span}\{X_{1},X_{2}\}\oplus\mathrm{span}\{X_{3}\}\oplus\mathrm{span}\{X_{4}\},

where the only nonvanishing relations are
[X_{1},X_{2}]=X_{3},[X_{1},X_{3}]=X_{4}
. We identify
\mathbb{E}
with
\mathbb{R}^{4}
by means of exponential coordinates, and we define
\mathbb{W}\coloneqq\{x_{1}=0\}
, and
\mathbb{L}\coloneqq\{x_{2}=x_{3}=x_{4}=0\}
. Then, by explicit computations that can be found in
[
Koz15
, Section 4.4.1]
, we get that, given a continuous function
\widetilde{\varphi}:\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
, with
\widetilde{U}
open, the projected vector fields on
\mathbb{W}
are

(33)

D_{X_{2}}^{\varphi}=\partial_{x_{2}}+\varphi\partial_{x_{3}}+\frac{\varphi^{2}}{2}\partial_{x_{4}},\quad D_{X_{3}}^{\varphi}=\partial_{x_{3}}+\varphi\partial_{x_{4}},\quad D^{\varphi}_{X_{4}}=\partial_{x_{4}}.

In
[
Koz15
, Setion 4.4.1]
, one can find the computations of the projected vector fields also for the pair of complementary subgroups
\left(\{x_{2}=0\},\{x_{1}=x_{3}=x_{4}=0\}\right)
. It is worth mentioning that in
[
Koz15
, Section 4.5]
there are some counterexamples, for the Engel group
\mathbb{E}
with the splitting discussed here, to some of the statements, discussed in
Section

3.1
, and
Section

3.1
, that holds for Carnot groups of step 2. For one of these examples, see also
Section

4.4
.

We now prove a proposition about the general form of the projected vector fields, in an arbitrary Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
, in exponential coordinates. The proposition below can be found in
[
Koz15
, Proposition 4.1.15]
, but we however write the proof for the sake of completeness.

Proposition 3.9

(Projected vector fields in coordinates)
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
such that
\mathbb{L}
is horizontal and
k
-dimensional, and let
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
