be a continuous function on an open set
\widetilde{U}
. Fix an adapted basis
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
such that
\mathbb{W}=\exp\left(\mathrm{span}\{X_{k+1},\dots,X_{n}\}\right)
and
\mathbb{L}=\exp\left(\mathrm{span}\{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\}\right)
. If we identify
\mathbb{G}
with
\mathbb{R}^{n}
by means of exponential coordinates associated with
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
, then the vector fields
D^{\varphi}_{j}\coloneqq D^{\varphi}_{X_{j}}
defined in (
26
) have the following expression:

(34)

D^{\varphi}_{j|_{(0,\dots,0,x_{k+1},\dots,x_{n})}}=\partial_{x_{j}}+\sum_{i=n_{\deg j}+1}^{n}P_{i}^{j}(\varphi^{(1)},\dots,\varphi^{(k)},x_{k+1},\dots,x_{n_{\deg i-1}})\partial_{x_{i}},\quad\forall j=k+1,\dots,n,

where
\varphi
is the composition of
\widetilde{\varphi}
with the exponential map,
\varphi^{(i)}=\varphi^{(i)}(x_{k+1},\dots,x_{n})
denotes the
i
-th component of
\varphi
, and
P_{i}^{j}
is a polynomial of homogeneous degree
\deg i-\deg j
, with the convention that the degree of the
\varphi^{(i)}
components in the polynomial is 1. For the notation
n_{\square}
and
\deg
, see the discussion before
Section

2.1
.

Proof.

Since
\mathbb{L}
is horizontal, then
\mathbb{W}
is normal and (
28
) holds. Now the result follows from (
28
) and the following general fact that holds for arbitrary Carnot groups. Let us fix, in exponential coordinates,
x\in\mathbb{G}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The differential of the left (respectively right) translation evaluated at a point
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{G}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{n}
, that we denote by
\de(L_{x})_{x^{\prime}}
(respectively
\de(R_{x})_{x^{\prime}}
), is a matrix with identity
(m_{\ell}\times m_{\ell})
-blocks on the diagonal, with
1\leq\ell\leq s
, and moreover the element of position
ij
is a polynomial in the coordinates of
x,x^{\prime}
of homogeneous degree
\deg i-\deg j
, if
\deg i>\deg j
. Instead if
\deg i<\deg j
the element of position
ij
is zero. This last statement about the structure of the differential of left and right translations follows by the explicit expression of the product in coordinates, see
[
FSSC03a
, Proposition 2.1]
.
∎

Remark 3.10
.

Notice that, for notational purposes,
Section

3.1
is stated just for
\mathbb{L}
horizontal but an expression similar to (
34
) holds also in the more general case in which
\mathbb{W}
is normal, see also
[
Koz15
, Proposition 4.1.15]
. The difference is that the zeros should be put in the components of
\mathbb{L}
, which are not necessarily all in the first layer, and the
\varphi^{(i)}
components in the polynomial are not necessarily of degree 1.

Lemma 3.11

(Locally connectible with projected vector fields)
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be complementary subgroups of
\mathbb{G}
with
\mathbb{W}
normal, and let
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
be a continuous function on an open set
\widetilde{U}
. Then, for every
\widetilde{U}^{\prime}\Subset\widetilde{U}
and every
\widetilde{w}\in\widetilde{U}^{\prime}
, there exists a neighborhood
\widetilde{V}\Subset\widetilde{U}^{\prime}
of
\widetilde{w}
such that, for every
\widetilde{v},\widetilde{v}^{\prime}\in\widetilde{V}
there exists a path, entirely contained in
\widetilde{U}^{\prime}
, connecting
\widetilde{v}
to
\widetilde{v}^{\prime}
, made of a finite concatenation of integral curves for the vector fields
D^{\varphi}_{W}
, for
W\in{\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})
.

Proof.

We give the proof in the case
\mathbb{L}
is horizontal and
k
-dimensional. The same proof can be given in the general case in which
\mathbb{W}
is normal taking
Section

3.1
into account. We fix an adapted basis
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, such that
\mathbb{W}=\exp(\mbox{span}\{X_{k+1},\dots,X_{n}\})
and
\mathbb{L}=\exp(\mbox{span}\{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\})
. We denote by
\varphi\colon U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n-k}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
the composition of
\widetilde{\varphi}
with the exponential coordinates and we set
D^{\varphi}_{j}\coloneqq D_{X_{j}}^{\varphi}
for every
j=k+1,\dots,n
. Using the particular form of
D^{\varphi}_{j}
, see (
34
), we shall prove that
U
is locally connectible by means of integral curves of the vector fields
D^{\varphi}_{j}
, with
j=k+1,\dots,n
. Indeed, if we fix
w\in U
, and
j\in\{k+1,\dots,n\}
, Peano’s Theorem
[
Hal80
, Theorem 1.1]
and the estimate for the existing time
[
Hal80
, Corollary 1.1]
imply that, for every open neighborhood
U^{\prime}\Subset U
of
w
, there exist
0<\alpha\coloneqq\alpha(U^{\prime},\varphi)
and an open neighborhood
V^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq V^{\prime\prime}(U^{\prime},\varphi)
of
w
with
V^{\prime\prime}\Subset U^{\prime}
, such that, for every
v\in V^{\prime\prime}
, there exists at least one integral curve
\gamma
of
D^{\varphi}_{j}
starting from
v
, defined in
[-\alpha,\alpha]
, and such that
\gamma([-\alpha,\alpha])\subseteq U^{\prime}
.

We can iterate the argument for each
j
, eventually considering a smaller neighborhood
V^{\prime\prime}
and a smaller time at each stage of the iteration. Thus we obtain a neighborhood
V^{\prime}\Subset U^{\prime}
of
w
and a time
\delta>0
such that, starting from an arbitrary point in
V^{\prime}
, there exists a concatenation of
n-k
integral curves of
D^{\varphi}_{j}
, with
j=k+1,\dots,n
, with interval of definition containing
[-\delta,\delta]
, and this concatenation is supported in
U^{\prime}
. Eventually, up to reducing
V^{\prime}
, we deduce that for every
U^{\prime}\Subset U
containing
w
there exists a neighborhood
V\Subset V^{\prime}\Subset U^{\prime}
of
w
such that, for every
v,v^{\prime}\in V
, there exists at least one path from
v
to
v^{\prime}
, made of integral curves of the vector fields
D^{\varphi}_{j}
, with
j=k+1,\dots,n
, entirely contained in
U^{\prime}
. We remark that this can be done taking into account that any integral curve of
D^{\varphi}_{j}
is a line along the
j
-th coordinate, and adjusting one coordinate at time. When we adjust one coordinate, we do not have the check the previous ones because of the triangular form of the vector fields
D^{\varphi}_{j}
, see (
34
).
∎

3.2.
Invariance properties of
D^{\varphi}

Now we prove some invariance properties of the vector fields
D^{\varphi}
under the operation of translating graphs that we have introduced in
Section

2.3
.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be two complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
, with
\mathbb{L}

k
-dimensional and horizontal and let
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
be a continuous function defined on
\widetilde{U}
open. Take
W\in{\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})
, let
\widetilde{f}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
be a
C^{\infty}
function and let us denote
D^{\varphi}\coloneqq D^{\varphi}_{W}
. Fix
q\in\mathbb{G}
and denote by
\widetilde{\varphi}_{q}
and
\widetilde{f}_{q}
the translated functions defined as in (
15
) with domain
\widetilde{U}_{q}
. Denote by
f,\varphi\colon U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n-k}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
f_{q},\varphi_{q}\colon U_{q}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n-k}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
the composition of
\widetilde{f},\widetilde{\varphi},\widetilde{f}_{q},\widetilde{\varphi}_{q}
with the exponential coordinates, respectively. Then, for every
w\in U_{q}
, the following equality holds in exponential coordinates

(35)

D^{\varphi_{q}}_{|_{w}}(f_{q})=D^{\varphi}_{|_{\pi_{\mathbb{W}}(q^{-1}\cdot w)}}(f),

where
D^{\varphi}(f)
, for a vector valued
f
, stands for the vector
(D^{\varphi}(f^{(1)}),\dots,D^{\varphi}(f^{(k)}))
.

Proof.

We stress a little abuse of notation throughout the proof, for the sake of simplicity. We exploit the identifications as in
Section

2.1
without explicitly write the exponential map
F
or
F^{-1}
.

By (
26
) and (
16
), if we fix
w\in U_{q}
and set
g\coloneqq\pi_{\mathbb{W}}(q^{-1}\cdot w)\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}(\pi_{\mathbb{W}}(q^{-1}\cdot w))
, we get

(36)

D^{\varphi_{q}}_{|_{w}}(f_{q})=W_{|_{w\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}_{q}(w)}}(f_{q}\circ\pi_{\mathbb{W}})=W_{|_{q\cdot g}}(f_{q}\circ\pi_{\mathbb{W}})=W_{|_{g}}(f_{q}\circ\pi_{\mathbb{W}}\circ L_{q}),

where in the last equality we used the fact that
W
is left-invariant, namely
W_{|_{q\cdot g}}=\de(L_{q})_{g}(W_{|_{g}})
. By definition of
g
and the definition of
D^{\varphi}
, see (
26
), one has

(37)

D^{\varphi}_{|_{\pi_{\mathbb{W}}(q^{-1}\cdot w)}}(f)=W_{|_{g}}(f\circ\pi_{\mathbb{W}}).

Thus, taking (
36
) and (
37
) into account, we are left to show that

(38)

W_{|_{g}}(f_{q}\circ\pi_{\mathbb{W}}\circ L_{q})=W_{|_{g}}(f\circ\pi_{\mathbb{W}}).

Indeed, if
a\in\mathbb{G}
, we have

(39)

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{q}\circ\pi_{\mathbb{W}}\circ L_{q}(a)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{f}_{q}\circ\pi_{\mathbb{W}}(q\cdot a)=\widetilde{f}_{q}\circ\pi_{\mathbb{W}}\left(\left(q_{\mathbb{W}}\cdot q_{\mathbb{L}}\cdot a_{\mathbb{W}}\cdot q_{\mathbb{L}}^{-1}\right)\cdot q_{\mathbb{L}}\cdot a_{\mathbb{L}}\right)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{f}_{q}(q_{\mathbb{W}}\cdot q_{\mathbb{L}}\cdot a_{\mathbb{W}}\cdot q_{\mathbb{L}}^{-1})=q_{\mathbb{L}}\cdot\widetilde{f}(a_{\mathbb{W}})=q_{\mathbb{L}}\cdot\widetilde{f}\circ\pi_{\mathbb{W}}(a),

where in the third equality we used that
\mathbb{W}
is a normal subgroup, and in the fourth equality we used (c) of
Section

2.3
. Then, the functions
\widetilde{f}_{q}\circ\pi_{\mathbb{W}}\circ L_{q}
and
\widetilde{f}\circ\pi_{\mathbb{W}}
differ only by a left translation of the element
q_{\mathbb{L}}
. Thus, in exponential coordinates, they are
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-valued functions that differ by the fixed Euclidean translation of the
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-vector corresponding to
q_{\mathbb{L}}
. This last observation comes from the fact that, in exponential coordinates, the operation of the group restricted to
\mathbb{L}
is the Euclidean sum, being
\mathbb{L}
horizontal, see
[
FSSC03a
, Proposition 2.1]
. Finally, (
38
) holds true by the fact that, component by component, we are differentiating along a vector field two functions that differ by a fixed constant.
∎

Lemma 3.13
.

Consider the setting of the
Section

3.2
above, let
T>0
and let
\widetilde{\gamma}\colon[0,T]\to\widetilde{U}
be a
C^{1}
regular solution of the Cauchy problem

(40)

\begin{cases}\widetilde{\gamma}^{\prime}(t)=D^{\varphi}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}(t),\\
\widetilde{\gamma}(0)=w.\end{cases}

Then for every
q\in\mathbb{G}
there exists a unique
C^{1}
map
\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}\colon[0,T]\to\widetilde{U}_{q}
such that

(41)

\pi_{\mathbb{W}}(q^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}(t))=\widetilde{\gamma}(t),\qquad\forall t\in[0,T].

In addition,
\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}
is a solution of the Cauchy problem

(42)

\begin{cases}\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}^{\prime}(t)=D^{\varphi_{q}}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}(t),\\
\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}(0)=q_{\mathbb{W}}\cdot q_{\mathbb{L}}\cdot w\cdot q_{\mathbb{L}}^{-1}.\end{cases}

Moreover, if there exists a continuous function
\omega\colon U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n-k}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
such that

\varphi(\gamma(t))-\varphi(\gamma(0))=\int_{0}^{t}\omega(\gamma(s))\de\!s,\qquad\forall t\in[0,T],

then, there exists a continuous function
\bar{\omega}_{q}\colon U_{q}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n-k}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
such that

\varphi_{q}(\gamma_{q}(t))-\varphi_{q}(\gamma_{q}(0))=\int_{0}^{t}\bar{\omega}_{q}(\gamma_{q}(s))\de\!s,\qquad\forall t\in[0,T].

Proof.

We stress a little abuse of notation throughout the proof, for the sake of simplicity. We exploit the identifications as in
Section

2.1
without explicitly write the exponential map
F
or
F^{-1}
.

For every
q\in\mathbb{G}
, we define

\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}(t)\coloneqq q_{\mathbb{W}}\cdot q_{\mathbb{L}}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}(t)\cdot q_{\mathbb{L}}^{-1},\qquad\forall t\in[0,T].

Then
\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}
takes values in
\widetilde{U}_{q}
, see item (c) of
Section

2.3
, and
\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}(0)=q_{\mathbb{W}}\cdot q_{\mathbb{L}}\cdot w\cdot q_{\mathbb{L}}^{-1}
. Moreover, one also has

\pi_{\mathbb{W}}(q^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}(t))=\pi_{\mathbb{W}}(q_{\mathbb{L}}^{-1}\cdot q_{\mathbb{W}}^{-1}\cdot q_{\mathbb{W}}\cdot q_{\mathbb{L}}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}(t)\cdot q^{-1}_{\mathbb{L}})=\widetilde{\gamma}(t),\quad\forall t\in[0,T].

Moreover, if we impose (
41
), the uniqueness of
\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}
is guaranteed by the second equation of (
17
), and the equivalence

(43)

q_{\mathbb{L}}^{-1}\cdot q_{\mathbb{W}}^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}(t)\cdot q_{\mathbb{L}}=\widetilde{\gamma}(t)\Leftrightarrow\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}(t)=q_{\mathbb{W}}\cdot q_{\mathbb{L}}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}(t)\cdot q_{\mathbb{L}}^{-1},\qquad\forall t\in[0,T].

Now we want to check that
\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}
is a solution to the Cauchy problem (
42
). We work in exponential coordinates. For every vector-valued function
f\colon U_{q}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n-k}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
of class
C^{\infty}
, we have that, for every
t\in[0,T]
, it holds, in exponential coordinates, the following chain of equalities:

(44)

\displaystyle D^{\varphi_{q}}_{|_{{\gamma}_{q}(t)}}(f)

\displaystyle=D^{\varphi_{q}}_{|_{{\gamma}_{q}(t)}}((f_{q^{-1}})_{q})=D^{\varphi}_{|_{{\gamma}(t)}}(f_{q^{-1}})

\displaystyle={\gamma}^{\prime}(t)(f_{q^{-1}})=\frac{\de}{\de t}(f_{q^{-1}}\circ\gamma(t))

\displaystyle=\frac{\de}{\de t}\left(f_{q^{-1}}\circ\pi_{\mathbb{W}}\circ L_{q^{-1}}({\gamma}_{q}(t))\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\de}{\de t}\left((q^{-1})_{\mathbb{L}}\cdot f\circ\pi_{\mathbb{W}}({\gamma}_{q}(t))\right)=\frac{\de}{\de t}\left((q^{-1})_{\mathbb{L}}\cdot f\circ{\gamma}_{q}(t))\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\de}{\de t}\left(f\circ{\gamma}_{q}(t)\right)={\gamma}^{\prime}_{q}(t)(f),

where in the first equality we used item (b) of
Section

2.3
, and in the second one we used (
35
) and the fact that
\pi_{\mathbb{W}}(q^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}(t))=\widetilde{\gamma}(t)
. In the fifth equality we used the coordinate version of
\pi_{\mathbb{W}}(q^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}(t))=\widetilde{\gamma}(t)
with a little abuse of notation, and in the sixth equality we used the coordinate version of (
39
) with
q^{-1}
in place of
q
. Finally, in the eighth equality we used the fact that, being
\mathbb{L}
horizontal, the product with a fixed element of
\mathbb{L}
, in exponential coordinates, is a Euclidean translation and hence it does not affect the time derivative. This comes from the explicit expression of the product in coordinates, see
[
FSSC03a
, Proposition 2.1]
. Thus the proof of (
42
) is finished by (
44
).

By a further inspection, following the equalities starting from the right hand side of the second line to the right hand side of the fourth line of (
44
), we have proved,
only by exploiting the fact that
\mathbb{W}
is normal
, that if
\widetilde{U}
is open then for every function
\widetilde{G}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
, for every
q\in\mathbb{G}
, and every
t\in[0,T]
, it holds

(45)

\widetilde{G}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}(t)=(q^{-1})_{\mathbb{L}}\cdot\widetilde{G}_{q}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}(t).

Since
\mathbb{L}
is horizontal, in exponential coordinates this equality reads as

(46)

G\circ\gamma(t)=G_{q}\circ\gamma_{q}(t)+(q^{-1})_{\mathbb{L}}.

Assume there exists a continuous map
\omega\colon U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n-k}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
as in the statement. Then, by composing with exponential coordinates we get a continuous
\widetilde{\omega}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
. We then define
\widetilde{\omega}_{q}
as in (
15
) and we set
\omega_{q}\colon U_{q}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n-k}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
to be the composition of
\widetilde{\omega}_{q}
with the exponential coordinates. We are then in a position to define
\overline{\omega}_{q}
by setting

(47)

\overline{\omega}_{q}(x)\coloneqq\omega_{q}(x)+(q^{-1})_{\mathbb{L}},\quad\forall x\in U_{q}.

Then, we have, for every
t\in[0,T]
,

(48)

\displaystyle\varphi_{q}(\gamma_{q}(t))-\varphi_{q}(\gamma_{q}(0))

\displaystyle=\varphi(\gamma(t))-\varphi(\gamma(0))=\int_{0}^{t}\omega(\gamma(s))\de\!s=

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\left(\omega_{q}(\gamma_{q}(s))+(q^{-1})_{\mathbb{L}}\right)\de\!s=\int_{0}^{t}\bar{\omega}_{q}(\gamma_{q}(s))\de\!s,

where in the first and in the third equality we used (
46
). This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.14
.

The curve
\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}
defined in (
43
) solves (
42
) also in the general case when
\mathbb{W}
is normal, but one should run more involved computations, because the invariance property (
35
) might not be true. We presented the invariance in (
35
) in the specific case
\mathbb{L}
is horizontal and
k
-dimensional because it will be frequently used in the last theorems of this section and in
Section

4
.

We give a sketch of the proof in the general case. For a reference, one can also read the first item of
[
Koz15
, Proposition 4.2.15]
. Given
q\in\mathbb{G}
, define the map
\sigma_{q}
on
\mathbb{W}
by
\sigma_{q}(w)\coloneqq q_{\mathbb{W}}\cdot q_{\mathbb{L}}\cdot w\cdot(q_{\mathbb{L}})^{-1}
. By (
17
), we get that
\sigma_{q^{-1}}(w)=\pi_{\mathbb{W}}(q^{-1}\cdot w)
. Then, in case
\mathbb{L}
is horizontal, the invariance property proved in (
35
) reads as
D^{\varphi_{q}}_{|_{w}}=D^{\varphi}\circ\sigma_{q^{-1}}(w)
. In case
\mathbb{L}
is not horizontal, using the properties stated in
Section

2.3
, one can prove that

D^{\varphi_{q}}_{|_{w}}=\de(\sigma_{q})_{\sigma_{q^{-1}}(w)}\left(D^{\varphi}\circ\sigma_{q^{-1}}(w)\right),

for every
w\in\widetilde{U}
and
q\in\mathbb{G}
. One can hence use the previous equality and the definition of
\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}
as in (
43
) to show that, if a curve
\widetilde{\gamma}
satisfies (
40
), then
\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}
satisfies (
42
).

3.3.
Metric properties of integral curves of
D^{\varphi}

In this subsection we study how the intrinsically Lipschitz regularity of
\widetilde{\varphi}
affects the metric regularity of the integral curves of the vector fields
D^{\varphi}
(see
Section

3.3
). We also see how the conclusions obtained can be improved when we assume
\widetilde{\varphi}
to be intrinsically differentiable (
Section

3.3
) or uniformly intrinsically differentiable (see
Section

3.3
and
Section

3.3
).

The following lemma is essentially the implication
(1)\Rightarrow(3)
of
[
Koz15
, Theorem 4.2.16]
. For the reader convenience, and for some benefit toward
Section

3.3
, we give the proof here, without going through all the precise estimates, and claiming no originality.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be two complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
, with
\mathbb{W}
normal, let
\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}
be an open neighborhood of the identity
e
and let
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\to\mathbb{L}
be a function such that
\widetilde{\varphi}(e)=e
. Assume there exists a constant
C>0
with

(49)

\|\widetilde{\varphi}(w)\|_{\mathbb{G}}\leq C\|w\|_{\mathbb{G}},\qquad\forall w\in\widetilde{U}.

Then for every integer
d\geq 1
, every
W\in{\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})\cap V_{d}
, and every integral curve
\widetilde{\gamma}\colon[0,T]\to\widetilde{U}
of
D_{W}^{\varphi}
starting from
e
, there exists
C^{\prime}
depending only on
C
and
W
, such that

(50)

\|\widetilde{\gamma}(t)\|_{\mathbb{G}}\leq C^{\prime}t^{1/d},\qquad\|\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{\gamma}(t))\|_{\mathbb{G}}\leq C^{\prime}t^{1/d},\qquad\forall t\in[0,T].

Proof.

We give the proof in case
\mathbb{L}
is
k
-dimensional and horizontal, just for notational purposes. Then, taking
Section

3.1
into account, the proof can be given in the general case. We recall that, from the fact that all the homogeneous norms are equivalent, we may fix
\|(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\|_{\mathbb{G}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}|x_{i}|^{1/\deg i}
. Then, in the case
\mathbb{L}
is horizontal,
\|\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}\|_{\mathbb{G}}=|\varphi\circ\gamma|
.

We fix an adapted basis
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
such that
W=X_{j}
for some
j\in\{k+1,\dots,n\}
. Then
\deg j=d
and, according to
Section

3.1
, we have, in exponential coordinates adapted to this basis, that
D^{\varphi}_{j}\coloneqq D^{\varphi}_{X_{j}}=D^{\varphi}_{W}
writes as

(51)

D^{\varphi}_{j}|_{(0,\dots,0,x_{k+1},\dots,x_{n})}=\partial_{x_{j}}+\sum_{i=n_{d}+1}^{n}P_{i}^{j}(\varphi^{(1)},\dots,\varphi^{(k)},x_{k+1},\dots,x_{n_{\deg i-1}})\partial_{x_{i}},

for some polynomials
P^{j}_{i}
of homogeneous degree
\mathrm{deg}i-d
. By the use of triangle inequality and Young’s inequality, we get that for every
i=n_{d}+1,\dots,n
there exists a constant
C_{1,i}>0
depending only on the polynomial
P_{i}^{j}
, and a constant
C_{2,i}>0
that depends only on
C_{1,i}
and on
n
, such that

(52)

\displaystyle|P_{i}^{j}(\varphi^{(1)},\dots,\varphi^{(k)},x_{k+1},\dots,x_{n_{\deg i-1}})|

\displaystyle\leq C_{1,i}\|(0,\dots,0,\varphi^{(1)},\dots,\varphi^{(k)},x_{k+1},\dots,x_{n_{\deg i-1}})\|_{\mathbb{G}}^{\deg i-d}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2,i}(\|(0,\dots,0,\varphi^{(1)},\dots,\varphi^{(k)},0,\dots,0)\|_{\mathbb{G}}^{\deg i-d}

\displaystyle+\|(0,\dots,0,x_{k+1},\dots,x_{n})\|_{\mathbb{G}}^{\deg i-d}).

Fix
t\in[0,T]
and define

m_{\gamma}(t)\coloneqq\max_{s\in[0,t]}\|\widetilde{\gamma}(s)\|_{\mathbb{G}},\qquad m_{\varphi}(t)\coloneqq\max_{s\in[0,t]}|\varphi(\gamma(s))|.

Then, by the fact that
\widetilde{\gamma}
is an integral curve of
D^{\varphi}_{j}
and the particular form of
D^{\varphi}_{j}
in (
51
), we get that
\gamma_{j}(t)=t
for every
t\in[0,T]
, and
\gamma_{\ell}(t)\equiv 0
for every
t\in[0,T]
and every
\ell\neq j
with
1\leq\ell\leq n_{d}
. The estimate (
52
) implies that, for every
i\geq n_{d}+1
, one has

(53)

\begin{split}|\gamma_{i}(s)|&\leq\int_{0}^{s}\left|P_{i}^{j}\left(\varphi^{(1)}(\gamma(r)),\dots,\varphi^{(k)}(\gamma(r)),\gamma_{k+1}(r),\dots,\gamma_{n_{\deg i-1}}(r)\right)\right|\de\!r\\
&\leq tC_{2,i}\left((m_{\gamma}(t))^{\deg i-d}+(m_{\varphi}(t))^{\deg i-d}\right)\leq tC_{3,i}(m_{\gamma}(t))^{\deg i-d},\qquad\forall s\in[0,t],\end{split}

where in the last inequality we used
m_{\varphi}(t)\leq Cm_{\gamma}(t)
for every
t\in[0,T]
, that comes from the hypothesis (
49
), and where
C_{3,i}
depends only on
C_{2,i}
and
C
. Thus, from (
53
) and the fact that the only other nonzero component of
\gamma
is
\gamma_{j}(t)=t
, we get

\begin{split}\|\widetilde{\gamma}(s)\|_{\mathbb{G}}&\leq C_{4}\left(t^{1/d}+\sum_{i=m_{d}+1}^{n}t^{1/\deg i}(m_{\gamma}(t))^{1-d/\deg i}\right)\\
&\leq nC_{4}\max_{i\in\{n_{d}+1,\dots,n\}}\left\{t^{1/d},t^{1/\deg i}(m_{\gamma}(t))^{1-d/\deg i}\right\},\end{split}

for every
s\in[0,t]
, where
C_{4}
depends only on all the constants
C_{3,i}
, with
i\geq m_{d}+1
. Maximizing the previous inequality with respect to
s\in[0,t]
, gives

m_{\gamma}(t)\leq nC_{4}\max_{i\in\{n_{d}+1,\dots,n\}}\left\{t^{1/d},t^{1/\deg i}(m_{\gamma}(t))^{1-d/\deg i}\right\},

for every
t\in[0,T]
. As a consequence of this inequality we get
m_{\gamma}(t)\leq C_{5}t^{1/d}
for all
t\in[0,T]
, for a constant
C_{5}
depending only on
C_{4}
and on
\mathbb{G}
. We have thus proved

\|\widetilde{\gamma}(t)\|_{\mathbb{G}}\leq C_{5}t^{1/d},\qquad\forall t\in[0,T].

To conclude the proof, it is enough to use (
49
) and choose
C^{\prime}\coloneqq\max\{C_{5},CC_{5}\}
. Finally, taking into account all the dependencies of the constants, the constant
C^{\prime}
only depends on
C
and on the coefficients of
D^{\varphi}_{j}
in coordinates, and thus ultimately on the vector field
W\in\mathrm{Lie}(\mathbb{W})
.
∎

Remark 3.16
.

Condition (
49
) in
Section

3.3
can be deduced as soon as
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
is intrinsically Lipschitz at
e
, see the item (c) of
Section

2.3
. We will give a general statement in this direction in the forthcoming
Section

3.3
, that is a restatement of the implication
(1)\Rightarrow(2)\&(3)
of
[
Koz15
, Theorem 4.2.16]
.

Notice that in
Section

3.3
we only exploited the particular triangular form of
D^{\varphi}_{j}
, see (
34
). The same result as in
Section

3.3
holds if we take the integral curves, starting from
e
, of any vector field of the same form as the right hand side of (
51
), satisfying the homogeneity conditions on the polynomials
P^{j}_{i}
given in
Section

3.1
. On the contrary, for the forthcoming
Section

3.3
, we need that we are dealing precisely with the vector fields
D^{\varphi}_{j}
in order to use the invariance properties in
Section

3.2
, and
Section

3.2
.

Proposition 3.17
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be two complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
, with
\mathbb{W}
normal, let
\widetilde{U}
be an open subset of
\mathbb{W}
, and let
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\to\mathbb{L}
be an intrinsically
L
-Lipschitz function with Lipschitz constant
L>0
.

Then for every integer
d\geq 1
, every
W\in{\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})\cap V_{d}
, and every integral curve
\widetilde{\gamma}\colon[0,T]\to\widetilde{U}
of
D_{W}^{\varphi}
, there exists a constant
C^{\prime}>0
depending only on
L
and
W
such that

(54)

\|\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{\gamma}(s))^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}^{-1}(s)\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}(t)\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{\gamma}(s))\|_{\mathbb{G}}\leq C^{\prime}|t-s|^{1/d},\quad\mbox{for }\quad 0\leq s<t\leq T;

(55)

\|\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{\gamma}(s))^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{\gamma}(t))\|_{\mathbb{G}}\leq C^{\prime}|t-s|^{1/d},\quad\mbox{for }\quad 0\leq s<t\leq T.

Proof.

Fix
s\in[0,T]
and define
q\coloneqq\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{\gamma}(s))^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}(s)^{-1}
. By exploiting item (d) of
Section

2.3
, item (c) of
Section

2.3
, and the fact that
\widetilde{\varphi}
is intrinsically
L
-Lipschitz, we get that
\widetilde{\varphi}_{q}(e)=e
and
\|\widetilde{\varphi}_{q}(w)\|_{\mathbb{G}}\leq L\|w\|_{\mathbb{G}}
for every
w\in\widetilde{U}_{q}
. We now apply
Section

3.2
to get that the curve
\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}
defined by
\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}(t)\coloneqq\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{\gamma}(s))^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}^{-1}(s)\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}(t)\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{\gamma}(s))
is an integral curve of the vector field
D^{\varphi_{q}}_{W}
. Notice that this curve takes values in
\widetilde{U}_{q}
as noticed in
Section

3.2
. We stress that
Section

3.2
is stated only for
\mathbb{L}
horizontal, but it also holds in case
\mathbb{W}
is normal, see
Section

3.2
.

Define
\widetilde{\gamma}^{+}_{q}(\cdot)\coloneqq\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}(\cdot+s)
. Since
\widetilde{\gamma}^{+}_{q}(0)=e
, we are in a position to apply
Section

3.3
to the function
\widetilde{\varphi}_{q}
and to the curve
\widetilde{\gamma}^{+}_{q}
. Evaluating the first inequality of (
50
) at time
t-s
we get (
54
). Finally, by (
45
) - that holds in the general case in which
\mathbb{W}
is normal - and by evaluating the second inequality of (
50
) at time
t-s
, we get (
55
).
∎

Remark 3.18

(An improvement of
[
ALD19
, Proposition 6.6]
)
.

A simple modification of the proof of
Section

3.3
provides a general argument for the second part of
[
ALD19
, Proposition 6.6]
, that was proved only in the setting of Carnot groups of step 2 and in case
\mathbb{L}
is 1-dimensional. The generalization reads as follows. Fix two complementary subgroups
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
, with
\mathbb{W}
normal, and an intrinsically Lipschitz function
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
, where
\widetilde{U}
is open. Consider a solution
\widetilde{\gamma}:I\to\widetilde{U}
of

(56)

\widetilde{\gamma}^{\prime}(t)=\sum_{j=k+1}^{m}a_{j}(t)(D^{\varphi}_{X_{j}})_{|_{\widetilde{\gamma}(t)}},

for some controls
a_{j}(t)
of class
L^{\infty}(I)
, where
\{X_{k+1},\dots,X_{m}\}
is a basis of
\mathrm{Lie}(\mathbb{W})\cap V_{1}
. Then the curve
\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}
is Lipschitz.

To prove this last statement one first proves the analogous of
Section

3.3
for curves satisfying (
56
). The estimates are done in the same way but the constant
C^{\prime}
also depends on a uniform bound of the controls
a_{j}(\cdot)
in
L^{\infty}(I)
. In order to conclude, one can run the same argument of
Section

3.3
, taking into account that the invariance property shown in
Section

3.2
also holds for curves satisfying (
56
) with exactly the same proof. For the sketch of the proof of
Section

3.2
in the general case when
\mathbb{W}
is normal we refer the reader to
Section

3.2
.

The forthcoming
Section

3.3
is, to our knowledge, new. It tells us what are the metric properties of the integral curves
\widetilde{\gamma}
of
D^{\varphi}
whenever
\widetilde{\varphi}\in{\rm ID}(\widetilde{U},\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
. The counterpart of
Section

3.3
in the setting of the Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{n}
is already known: for the case in which
\mathbb{L}
is one-dimensional, the proof follows from the argument of
[
SC16
, Theorem 4.95,
(3)\Rightarrow(2)
]
, while for the case in which
\mathbb{L}
is
k
-dimensional, the proof is in
[
Cor19
, Proposition 4.6]
. A weaker version of this proposition, which also requires
\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}
to be
C^{1}
, has appeared in
[
ASCV06
, Proposition 3.7]
in the Heisenberg group, for
\mathbb{L}
one-dimensional, and in
[
DD18
, Proposition 5.6]
in Carnot groups of step 2, for
\mathbb{L}
one-dimensional.

Proposition 3.19
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be two complementary subgroups in a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
, with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and k-dimensional, let
\widetilde{U}
be an open set in
\mathbb{W}
and let
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\to\mathbb{L}
be an intrinsically differentiable function at
w_{0}\in\widetilde{U}
. Then the following facts hold.

(i)

For every
W\in{\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})\cap V_{1}
and every integral curve
\widetilde{\gamma}\colon[0,T]\to\widetilde{U}
of
D_{W}^{\varphi}
starting from
w_{0}
, the composition
\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}
is differentiable at 0 and

(57)

\frac{\de}{\de t}_{|_{t=0}}(\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\widetilde{\gamma})(t)=\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi(w_{0})(\exp W).

(ii)

For every
d>1
,
W\in{\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})\cap V_{d}
and every integral curve
\widetilde{\gamma}\colon[0,T]\to\widetilde{U}
of
D^{\varphi}_{W}
starting from
w_{0}
, the following holds:

(58)

\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\|\widetilde{\varphi}(w_{0})^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{\gamma}(t))\|_{\mathbb{G}}}{t^{1/d}}=0.

Proof.

Notice that, since
\mathbb{L}
is horizontal, the homogeneous norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{G}}
restricted to
\mathbb{L}
is equivalent to the Euclidean norm of the exponential coordinates. First of all, by item (i) of
Section

2.3
,
\widetilde{\varphi}
is continuous at
w_{0}
. If we define
q\coloneqq{\widetilde{\varphi}}(w_{0})^{-1}\cdot w_{0}^{-1}
we get, from item (d) of
Section

2.3
, that
\widetilde{\varphi}_{q}(e)=e
and, from item (ii) of
Section

2.3
, that
\widetilde{\varphi}_{q}
is intrinsically differentiable at
e
with
\de^{\varphi_{q}}\!\varphi_{q}(e)=\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi(w_{0})
. By (
21
), (
20
) and the triangle inequality, for every
\widetilde{V}\Subset\widetilde{U}_{q}
containing
e
, there exists a constant
C
such that
|\varphi_{q}(w)|\leq C\|w\|_{\mathbb{G}}
for every
w\in\widetilde{V}
.

(i) Fix
W\in{\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})\cap V_{1}
and
\widetilde{\gamma}
as in the assumption. By the first part of
Section

3.2
, there exists
\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}\colon[0,T]\to\widetilde{U}_{q}\subseteq\mathbb{W}
such that
\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}
is an integral curve of
D^{\varphi_{q}}_{W}
starting from
e
. Then, since
|\varphi_{q}(w)|\leq C\|w\|_{\mathbb{G}}
for every
w\in\widetilde{V}
, and since for sufficiently small times
t>0
it holds
\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}([0,t])\subseteq\widetilde{V}
, we are in the setting of
Section

3.3
. Thus, from the first inequality in (
50
), we can write

\limsup_{t\to 0}\frac{\|\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}(t)\|_{\mathbb{G}}}{t}<+\infty.

Fix an adapted basis
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
such that
\mathbb{L}=\exp(\mathrm{span}\{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\})
and
\mathbb{W}=\exp(\mathrm{span}\{X_{k+1},\dots,X_{n}\})
. We use the notation of
Section

2.1
and
Section

2.3
. By using the previous inequality we get that there is a constant
\bar{C}>0
such that, for every small enough
t\in[0,T]
,

(59)

\frac{|\varphi_{q}(\gamma_{q}(t))-\varphi_{q}(\gamma_{q}(0))-t\nabla^{\varphi_{q}}\varphi_{q}(e)(W)|}{t}\leq\bar{C}\frac{|\varphi_{q}(\gamma_{q}(t))-\varphi_{q}(\gamma_{q}(0))-t\nabla^{\varphi_{q}}\varphi_{q}(e)(W)|}{\|\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}(t)\|_{\mathbb{G}}}.

Notice that
\varphi_{q}(\gamma_{q}(0))=\varphi_{q}(0)=0
. Moreover, using the particular form of the projected vector fields in (
34
) and the fact that
W\in V_{1}
, it is easy to see that
\pi_{\mathbb{V}_{1}}(\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}(t))=\exp(tW)
for all
t\in[0,T]
. By exploiting the fact that the intrinsic differential is linear on the horizontal components (see
Section

2.3
), we get that for all
t\in[0,T]

t\nabla^{\varphi_{q}}\varphi_{q}(e)(W)=\nabla^{\varphi_{q}}\varphi_{q}(e)(tW)=\exp^{-1}\left(\de^{\varphi_{q}}\!\varphi_{q}(e)(\exp(tW))\right).

Let us conclude the proof. The intrinsic differentiability of
\widetilde{\varphi}_{q}
at
e
provides (
21
). Thus, by exploiting
\pi_{\mathbb{V}_{1}}(\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}(t))=\exp(tW)
for all
t\in[0,T]
, the previous equality, and the fact that the intrinsic differential depends only on the projection on
\mathbb{V}_{1}
(see
Section

2.3
), the right hand side of (
59
) goes to zero as
t\to 0
. Thus, also the left hand side goes to zero as
t\to 0
and this means that

\frac{\de}{\de t}_{|_{t=0}}(\varphi_{q}\circ\gamma_{q})(t)=\nabla^{\varphi_{q}}\varphi_{q}(e)(W).

By using (
46
), and since
\nabla^{\varphi_{q}}\varphi_{q}(e)=\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi(w_{0})
, we get (
57
). This concludes the proof of (i).

(ii) Assume
W\in{\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})\cap V_{d}
with
d>1
, and
\widetilde{\gamma}
as in the assumption. We proceed with the same argument as in (i). Then, following the lines of the proof in item (i) and by the first inequality in (
50
), we obtain that there exists
\bar{C}>0
such that for sufficiently small
t\in[0,T]

(60)

\frac{|\varphi_{q}(\gamma_{q}(t))-\varphi_{q}(\gamma_{q}(0))|}{t^{1/d}}\leq\bar{C}\frac{|\varphi_{q}(\gamma_{q}(t))-\varphi_{q}(\gamma_{q}(0))|}{\|\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}(t)\|_{\mathbb{G}}}.

Since
W\in V_{d}
with
d>1
and
\varphi_{q}(0)=0
, the projection of every integral curve of
D^{\varphi_{q}}_{W}
, starting from 0, on the horizontal bundle is zero. This follows by exploiting the particular form of
D^{\varphi_{q}}
in coordinates, see (
34
). Then the intrinsic differentiability of
\widetilde{\varphi}_{q}
at
e
jointly with (
60
), the fact that the projection of
\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}
on
\mathbb{V}_{1}
is zero, and that the intrinsic gradient is linear on
V_{1}
(see
Section

2.3
) yields, with the same reasoning as before,

\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{|\varphi_{q}(\gamma_{q}(t))-\varphi_{q}(\gamma_{q}(0))|}{t^{1/d}}=0.

Then, by using (
46
) we conclude (
58
) and thus the proof.
∎

Remark 3.20
.

For the ease of notation, we considered in
Section

3.3
only intervals
[0,T]
, and thus we got conclusions only on the right limits and the right derivatives. The same proof provides the same conclusion on the full limit, or the full derivative, whenever the interval is centered at the origin.

Now we want to deduce metric properties of
\widetilde{\varphi}
when we know that it is UID. The following proposition shows that any uniformly intrinsically differentiable function
\widetilde{\varphi}
is
\frac{1}{s}
-little Hölder continuous on any Carnot group of step
s
, when read in exponential coordinates. It is a generalization of
[
ASCV06
, Proposition 4.4]
.

Proposition 3.21
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be two complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and k-dimensional, and let
\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}
be an open set. If
\widetilde{\varphi}\in{\rm UID}(\widetilde{U},\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
, then such a function read in exponential coordinates is in
h^{1/s}_{\rm loc}(U;\mathbb{R}^{k})
, that is

\varphi\in C(U;\mathbb{R}^{k})
and for all
U^{\prime}\Subset U
one has

(61)

\lim_{r\to 0}\left(\sup\left\{\frac{|\varphi(b)-\varphi(a)|}{|b-a|^{1/s}}:a,b\in U^{\prime},0<|b-a|<r\right\}\right)=0.

Proof.

We fix an adapted basis
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
such that
\mathbb{L}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\})
and
\mathbb{W}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{k+1},\dots,X_{n}\})
. We use the convention in
Section

2.1
and
Section

2.3
, taking into account the little abuse of notation as in
Section

2.3
. In these coordinates, up to bi-Lipschitz equivalence, we can suppose to work with the anisotropic norm. If
a\in U
, we denote by
a^{1},\dots,a^{s}
the vector of components of
a
in each layer, so
a^{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{j}}
for every
j=1,\dots,s
and
a=(a^{1},\dots,a^{s})
. For any
a_{0}\in U
and
r>0
we set

\rho_{a_{0}}(r)\coloneqq\sup\left\{\frac{|\varphi(b)-\varphi(a)-\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi_{a_{0}}(a^{-1}b)|}{\|\widetilde{\varphi}(a)^{-1}a^{-1}b\,\widetilde{\varphi}(a)\|}\,:\,a,b\in B(a_{0},r)\cap U,a\neq b\right\}.

Assuming
\widetilde{\varphi}\in{\rm UID}(\widetilde{U},\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
, we have by (
22
)

(62)

\lim_{r\to 0}\rho_{a_{0}}(r)=0,

for every
a_{0}\in U
. Fix
U^{\prime}\Subset U
with
a_{0}\in U^{\prime}
. From
Section

2.3
, since the intrinsically linear function
\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi_{a_{0}}
depends only on the variables on the first layer of
\mathbb{W}
, and it is homogeneous, we can find a constant
C>0
depending on
a_{0}
for which

|\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi_{a_{0}}(a^{-1}b)|\leq C|b^{1}-a^{1}|,\qquad\forall a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

and, consequently, we have

(63)

\displaystyle\frac{|\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi_{a_{0}}(a^{-1}b)|}{|b-a|^{1/s}}\leq Cr^{1-1/s},

for all
a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
0<|a-b|<r<1
. By a consequence of
Section

2.1
, see
[
FS16
, Corollary 3.13]
, we have with a little abuse of notation

\widetilde{\varphi}(a)^{-1}a^{-1}b\,\widetilde{\varphi}(a)=b-a+\mathcal{P}(\widetilde{\varphi}(a),a^{-1}b),

for every
a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where

\mathcal{P}(\widetilde{\varphi}(a),a^{-1}b)\coloneqq(\mathcal{P}^{1}(\widetilde{\varphi}(a),a^{-1}b),\dots,\mathcal{P}^{s}(\widetilde{\varphi}(a),a^{-1}b)),

with
\mathcal{P}^{1}(\widetilde{\varphi}(a),a^{-1}b)=0
. Moreover, for each
i=2,\dots,s
, there is
C_{i}>0
depending only
U^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{\varphi}
such that

|\mathcal{P}^{i}(\widetilde{\varphi}(a),a^{-1}b)|\leq C_{i}\left(|b^{1}-a^{1}|+\dots+|b^{i-1}-a^{i-1}|\right),

for all
a,b\in U^{\prime}
. Hence there exists
C^{\prime}>0
depending only on
C_{i}
and on the group
\mathbb{G}
such that

\frac{\|\widetilde{\varphi}(a)^{-1}a^{-1}b\,\widetilde{\varphi}(a)\|}{|b-a|^{1/s}}\leq C^{\prime},\qquad\forall a,b\in U^{\prime}\text{ with }0<|a-b|<1.

Finally, by the last inequality together with (
63
), we get

(64)

\displaystyle\frac{|\varphi(b)-\varphi(a)|}{|b-a|^{1/s}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|\varphi(b)-\varphi(a)-\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi_{a_{0}}(a^{-1}b)|}{\|\widetilde{\varphi}(a)^{-1}a^{-1}b\,\widetilde{\varphi}(a)\|}\,\frac{\|\widetilde{\varphi}(a)^{-1}a^{-1}b\,\widetilde{\varphi}(a)\|}{|b-a|^{1/s}}+\frac{|\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi_{a_{0}}(a^{-1}b)|}{|b-a|^{1/s}}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\rho_{a_{0}}(r)+Cr^{1-1/s},

for all
a_{0}\in U
and all
a,b\in U^{\prime}\cap B(a_{0},r)
with
0<|a-b|<r<1
. We stress that, ultimately,
C^{\prime}
depends only
U^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{\varphi}
, while
C
depends only on
a_{0}
.

We conclude the proof by contradiction. Assume we can find
U^{\prime}\Subset U
, two sequences
(a_{h})
and
(b_{h})
in
U^{\prime}
, and an infinitesimal sequence
(r_{h})
of positive numbers such that
0<|a_{h}-b_{h}|<r_{h}
and

\frac{|\varphi(b_{h})-\varphi(a_{h})|}{|b_{h}-a_{h}|^{1/s}}>M,

for some
M>0
. Since
\overline{U^{\prime}}
is compact, we can assume that, up to passing to subsequences, both
(a_{h})
and
(b_{h})
converge to some
a_{0}\in\overline{U^{\prime}}
. By (
64
) we would find some
M^{\prime}>0
such that that

\rho_{a_{0}}(r_{h})>M^{\prime},

for arbitrarily large
h\in\mathbb{N}
, a contradiction to (
62
).
∎

The previous proposition tells us what is the regularity of
\varphi
in
all
the exponential coordinates, in case it is UID. Actually, we can refine
Section

3.3
by improving the property (
58
). We stress that the forthcoming proposition would also follow from the implication (1)
\Rightarrow
(2) of
[
Koz15
, Theorem 4.3.1]
but, up to our knowledge, the proof presented here is new. Indeed, in (1)
\Rightarrow
(2) of
[
Koz15
, Theorem 4.3.1]
it is proved that if the intrinsic graph of
\varphi
is a co-horizontal
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surface with complemented tangents, then (
65
) holds. Then the following
Section

3.3
would be a consequence of that implication and
Section

2.3
. Instead, we here give a direct proof within our context. In conclusion we obtain, in a different way, the implication (1)
\Rightarrow
(2) of
[
Koz15
, Theorem 4.3.1]
by making use of
Section

3.3
and
Section

2.3
.

Proposition 3.22
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be two complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
, with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and
k
-dimensional. Let
\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}
be open and
\widetilde{\varphi}\in{\rm UID}(\widetilde{U},\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
. Fix an adapted basis
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
in which
\mathbb{W}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{k+1},\dots,X_{n}\})
,
\mathbb{L}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\})
and let
\widetilde{V}\Subset\widetilde{U}
. Then

(65)

\lim_{\varrho\to 0}\!\left(\!\sup\left\{\frac{|\varphi(\gamma(t))-\varphi(\gamma(s))|}{|t-s|^{1/{\deg j}}}:j=m+1,\dots,n,\,\gamma^{\prime}=D^{\varphi}_{X_{j}}\circ\gamma,\gamma\subseteq V,0<|t-s|\leq\varrho\right\}\right)\!=0.

Proof.

We use the convention in
Section

2.1
and
Section

2.3
, taking into account the little abuse of notation as in
Section

2.3
. By item (a) of
Section

2.3
we have that
\widetilde{\varphi}
is intrinsically Lipschitz on
\widetilde{V}\Subset\widetilde{U}
. We denote by
C
the constant for which
\widetilde{\varphi}
is intrinsically
C
-Lipschitz in
\widetilde{V}
.

Fix
w_{0}\in\widetilde{V}
. Let us take
m+1\leq j\leq n
, and an integral curve
\widetilde{\gamma}\colon I\to\widetilde{V}\subseteq\mathbb{W}
of
D^{\varphi}_{X_{j}}
. Without loss of generality we may assume that the curve is defined on
I=[0,T]
, with
T>0
possibly depending on the curve. By the particular form of
D^{\varphi}_{j}
in coordinates, see (
34
), and the fact that
j\geq m+1
, we have that the projection of
\widetilde{\gamma}
on
\mathbb{V}_{1}
is constant. Then, since by
Section

2.3

\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi_{w_{0}}
depends only on the projection on
\mathbb{V}_{1}
and it is linear, we have, for all
t,s\in[0,T]
,

(66)

\frac{|\varphi(\gamma(t))-\varphi(\gamma(s))-\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi_{w_{0}}(\gamma(s)^{-1}\cdot\gamma(t))|}{\|\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{\gamma}(s))^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}(s)^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}(t)\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{\gamma}(s))\|}=\frac{|\varphi(\gamma(t))-\varphi(\gamma(s))|}{\|\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{\gamma}(s))^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}(s)^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}(t)\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{\gamma}(s))\|}.

Since
\widetilde{\varphi}
is intrinsically
C
-Lipschitz in
\widetilde{V}
, by (
54
) there exists a constant
C_{j}>0
depending only on
j,C
and the adapted basis such that

(67)

\|\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{\gamma}(s))^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}(s)^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}(t)\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{\gamma}(s))\|\leq C_{j}|t-s|^{1/\deg j},\qquad\forall 0\leq s<t\leq T.

In particular, we can find a constant
C^{\prime}>0
depending only on
C
and the adapted basis such that for every
j=m+1,\dots,n
, for every integral curve
\widetilde{\gamma}\colon[0,T]\to\widetilde{V}
of
D^{\varphi}_{X_{j}}
and every
0\leq s<t\leq T
we have

(68)

\frac{|\varphi(\gamma(t))-\varphi(\gamma(s))|}{\|\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{\gamma}(s))^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}(s)^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}(t)\cdot\varphi(\widetilde{\gamma}(s))\|}\geq\frac{|\varphi(\gamma(t))-\varphi(\gamma(s))|}{C^{\prime}|t-s|^{1/\deg j}}.

Combining (
68
) and (
66
) we get

(69)

\frac{|\varphi(\gamma(t))-\varphi(\gamma(s))|}{|t-s|^{1/\deg j}}\leq C^{\prime}\frac{|\varphi(\gamma(t))-\varphi(\gamma(s))-\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi_{w_{0}}(\gamma(s)^{-1}\cdot\gamma(t))|}{\|\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{\gamma}(s))^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}(s)^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}(t)\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{\gamma}(s))\|},

for every
j=m+1,\dots,n
, every integral curve
\widetilde{\gamma}\colon[0,T]\to\widetilde{V}
of
D^{\varphi}_{X_{j}}
, every
0\leq t<s\leq T
and every
w_{0}\in\widetilde{V}
.
If
|t-s|\leq\varrho
, by the estimate in
Section

2.1
and (
67
), we get

(70)

\|\widetilde{\gamma}(s)^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}(t)\|\leq C(\varrho),

for every
j=m+1,\dots,n
and every integral curve
\widetilde{\gamma}\colon[0,T]\to\widetilde{V}
of
D^{\varphi}_{X_{j}}
, where
C(\varrho)
is a continuous increasing function such that
\lim_{\varrho\to 0}C(\varrho)=0
, depending on
\widetilde{\varphi}
and independent on the choices of
j
and
\widetilde{\gamma}
.

Assume by contradiction that (
65
) is false. Then there exist
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, a sequence of integral curves
\widetilde{\gamma}_{\ell}\colon[0,T_{\ell}]\to\widetilde{V}
of
D^{\varphi}_{X_{i_{\ell}}}
, for some
i_{\ell}\in\{m+1,\dots,n\}
, and sequences of times
0\leq t_{\ell}<s_{\ell}\leq T_{\ell}
such that
|t_{\ell}-s_{\ell}|\to 0
as
\ell\to\infty
and

(71)

\frac{|\varphi(\gamma_{\ell}(t_{\ell}))-\varphi(\gamma_{\ell}(s_{\ell}))|}{|t_{\ell}-s_{\ell}|^{1/\deg(i_{\ell})}}\geq\varepsilon_{0}.

By compactness, up to passing to subsequences, there exists
\widetilde{v}\in\widetilde{V}
such that
\widetilde{\gamma}_{\ell}(t_{\ell})\to\widetilde{v}
. Then, by the uniform control (
70
) and the fact that
|t_{\ell}-s_{\ell}|\to 0
, we get also
\widetilde{\gamma}_{\ell}(s_{\ell})\to\widetilde{v}
. Since
\widetilde{\varphi}
is uniformly intrinsically differentiable at
\widetilde{v}
, we get, by
Section

2.3
(see (
22
)) that the right hand side of (
69
) evaluated at
\widetilde{\gamma}=\widetilde{\gamma}_{\ell}
,
t=t_{\ell}
,
s=s_{\ell}
, and
w_{0}=\widetilde{v}
goes to 0 as
\ell\to+\infty
. Passing (
69
) to the limit (with
j=i_{\ell}
) gives the sought contradiction with (
71
).
∎

Remark 3.23
.

In case
\mathbb{G}
is a Carnot group of step 2 and rank
m
, and we choose an adapted basis of the Lie algebra such that
\mathbb{L}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\})
and

\mathbb{W}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{k+1},\dots,X_{m},X_{m+1},\dots,X_{n}\}),

we have that
D_{X_{j}}^{\varphi}=\partial_{x_{j}}
for
m+1\leq j\leq n
, see
Section

3.1
. If
\widetilde{\varphi}\in{\rm UID}(\widetilde{U},\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
then
Section

3.3

tells us that on every compact subset of
U
, the function
\varphi
is uniformly
1/2
-little Hölder continuous in the vertical coordinates. Moreover, (
57
) and the fact the
\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi
is continuous, see
Section

2.3
, tell us that along the integral curves of
D_{X_{j}}^{\varphi}
, with
k+1\leq j\leq m
,
\varphi
is
C^{1}
. Then, by exploiting the triangular form of the vector fields
D^{\varphi}
in (
34
), one could use the previous informations to prove that
\varphi
is locally 1/2-little Hölder continuous in
all
the variables, and thus give an alternative proof of
Section

3.3
in the step-2 case. This is essentially the idea of the proof in
[
ASCV06
, Proposition 4.4]
.

3.4.
Definition of broad*

In this subsection we give the notion of broad* solution to the system
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
, with
\omega
continuous. Eventually we show that an intrinsically differentiable function
\varphi
with continuous intrinsic gradient
\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi
(see
Section

2.3
) is a broad* solution to
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
, with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and
k
-dimensional. Let
\widetilde{U}
be an open subset of
\mathbb{W}
and let
\widetilde{\varphi}:\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
be a continuous function. We fix an adapted basis
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, such that
\mathbb{W}=\exp(\mbox{span}\{X_{k+1},\dots,X_{n}\})
and
\mathbb{L}=\exp(\mbox{span}\{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\})
. We give the notion of broad* solution of the system

(72)

\left(\begin{matrix}D^{\varphi}_{X_{k+1}}\varphi^{(1)}&\dots&D^{\varphi}_{X_{m}}\varphi^{(1)}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
D^{\varphi}_{X_{k+1}}\varphi^{(k)}&\dots&D^{\varphi}_{X_{m}}\varphi^{(k)}\\
\end{matrix}\,\right)=\left(\,\begin{matrix}\omega_{1\,k+1}&\dots&\omega_{1\,m}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\omega_{k\,k+1}&\dots&\omega_{k\,m}\\
\end{matrix}\,\right),

where
\omega\coloneqq\left(\omega_{\ell j}\right)\colon U\to\mathbb{R}^{k\times(m-k)}
, with
\ell\in\{1,\dots,k\}
,
j\in\{k+1,\dots,m\}
, is a continuous matrix valued function, and where we refer to the notation introduced in
Section

2.1
.

Definition 3.24

(Broad* and broad solutions)
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
, with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and
k
-dimensional. Let
\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}
be open and let
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\to\mathbb{L}
be a continuous function. Consider an adapted basis
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
such that
\mathbb{L}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\})
and
\mathbb{W}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{k+1},\dots,X_{n}\})
. Let
\omega\coloneqq\left(\omega_{\ell j}\right)\colon U\to\mathbb{R}^{k\times(m-k)}
be a continuous matrix valued function with
\ell\in\{1,\dots,k\}
and
j\in\{k+1,\dots,m\}
. We say that
\varphi\eqqcolon(\varphi^{(1)},\dots,\varphi^{(k)})\in C(U;\mathbb{R}^{k})
is a
broad* solution
of
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
in
U
if for every
a_{0}\in U
there exist
0<\delta_{2}<\delta_{1}
and
m-k
maps
E_{j}^{\varphi}\colon\overline{B(a_{0},\delta_{2})}\times[-\delta_{2},\delta_{2}]\to\overline{B(a_{0},\delta_{1})}
for
j=k+1,\dots,m
,
where the balls are considered restricted to
U
, satisfying the following two properties.

(a)

For every
a\in\overline{B(a_{0},\delta_{2})}
and every
j=k+1,\dots,m
, the map
E_{j}^{\varphi}(a)\coloneqq E_{j}^{\varphi}(a,\cdot)
is
C^{1}
regular and it is a solution of the Cauchy problem

\begin{cases}\dot{\gamma}=D^{\varphi}_{j}\circ\gamma&\\
\gamma(0)=a,&\end{cases}

in the interval
[-\delta_{2},\delta_{2}]
, where the vector field
D^{\varphi}_{j}\coloneqq D^{\varphi}_{X_{j}}
is defined in (
26
).

(b)

For every
a\in\overline{B(a_{0},\delta_{2})}
, for every
t\in[-\delta_{2},\delta_{2}]
, every
j=k+1,\dots,m
and every
\ell=1,\dots,k
one has

\varphi^{(\ell)}(E_{j}^{\varphi}(a,t))-\varphi^{(\ell)}(a)=\int_{0}^{t}\omega_{\ell j}(E_{j}^{\varphi}(a,s))\,\de\!s.

We say that
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
in the
broad sense on
U
if for every
W\in{\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})\cap V_{1}
and every
\gamma:I\to U
integral curve of
D^{\varphi}_{W}
, it holds that

\frac{\de}{\de s}_{|_{s=t}}(\varphi\circ\gamma)(s)=\omega(W)(\gamma(t)),\qquad\forall t\in I,

where by
\omega(W)
we mean the matrix
\omega
applied to the
(m-k)
-vector
W
.

Remark 3.25
.

We stress that, in the setting of
Section

3.4
, if
\widetilde{\varphi}\in C^{1}(\widetilde{U})
then
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi
both pointwise and in the broad sense on
U
. First
\widetilde{\varphi}\in{\rm UID}(\widetilde{U},\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
by
[
DD18
, Theorem 4.9]
, because
\widetilde{\varphi}\in C^{1}(\widetilde{U})
. Then we can consider the intrinsic gradient
\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi
as in
Section

2.3
, which is continuous, see
Section

2.3
. Thus the claim is an outcome of point (i) of
Section

3.3
and point (c) of
Section

4.2
, that becomes a pointwise equality if
\widetilde{\varphi}
is
C^{1}(\widetilde{U})
, see the proof of
Section

4.2
.

Remark 3.26
.

Let us notice that the definition given in
Section

3.4
is a priori susceptible to the choice of an adapted basis. Nevertheless, when it is coupled with the vertically broad* hölder condition in the same basis, see
Section

4.1
, it is independent of this choice. This is an outcome of
Theorem

4.17
. Indeed, from (d)
\Rightarrow
(a) of
Theorem

4.17
, it follows that the broad* condition and the vertically broad* hölder condition on a fixed basis imply that
\widetilde{\varphi}
is UID. Thus, from item (i) of
Section

3.3
, we get that the broad* condition is satisfied for every other basis. Finally, from
Section

3.3
, we get that also the vertically broad* hölder condition holds in every other basis.

With the above reasoning, we remark that we can conclude something stronger: if the broad* condition and the vertically broad* hölder condition hold on a fixed basis, then they hold uniformly on the choice of
W\in{\rm Lie}(\mathbb{W})
with bounded norm, see
Section

1.2
, and
Section

1.2
.

The following result is already known in the Heisenberg groups
\mathbb{H}^{n}
: in case
\mathbb{L}
is one-dimensional, it is proved in
[
SC16
, (3)
\Rightarrow
(2) & Theorem 4.95]
, while in case
\mathbb{L}
is
k
-dimensional it is proved in
[
Cor19
, Theorem 1.4, (iii)
\Rightarrow
(ii)]
. We here generalize it to arbitrary Carnot groups, in the case
\mathbb{L}
is horizontal and
k
-dimensional.

Proposition 3.27
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
, with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and
k
-dimensional, and consider an adapted basis of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
such that
\mathbb{W}=\exp(\mathrm{span}\{X_{k+1},\dots,X_{n}\})
and
\mathbb{L}=\exp(\mathrm{span}\{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\})
. Let
\widetilde{U}
be an open subset of
\mathbb{W}
, and
\widetilde{\varphi}\in{\rm ID}(\widetilde{U},\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
be such that
\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi
is continuous on
\widetilde{U}
. Denote by
\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi
the
k\times(m-k)
matrix that represents
\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi
in coordinates, see
Section

2.3
.

Then, we have that

(73)

\frac{\de}{\de t}_{|_{t=t_{0}}}(\varphi^{(\ell)}\circ\gamma)(t)=\nabla^{\varphi}_{\ell j}\varphi(\gamma(t_{0})),

for every
j=k+1,\dots,m
, every integral curve
\widetilde{\gamma}\colon I\to\widetilde{U}
of
D^{\varphi}_{j}\coloneqq D^{\varphi}_{X_{j}}
, every
\ell=1,\dots,k
, and every
t_{0}\in I
. In particular the function
\varphi
is a broad solution, and thus also a broad* solution, of the system
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi
.

Proof.

Equation (
73
) directly follows from (
57
) seen in coordinates. Then, from (
73
) and the fact that
\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi
is continuous by hypothesis, the second part of the thesis follows.
∎

4.
Main Theorems in arbitrary Carnot groups

In this section we prove
Theorem

4.17
, that is
Theorem

1.6
in the introduction. We deal with an arbitrary Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
along with a continuous function
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
, where
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
are complementary subgroups of
\mathbb{G}
, with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and
k
-dimensional, and
\widetilde{U}
is an open subset of
\mathbb{W}
.

In
Section

4.1
, we study how Hölder properties of
\widetilde{\varphi}
along integral curves of the vector fields
D^{\varphi}
as defined in (
26
) affect the intrinsic regularity of the function
\widetilde{\varphi}
. The main result of this section is a converse of
Section

3.3
: if
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
holds in the broad* sense (see
Section

3.4
) and there is, locally around every point, a family of curves satisfying the little Hölder regularity condition (
65
) (we shall call this property vertically broad* hölder regularity, see
Section

4.1
), then
\widetilde{\varphi}
is uniformly intrinsically differentiable. For the full statement, see
Section

4.1
. We notice that, taking
Section

3.3
into account, the latter proposition generalizes
[
ASCV06
, Theorem 5.7]
, which deals with the case
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{H}^{n}
and
\mathbb{L}
one-dimensional,
[
DD18
, Theorem 5.8, (4)
\Rightarrow
(2)]
, which is proved in case
\mathbb{G}
has step 2 and
\mathbb{L}
is one-dimensional, and
[
Cor19
, Theorem 5.3]
that solves the problem for
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{H}^{n}
with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and
k
-dimensional. We remark that, also in these cases, we obtain slightly stronger results, requiring just a locally 1/2-little Hölder regularity in the vertical components.

Section

4.1
could also be obtained by a combination of (2)
\Rightarrow
(1) of
[
Koz15
, Theorem 4.3.1]
and
Section

2.3
, which is proved in
[
DD18
]
. The idea of (2)
\Rightarrow
(1) in
[
Koz15
, Theorem 4.3.1]
is to show that the Hölder conditions on
\widetilde{\varphi}
along a family of integral curves, that is the assumptions of
Section

4.1
, imply that the intrinsic graph of
\widetilde{\varphi}
is a co-horizontal
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surface with complemented tangents. To prove this latter fact the author uses a characterization of co-horizontal
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surfaces by means of uniform Hausdorff convergence to tangents - see
[
Koz15
, Theorem 3.1.12]
- that is in turn based on the so-called four cones Theorem, see
[
BK14
, Theorem 1.2]
. With the independent proof we give in
Section

4.1
, with more analytic flavor, we stress we can indirectly obtain (2)
\Rightarrow
(1) of
[
Koz15
, Theorem 4.3.1]
by making use of
Section

4.1
and
Section

2.3
. We also obtained (1)
\Rightarrow
(2) of
[
Koz15
, Theorem 4.3.1]
, see the discussion before
Section

3.3
.

Our proof of
Section

4.1
requires
Section

4.1
, that is stated only for
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and
k
-dimensional. The
Section

4.1
is a converse of
Section

3.3
, i.e., it can be roughly read in the following way: the uniform Hölder regularity of the curves
\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}
, where
\widetilde{\gamma}
is an integral curve of the vector field
D^{\varphi}
, implies the intrinsically Lipschitz regularity of
\widetilde{\varphi}
. We give a proof of
Section

4.1
as we crucially need it for the proof of
Section

4.1
, but we remark that a more general statement can be given, in case
\mathbb{W}
is normal, with a proof that is very similar to the one of
Section

4.1
. Notice that the general statement with
\mathbb{W}
normal can be found in (3)
\Rightarrow
(1) of
[
Koz15
, Theorem 4.2.16]
. For more details, see
Section

4.1
.

As a by-product, we obtain some analytical results, that can have their own independent interests. The first one is given by
Section

4.1
and it states that broad* regularity implies broad regularity. Roughly speaking, having a function that is Hölder regular on a
precise family
of integral curves implies the Hölder regularity on
every
integral curve. Then, in
Section

4.1
, we prove that every intrinsically differentiable function that is vertically broad* hölder (see
Section

4.1
), and that has a continuous intrinsic gradient, is uniformly intrinsically differentiable. We do not know at present whether the assumption on the vertically broad* hölder regularity can be dropped in
Section

4.1
, see also
Section

4.1
. We expect that the hypothesis on the vertically broad* hölder regularity in
Section

4.1
can be dropped in general, see also the paragraph
Geometric characterizations of intrinsic differentiability
in the introduction. From the results proved in
[
BSC10b
]
and
[
Cor19
]
, we know that the assumption on the vertically broad* hölder regularity in
Section

4.1
is not necessary in the case of the Heisenberg groups
\mathbb{H}^{n}
, with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal
k
-dimensional, see also the introduction to
Section

5
. We stress we obtain that we can remove the assumption on the vertically broad* hölder regularity in
Section

4.1
also in the case of step-2 Carnot groups with
\mathbb{L}
one-dimensional, see
Section

6
.

In
Section

4.2
we focus on the case in which
\mathbb{L}
is one-dimensional and we prove
Section

4.2
. We present an area formula that represents the perimeter of the subgraph of a uniformly intrinsically differentiable function
\varphi
in terms of the density
\sqrt{1+|\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi|^{2}}
. For more details about the area formula and a representation involving the Hausdorff measures, we refer the reader to
Section

4.2
. In
Section

4.2
we also prove that, whenever the target
\mathbb{L}
is one-dimensional, every uniformly intrinsically differentiable function
\varphi
is
a distributional solution
of the system
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi
and, in
Section

4.2
, we deduce that if
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
holds in the broad* sense with a continuous datum
\omega
and
\varphi
is vertically broad* hölder, then
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
in the sense of distributions. We do not know, in general, if in
Section

4.2
we can remove the assumption on the vertically broad* hölder regularity. In fact, one can remove the hypothesis on the vertically broad* hölder regularity in
Section

4.2
in Heisenberg groups, and it is a consequence of the results in
[
BSC10b
]
. We stress that thanks to the results obtained in
Section

6
, we drop the assumption on the vertically broad* hölder regularity in
Section

4.2
also in the case of step-2 Carnot groups.

It is interesting to investigate the converse implication: if one has
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
in the sense of distributions with a continuous function
\omega
, is it true that
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
in the broad* sense? This is actually the case in the Heisenberg groups, see
[
BSC10a
]
, and the techniques used in
Section

6
seem a good tool to address this implication in arbitrary step-2 Carnot groups. We will not address this issue in this paper and it will be the target of further investigations. It is however interesting to notice that, in some examples besides the step-2 case and for particular
\varphi
, one can obtain that if
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
holds in the sense of distributions with a continuous function
\omega
, then
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
in the broad* sense. We will not discuss this issue in the paper, but we refer the reader to
[
ABC16
]
.

In
Section

4.3
we come back to the general case in which the target
\mathbb{L}
is horizontal and not necessarily one-dimensional. We prove that if
\varphi
is locally approximable with a sequence of smooth functions whose intrinsic derivatives converge to a continuous function
\omega
, then
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
in the broad*, see
Section

4.3
. This notion of local approximability has been first introduced and studied in
[
ASCV06
]
, see also
Section

4.3
. We exploit this result to prove that every uniformly intrinsically differentiable function
\varphi
always solves
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi
in the broad* sense.

In
Section

4.4
, we combine some of the previous results together to prove our main theorem
Theorem

4.17
, which is
Theorem

1.6
in the introduction. Notice that our result provides the generalization to all Carnot groups, and to any possible horizontal and
k
-dimensional target
\mathbb{L}
, of
[
DD18
, Theorem 5.8]
. We stress that
Theorem

4.17
will be strengthened in
Section

6
dropping the hypothesis on the vertical broad* hölder regularity in the setting of Carnot groups of step 2. We stress that, in general, the assumption on the vertical broad* hölder regularity cannot be dropped in
Theorem

4.17
, see
Section

4.4
for a counterexample in the easiest step-3 group, namely the Engel group.

4.1.
From regularity of
\varphi
along integral curves of
D^{\varphi}
to regularity of
\varphi

In this subsection we show how the Hölder regularity of
\varphi
along integral curves of
D^{\varphi}
affects the intrinsic regularity of
\widetilde{\varphi}
.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
, with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and
k
-dimensional, let
\widetilde{U}
be open, and let
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
be a continuous function with
e\in\widetilde{U}
and
\widetilde{\varphi}(e)=e
.

Let
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
be an adapted basis of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
such that
\mathbb{L}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\})
and
\mathbb{W}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{k+1},\dots,X_{n}\})
. Denote by

D^{\varphi}_{j}\coloneqq D^{\varphi}_{X_{j}}
, for every
j=k+1,\dots,n
. Let
L>0
.

Fix
v\in U
and consider a concatenation of curves
\gamma_{k+1},\dots,\gamma_{n}
in
U
connecting
0
to
v
such that
\gamma_{j}\colon I_{j}\to U
is an integral curve of
D^{\varphi}_{j}
for
j=k+1,\dots,n
. Assume that the function
\varphi\circ\gamma_{j}
is
\frac{1}{\mathrm{deg}j}
-Hölder continuous on
I_{j}
, for
j=k+1,\dots,n
, with Hölder constant
L
.
Then, there exists
C>0
only depending on
L,\mathbb{W}
,
\mathbb{L}
and the adapted basis
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
such that

(74)

|\varphi\circ\gamma_{j}(t)|\leq C\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}},\qquad\forall t\in I_{j},\quad\forall j=k+1,\dots,n,

(75)

\;\;\;|\gamma_{j}^{(i)}(t)|\leq C\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}^{\deg i},\qquad\;\forall t\in I_{j},\quad\forall i,j=k+1,\dots,n,

where
\gamma^{(i)}_{j}
denotes the
i
-th component of
\gamma_{j}
in exponential coordinates.

Proof.

Up to bi-Lipschitz equivalence, we can prove the result choosing the anisotropic norm
\|x\|_{\mathbb{G}}\coloneqq\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}|x_{\ell}|^{1/\deg\ell}
. For the sake of readability, we fix
k=1
and assume that all the layers
V_{i}
of the algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, with
i\geq 2
, are 1-dimensional, so that
\deg i=i-1
for every
i=2,\dots,n
. The proof in the general case only requires a typographical effort to deal with multiple components in each layer and the fact that we have more zeros in the components of the first layer.

We work in exponential coordinates so that
v=(0,v_{2},\dots,v_{n})
, and denote the extremal points of the concatenation of
\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{n}
by the following chain

(76)

\displaystyle e=(0,0,\dots,0)

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\gamma_{2}}(0,v_{2},r_{3,2},\dots,r_{n,2})\rightarrow_{\gamma_{3}}\dots

\displaystyle\dots

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\gamma_{j}}(0,v_{2},\dots,v_{j},r_{j+1,j},\dots,r_{n,j})\rightarrow_{\gamma_{j+1}}\dots

\displaystyle\dots

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\gamma_{n}}v=(0,v_{2},\dots,v_{n}).

In the previous chain, we used the triangular form of
D^{\varphi}_{j}
given in (
34
), so that the flow along
D^{\varphi}_{j}
does not affect the coordinates with index less than
j
. Notice also that, without loss of generality we can assume
I_{j}\subseteq[-|v_{j}-r_{j,j-1}|,|v_{j}-r_{j,j-1}|]
, with the convention
r_{2,1}\coloneqq 0
. Indeed, again by using (
34
), in order to correct the error
r_{j,j-1}
in the
j
-th component, we have to flow for a time
v_{j}-r_{j,j-1}
along
D^{\varphi}_{j}
.

We prove (
74
) and (
75
) by induction on
j
. When we say that a constant depends on
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
we mean that also depends on the chosen adapted basis
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
.

By assumption, the curve
\varphi\circ\gamma_{2}
is
L
-Lipschitz on
I_{2}
and
\varphi\circ\gamma_{2}(0)=0
. Consequently, we have that

(77)

|\varphi\circ\gamma_{2}(t)|=|\varphi\circ\gamma_{2}(t)-\varphi\circ\gamma_{2}(0)|\leq L|t|\leq L|v_{2}|\leq L\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}},\qquad\forall t\in I_{2}.

Therefore (
74
) is proved for
j=2
.

Next we shall prove (
75
) for
j=2
by means of induction on
i
. For
i=2
, we have
\gamma_{2}^{(2)}(t)=t
and then

|\gamma_{2}^{(2)}(t)|=|t|\leq|v_{2}|\leq\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}},\qquad\forall t\in I_{2}.

Assume now that for some
i_{0}\geq 2
, there is a constant
C_{2,i_{0}}>0
such that

(78)

|\gamma_{2}^{(i)}(t)|\leq C_{2,i_{0}}\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}^{\deg i}=C_{2,i_{0}}\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}^{i-1},\quad\forall t\in I_{2},\quad\forall i=2,\dots,i_{0}.

We want to prove that there exists
C_{2,i_{0}+1}>0
such that

|\gamma_{2}^{(i_{0}+1)}(t)|\leq C_{2,i_{0}+1}\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}^{\deg(i_{0}+1)}=C_{2,i_{0}+1}\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}^{i_{0}},\quad\forall t\in I_{2},

where
C_{2,i_{0}+1}
only depends on
i_{0},L,C_{2,i_{0}},\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
. By using the particular triangular form of
D_{2}^{\varphi}
in exponential coordinates, see (
34
), we get that

(79)

|\gamma_{2}^{(i_{0}+1)}(t)|=\left|\int_{0}^{t}P_{i_{0}+1}^{2}(\varphi\circ\gamma_{2}(s),\gamma_{2}^{(2)}(s),\dots,\gamma_{2}^{(i_{0})}(s))\de\!s\right|,\quad\forall t\in I_{2},

for some polynomial
P^{2}_{i_{0}+1}
of homogeneous degree
\deg(i_{0}+1)-\deg 2=i_{0}-1
. Then, from (
77
) and (
78
), we deduce there exists
C_{2,i_{0}+1}
depending on
i_{0}
,
L
,
C_{2,i_{0}}
,
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
such that

\left|P_{i_{0}+1}^{2}(\varphi\circ\gamma_{2}(t),\gamma_{2}^{(2)}(t),\dots,\gamma_{2}^{(i_{0})}(t))\right|\leq C_{2,i_{0}+1}\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}^{i_{0}-1},\qquad\forall t\in I_{2},

and thus, by using this inequality in (
79
), and since
|t|\leq|v_{2}|\leq\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}
, we get

|\gamma_{2}^{(i_{0}+1)}(t)|\leq C_{2,i_{0}+1}|t|\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}^{i_{0}-1}\leq C_{2,i_{0}+1}\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}^{i_{0}},\qquad\forall t\in I_{2}.

To conclude the proof of (
74
) and (
75
) in the case
j=2
, it is enough to set
C_{2}\coloneqq\max_{i=2,\dots,n}C_{2,i}
, where
C_{2,2}=\max\{L,1\}
.

Assume now that for some
2\leq j_{0}\leq n
and some constant
C_{j_{0}}>0
one has

(80)

|\varphi\circ\gamma_{j}(t)|\leq C_{j_{0}}\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}},\qquad\forall t\in I_{j},\quad\forall j=2,\dots,j_{0},\\

(81)

|\gamma_{j}^{(i)}(t)|\leq C_{j_{0}}\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}^{\deg i},\qquad\forall t\in I_{j},\quad\forall i\geq 2,\quad\forall j=2,\dots,j_{0}.

We want to find
C_{j_{0}+1}>0
only depending on
L,j_{0},C_{j_{0}},\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
such that

(82)

|\varphi\circ\gamma_{j_{0}+1}(t)|\leq C_{j_{0}+1}\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}},\qquad\forall t\in I_{j_{0}+1},

(83)

|\gamma_{j_{0}+1}^{(i)}(t)|\leq C_{j_{0}+1}\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}^{\deg i},\qquad\forall t\in I_{j_{0}+1},\quad\forall i=2,\dots,n.

To prove (
82
), we develop the following inequalities:

(84)

\displaystyle|\varphi\circ\gamma_{j_{0}+1}(t)|

\displaystyle\leq|\varphi\circ\gamma_{j_{0}+1}(t)-\varphi\circ\gamma_{j_{0}+1}(0)|+|\varphi\circ\gamma_{j_{0}+1}(0)|\leq L|t|^{1/\deg(j_{0}+1)}+|\varphi\circ\gamma_{j_{0}+1}(0)|

\displaystyle\leq L|v_{j_{0}+1}-r_{j_{0}+1,j_{0}}|^{1/j_{0}}+C_{j_{0}}\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}\leq L|v_{j_{0}+1}|^{1/j_{0}}+L|r_{j_{0}+1,j_{0}}|^{1/j_{0}}+C_{j_{0}}\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}

\displaystyle\leq L\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}+LC_{j_{0}}^{\frac{1}{j_{0}}}\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}+C_{j_{0}}\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}=\widetilde{C}_{j_{0}+1}\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}},\qquad\forall t\in I_{j_{0}+1},

where in the second inequality we used the fact that
\varphi\circ\gamma_{j_{0}+1}
is Hölder of constant
L
; in the third inequality we used the definition of
I_{j_{0}+1}
for the first term, while the estimate on the second term comes from (
80
) and the fact that
\gamma_{j_{0}+1}(0)
is the endpoint of
\gamma_{j_{0}}
; the fifth inequality is a consequence of (
81
), inequality
|v_{j_{0}+1}|^{1/j_{0}}\leq\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}
, and the fact that
r_{j_{0}+1,j_{0}}
is the endpoint of
\gamma_{j_{0}}^{(j_{0}+1)}
.

We are left to prove (
83
). First we notice that, by (
34
), for
2\leq i\leq j_{0}
we have
\gamma_{j_{0}+1}^{(i)}(t)\equiv v_{i}\leq\|v\|^{\deg i}_{\mathbb{G}}
for all
t\in I_{j_{0}+1}
. Using again (
34
), for
i=j_{0}+1
we have that
\gamma_{j_{0}+1}^{(j_{0}+1)}(t)=r_{j_{0}+1,j_{0}}+t
. Then, since
|t|\leq|v_{j_{0}+1}-r_{j_{0}+1,j_{0}}|
, for every
t\in I_{j_{0}+1}
, arguing similarly to (
84
), one can obtain

|\gamma_{j_{0}+1}^{(j_{0}+1)}(t)|\leq 2|r_{j_{0}+1,j_{0}}|+|v_{j_{0}+1}|\leq(2C_{j_{0}}+1)\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}^{j_{0}}.

Setting
C_{j_{0}+1,j_{0}+1}=\max\{2C_{j_{0}}+1,1\}
, we get

|\gamma_{j_{0}+1}^{(i)}(t)|\leq C_{j_{0}+1,j_{0}+1}\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}^{\deg i},\qquad\forall t\in I_{j_{0}+1},\quad\forall i=2,\dots,j_{0}+1.

Let us now proceed by induction on
i
and assume there exists
i_{0}\in\{j_{0}+1,\dots,n\}
and a constant
C_{j_{0}+1,i_{0}}>0
such that

(85)

|\gamma_{j_{0}+1}^{(i)}(t)|\leq C_{j_{0}+1,i_{0}}\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}^{\deg i}=C_{j_{0}+1,i_{0}}\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}^{i-1},\quad\forall t\in I_{j_{0}+1},\quad\forall i=2,\dots,i_{0}.

We want to find
C_{j_{0}+1,i_{0}+1}>0
only depending on
L,C_{j_{0}},C_{j_{0}+1,i_{0}},\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
such that

|\gamma_{j_{0}+1}^{(i_{0}+1)}(t)|\leq C_{j_{0}+1,i_{0}+1}\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}^{\deg(i_{0}+1)}=C_{j_{0}+1,i_{0}+1}\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}^{i_{0}},\quad\forall t\in I_{j_{0}+1}.

By using again (
34
), we get that, for every
t\in I_{j_{0}+1}
, one has

(86)

\displaystyle|\gamma_{j_{0}+1}^{(i_{0}+1)}(t)|

\displaystyle=\left|r_{i_{0}+1,j_{0}}+\int_{0}^{t}P_{i_{0}+1}^{j_{0}+1}(\varphi\circ\gamma_{j_{0}+1}(s),\gamma_{j_{0}+1}^{(2)}(s),\dots,\gamma_{j_{0}+1}^{(i_{0})}(s))\de\!s\right|

\displaystyle\leq|r_{i_{0}+1,j_{0}}|+\int_{0}^{t}\left|P_{i_{0}+1}^{j_{0}+1}(\varphi\circ\gamma_{j_{0}+1}(s),\gamma_{j_{0}+1}^{(2)}(s),\dots,\gamma_{j_{0}+1}^{(i_{0})}(s))\right|\de\!s,

for a polynomial
P^{j_{0}+1}_{i_{0}+1}
of homogeneous degree
\deg(i_{0}+1)-\deg(j_{0}+1)=i_{0}-j_{0}
. From (
84
) and (
85
), we deduce that there exists a constant
M>0
depending only on
j_{0},i_{0},L,\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
(it indeed depends on the coefficients of the polynomial, the constant
\widetilde{C}_{j_{0}+1}
, and the induction constant
C_{j_{0}+1,i_{0}}
) such that

\left|P_{i_{0}+1}^{j_{0}+1}(\varphi\circ\gamma_{j_{0}+1}(t),\gamma_{j_{0}+1}^{(2)}(t),\dots,\gamma_{j_{0}+1}^{(i_{0})}(t))\right|\leq M\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}^{i_{0}-j_{0}},\qquad\forall t\in I_{j_{0}+1},

and thus from (
86
) we get

(87)

|\gamma_{j_{0}+1}^{(i_{0}+1)}(t)|\leq|r_{i_{0}+1,j_{0}}|+M|t|\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}^{i_{0}-j_{0}}\leq|r_{i_{0}+1,j_{0}}|+M|v_{j_{0}+1}-r_{j_{0}+1,j_{0}}|\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}^{i_{0}-j_{0}},

for every
t\in I_{j_{0}+1}
. Notice that
r_{i_{0}+1,j_{0}}
is the endpoint of
\gamma_{j_{0}}^{(i_{0}+1)}
, as well as
r_{j_{0}+1,j_{0}}
is the endpoint of
\gamma_{j_{0}}^{(j_{0}+1)}
. Thus, by (
81
), we get that

|r_{i_{0}+1,j_{0}}|\leq C_{j_{0}}\|v\|^{\deg(i_{0}+1)}_{\mathbb{G}}=C_{j_{0}}\|v\|^{i_{0}}_{\mathbb{G}},

and

|v_{j_{0}+1}-r_{j_{0}+1,j_{0}}|\leq(1+C_{j_{0}})\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}^{\deg(j_{0}+1)}=(1+C_{j_{0}})\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}^{j_{0}}.

Replacing these last two equalities in (
87
), we get

|\gamma_{j_{0}+1}^{(i_{0}+1)}(t)|\leq C_{j_{0}+1,i_{0}+1}\|v\|^{i_{0}}_{\mathbb{G}},\qquad\forall t\in I_{j_{0}+1},

for the constant
C_{j_{0}+1,i_{0}+1}\coloneqq C_{j_{0}}+M(1+C_{j_{0}})
, which only depends on
j_{0},i_{0},L,\mathbb{W}
, and
\mathbb{L}
. Inequalities (
82
) and (
83
) are completed by choosing
C_{j_{0}+1}\coloneqq\max\{\max_{i=j_{0}+1,\dots,n}C_{j_{0}+1,i},\widetilde{C}_{j_{0}+1}\}
. To conclude the proof, it is enough to set
C\coloneqq\max_{j=2,\dots,n}C_{j}
.
∎

Remark 4.2

(An improvement of
Section

4.1
)
.

The careful reader could have noticed that the scheme of the proof
Section

4.1
above can be adapted to prove the very same statement, in the more general case in which
\mathbb{W}
is normal. We will not use the conclusions of this remark in what follows, but we nonetheless give a sketch of the proof of this fact.

Indeed, also in the case in which
\mathbb{W}
is normal, taking
Section

3.1
into account, the vector fields
D^{\varphi}_{j}
, for
k+1\leq j\leq n
, in exponential coordinates, have a triangular form analogous to
Section

3.1
. Then, if we adopt the same notation and setting as the statement of
Section

4.1
and if we assume that the curves
\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}_{j}
are
\frac{1}{\deg j}
-Hölder with respect to the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{G}}
, the same double-induction argument of the proof of
Section

4.1
implies that, for any
k+1\leq j\leq n
, and any
t\in I_{j}
, one has
\|\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}_{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{G}}\leq\!C\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}
, and
\|\widetilde{\gamma}_{j}(t)\|_{\mathbb{G}}\leq\!C\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}
, instead of (
74
), and (
75
),respectively.

Thus, by evaluating the general form of (
74
) at
j=n
and time
t
corresponding to the endpoint of
I_{n}
, we get
\|\widetilde{\varphi}(v)\|_{\mathbb{G}}\leq C\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}}
, with a constant
C
only depending on
L
,
\mathbb{W}
,
\mathbb{L}
and the basis adapted to the splitting. Then, if we assume that the bound
L
on the Hölder constant of
\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}_{j}
is uniform with respect to the choice of the integral curve
\gamma_{j}
of
D^{\varphi}_{j}
, with
k+1\leq j\leq n
, we get, by exploiting the fact that
\mathbb{W}
is locally
D^{\varphi}
-connectible according to
Section

3.1
, that

(88)

\|\widetilde{\varphi}(v)\|_{\mathbb{G}}\leq C\|v\|_{\mathbb{G}},

for every
v\in\widetilde{U}^{\prime}\Subset\widetilde{U}
, where the constant
C
only depends on
L
,
\mathbb{W}
,
\mathbb{L}
and the chosen basis adapted to the splitting.

Finally, if we do not necessarily assume
\widetilde{\varphi}(e)=e
as in the statement of
Section

4.1
, but we still assume that the
1/\deg j
-Hölder constant of
\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}_{j}
is uniformly bounded with respect to the choice of the integral curves
\widetilde{\gamma}_{j}
, the same translation argument as in the beginning of the proof of
Section

3.3
, joined with the conclusion (
88
) and the third point of
Section

2.3
, implies that
\widetilde{\varphi}
is intrinsically Lipschitz on
\widetilde{U}^{\prime}\Subset\widetilde{U}
. This last statement is the local converse of
Section

3.3
.

We finally remark that the improved result we described here is the implication (3)
\Rightarrow
(1) of
[
Koz15
, Theorem 4.2.16]
.

We now exploit
Section

4.1
to show a criterion to prove that a function is uniformly intrinsically differentiable in an arbitrary Carnot group. To do so we introduce the definition of the vertically broad* hölder property in a fixed adapted basis.

Definition 4.3

(Vertically broad* hölder and vertically broad hölder condition)
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be two complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and
k
-dimensional, and fix an adapted basis
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
such that
\mathbb{W}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{k+1},\dots,X_{n}\})
and
\mathbb{L}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\})
. Let us fix
\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}
an open subset and let us denote
D^{\varphi}_{j}\coloneqq D^{\varphi}_{X_{j}}
as defined in (
26
). We say that a continuous function
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\to\mathbb{L}
is
vertically broad* hölder
if for every
a_{0}
in
U
there exist
\delta_{a_{0}}>0
and neighborhoods
U^{\prime}_{a_{0}}\Subset U_{a_{0}}\Subset U
of
a_{0}
such that for every
a\in U^{\prime}_{a_{0}}
and every
j=m+1,\dots,n
one can find a
C^{1}
regular solution
E_{j}^{\varphi}(a)\colon[-\delta_{a_{0}},\delta_{a_{0}}]\to U_{a_{0}}
of the Cauchy problem

\begin{cases}\dot{\gamma}=D^{\varphi}_{j}\circ\gamma&\\
\gamma(0)=a&\end{cases}

such that

(89)

\lim_{\varrho\to 0}\left(\sup\left\{\frac{|\varphi(E_{j}^{\varphi}(a,t))-\varphi(E_{j}^{\varphi}(a,s))|}{|t-s|^{1/{\mathrm{deg}j}}}:j=m+1,\dots,n,a\in U^{\prime}_{a_{0}},0<|t-s|\leq\varrho\right\}\right)=0.

We moreover say that
\widetilde{\varphi}
is
vertically broad hölder
if for every
V\Subset U
one has

\lim_{\varrho\to 0}\left(\sup\left\{\frac{|\varphi(\gamma(t))-\varphi(\gamma(s))|}{|t-s|^{1/{\deg j}}}:j=m+1,\dots,n,\,\dot{\gamma}=D^{\varphi}_{j}\circ\gamma,\gamma\subseteq V,0<|t-s|\leq\varrho\right\}\right)=0.

Remark 4.4
.

Notice that
Section

4.1
is a priori susceptible to the choice of a basis adapted to the splitting. However, when it is coupled with the broad* condition in the same basis, see
Section

3.4
, it is independent on this choice. See
Section

3.4
for details.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
, with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and
k
-dimensional, and fix an adapted basis
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
such that
\mathbb{W}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{k+1},\dots,X_{n}\})
and
\mathbb{L}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\})
. Let
\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}
be open, let
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\to\mathbb{L}
be a vertically broad* hölder map and assume
\omega\colon U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n-k}\to\mathbb{R}^{k\times(m-k)}
is a continuous function such that
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
in the broad* sense on
U
.
Then
\widetilde{\varphi}\in\mathrm{UID}(\widetilde{U},\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
. Moreover
