\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
, where
\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi
is the intrinsic gradient defined in
Section

2.3
.

Proof.

Up to bi-Lipschitz equivalence, we can prove the result choosing the anisotropic norm
\|x\|_{\mathbb{G}}\coloneqq\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}|x_{\ell}|^{1/\deg\ell}
. For the sake of readability, we give the proof in the case
k=1
. The proof for a larger
k
only requires a typographical effort due to the fact that
\varphi
has more than one component.

Fix
a_{0}\in U
. According to the definition of vertically broad* hölder, we find
\delta_{a_{0}}>0
, neighborhoods
U^{\prime}_{a_{0}}\Subset U_{a_{0}}\Subset U
of
a_{0}
and
C^{1}
maps
E^{\varphi}_{j}(a)\colon[-\delta_{a_{0}},\delta_{a_{0}}]\to U_{a_{0}}
satisfying the conditions of
Section

4.1
. Define for every
\varrho>0
sufficiently small the quantity

(90)

f(\varrho)\coloneqq\sup\left\{\frac{|\varphi(E_{j}^{\varphi}(a,t))-\varphi(E_{j}^{\varphi}(a,s))|}{|t-s|^{1/{\mathrm{deg}j}}}:j=m+1,\dots,n,a\in U^{\prime}_{a_{0}},0<|t-s|\leq\varrho\right\},

which by assumption converges to
0
as
\varrho\to 0
. Throughout the proof, we will often write
E_{j}^{\varphi}
instead of
E_{j}^{\varphi}(a)
, where the dependence on the starting point has to be understood for a suitable
a\in U^{\prime}_{a_{0}}
.

We claim that
\varphi
is UID at
a_{0}
and
\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi_{a_{0}}(b)=\omega(a_{0})\cdot b^{1}
, for every
b\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
, where
b^{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{m-1}
denotes the projection of
b
onto the first
(m-1)
components, and where
\omega(a_{0})\cdot b^{1}\coloneqq\sum_{i=2}^{m}\omega_{i}(a_{0})\cdot b^{1}_{i}
denotes the usual scalar product on
\mathbb{R}^{m-1}
.

By (
22
), we just need to prove that

(91)

\lim_{\varrho\to 0}\left(\sup\left\{\frac{|\varphi(b)-\varphi(a)-\omega(a_{0})\cdot(b^{1}-a^{1})|}{\|\widetilde{\varphi}(a)^{-1}a^{-1}b\,\widetilde{\varphi}(a)\|}:a\in B(a_{0},\varrho),\|a^{-1}b\|<\varrho\right\}\right)=0.

Since
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
in the broad* sense, by
Section

3.4
, we can find neighborhoods
U^{\prime\prime}\Subset U^{\prime}\Subset U^{\prime}_{a_{0}}\Subset U
of
a_{0}
and
\delta>0
such that, for every
j=2,\dots,m
, one has
E_{j}^{\varphi}(U^{\prime\prime}\times[-\delta,\delta])\subseteq U^{\prime}
. We can improve this observation using the triangular form of
D^{\varphi}_{j}
, see (
34
), and arguing as in
Section

3.1
. Indeed, the
sets
U^{\prime\prime}
and
U^{\prime}
can be chosen small enough such that, for every
a,b\in U^{\prime\prime}
, there exists a path connecting
a
to
b
, entirely contained in
U^{\prime}
, made first by a concatenation of the maps
E_{2}^{\varphi},\dots,E_{m}^{\varphi}
(defined accordingly to
Section

3.4
) and then by a concatenation
E^{\varphi}_{m+1},\dots,E^{\varphi}_{n}
of integral curves of
D^{\varphi}_{m+1},\dots,D^{\varphi}_{n}
provided by the vertically broad* hölder condition.

Let use improve this conclusion. We know from
Section

3.2
that if
\widetilde{\gamma}
is an integral curve of
D^{\varphi}_{j}
, then
\widetilde{\gamma}_{q}\coloneqq q\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}\cdot(q_{\mathbb{L}})^{-1}
is an integral curve of
D^{\varphi_{q}}_{j}
, see (
42
). Then, by possibly taking a smaller
U^{\prime\prime}
, we can suppose without loss of generality that there exist neighborhoods
V^{\prime\prime}\Subset V^{\prime}
of
0
, such that, for every
a\in U^{\prime\prime}
and every
b^{\prime}\in V^{\prime\prime}
, there exists a path connecting
0
to
b^{\prime}
, entirely contained in
V^{\prime}
made of
q
-translations of exponential maps. Indeed, if
a,b\in U^{\prime\prime}
, it is enough to set
q\coloneqq\widetilde{\varphi}(a)^{-1}a^{-1}
and build the concatenation of
q
-translated of the maps
(E_{2}^{\varphi})_{q},\dots,(E_{m}^{\varphi})_{q}
, that are integral curves of
D^{\varphi_{q}}_{2},\dots,D^{\varphi_{q}}_{m}
, respectively, by
Section

3.2
, and then chain it with the
q
-translated curves
(E^{\varphi}_{m+1})_{q},\dots,(E^{\varphi}_{n})_{q}
, that are integral curves of
D^{\varphi_{q}}_{m+1},\dots,D^{\varphi_{q}}_{n}
, respectively. By construction, this concatenation connects
0
to
b^{\prime}=\widetilde{\varphi}(a)^{-1}a^{-1}b\widetilde{\varphi}(a)
. It suffices then to take a small enough

V^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\bigcup\{\widetilde{\varphi}(a)^{-1}a^{-1}b\,\widetilde{\varphi}(a):a,b\in U^{\prime\prime}\}.

Moreover, by (d) of
Section

2.3
, we get
\varphi_{q}(0)=0
and by (c) of
Section

2.3
, one also has
\varphi_{q}(b^{\prime})=\varphi_{q}(b^{\prime})-\varphi_{q}(0)=\varphi(b)-\varphi(a)
.
Notice also that
\left(\widetilde{\varphi}(a)^{-1}a^{-1}b\,\widetilde{\varphi}(a)\right)^{1}=b^{1}-a^{1}
, so that we have

(92)

\frac{|\varphi(b)-\varphi(a)-\omega(a_{0})\cdot(b^{1}-a^{1})|}{\|\widetilde{\varphi}(a)^{-1}a^{-1}b\,\widetilde{\varphi}(a)\|}=\frac{|\varphi_{q}(b^{\prime})-\varphi_{q}(0)-\omega(a_{0})\cdot(b^{\prime})^{1}|}{\|b^{\prime}\|},\quad\forall a,b\in U^{\prime\prime}.

For any
a,b\in U^{\prime\prime}
, we hence consider the concatenation
(E^{\varphi}_{2})_{q},\dots,(E^{\varphi}_{n})_{q}
of integral curves of
D^{\varphi_{q}}_{2},\dots,D^{\varphi_{q}}_{n}
entirely lying in
V^{\prime}
, constructed as above, that connects
0
to
b^{\prime}
. Similarly to (
76
), we denote the concatenation by the following chain

(93)

\displaystyle\bar{b}^{\prime}_{1}\coloneqq(0,0,\dots,0)

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{(E^{\varphi}_{2})_{q}}\bar{b}^{\prime}_{2}\coloneqq(0,b^{\prime}_{2},r_{3,2},\dots,r_{n,2})\rightarrow_{(E^{\varphi}_{3})_{q}}\dots

\displaystyle\dots

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{(E^{\varphi}_{j})_{q}}\bar{b}^{\prime}_{j}\coloneqq(0,b^{\prime}_{2},\dots,b^{\prime}_{j},r_{j+1,j},\dots,r_{n,j})\rightarrow_{(E^{\varphi}_{j+1})_{q}}\dots

\displaystyle\dots

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{(E^{\varphi}_{n})_{q}}\bar{b}^{\prime}_{n}\coloneqq b^{\prime}=(0,b^{\prime}_{2},\dots,b^{\prime}_{n}),

where each
(E^{\varphi}_{j})_{q}
is defined on
I_{j}\subseteq[-|b_{j}^{\prime}-r_{j,j-1}|,|b_{j}^{\prime}-r_{j,j-1}|]
, with the convention
r_{2,1}\coloneqq 0
. Since
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
in the broad* sense, by
Section

3.2
and in particular (
47
) and (
48
), we get that

(94)

(\varphi_{q}\circ(E_{j}^{\varphi}(a))_{q})^{\prime}(t)=(\overline{\omega}_{j})_{q}((E^{\varphi}_{j}(a))_{q}(t))=\omega_{j}(E^{\varphi}_{j}((a,t))),

for all
j=2,\dots,m
, all
t\in I_{j}
and all
a\in U^{\prime\prime}
; where the first equality follows from (
48
) and the second one by the definition of
(\overline{\omega}_{j})_{q}
, see (
46
) and (
43
). For every
a,b\in U^{\prime\prime}
, we can now perform the following estimates, which we subsequently explain:

(95)

\displaystyle|\varphi_{q}(b^{\prime})-

\displaystyle\varphi_{q}(0)-\omega(a_{0})\cdot(b^{\prime})^{1}|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{j=2}^{m}\left(\varphi_{q}(\bar{b}^{\prime}_{j})-\varphi_{q}(\bar{b}^{\prime}_{j-1})-\omega_{j}(a_{0})b^{\prime}_{j}\right)+\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}\left(\varphi_{q}(\bar{b}^{\prime}_{j})-\varphi_{q}(\bar{b}^{\prime}_{j-1})\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{j=2}^{m}\left(\omega_{j}(b_{j}^{*})-\omega_{j}(a_{0})\right)b^{\prime}_{j}+\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}\left(\varphi_{q}(\bar{b}^{\prime}_{j})-\varphi_{q}(\bar{b}^{\prime}_{j-1})\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{j=2,\dots,m}|

\displaystyle\omega_{j}(b_{j}^{*})-\omega_{j}(a_{0})|\|b^{\prime}\|+f\left(\sup_{j=m+1,\dots,n}|b^{\prime}_{j}-r_{j,j-1}|\right)\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}|b^{\prime}_{j}-r_{j,j-1}|^{1/\deg j}.

In the second equality we used (
94
) and, for every
j=2,\dots,m
, the point
b_{j}^{*}
is on
E_{j}^{\varphi}(I_{j})
and satisfies the conditions of Lagrange’s Theorem. In the third inequality we estimated the first term with the supremum norm and the second term by exploiting the definition of
f
in (
90
), but replacing in (
90
)
\varphi\circ E^{\varphi}_{j}
with
\varphi_{q}\circ(E^{\varphi}_{j})_{q}
. Indeed, by (
46
), the map
\varphi_{q}\circ(E^{\varphi}_{j})_{q}
is a Euclidean translation (in the horizontal coordinates) of
\varphi\circ E^{\varphi}_{j}
.

Notice that, by continuity and with simple estimates coming from
Section

2.1
, by definition of
b^{\prime}
and
b^{*}_{j}
one has

\displaystyle\lim_{\varrho\to 0}

\displaystyle\left(\sup\{\|b^{\prime}\|:a,b\in U^{\prime\prime},\|a^{-1}b\|\leq\varrho\}\right)=0,\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle\lim_{\varrho\to 0}

\displaystyle\left(\sup\{\|a_{0}^{-1}b^{*}_{j}\|:a,b\in U^{\prime\prime},\|a^{-1}b\|\leq\varrho\}\right)=0,\quad\forall j=2,\dots,n,

where we implicitly mean that
b^{*}_{j}
is also a function of the concatenation and the limit is uniform also with respect to that choice.
Moreover, the concatenation in (
93
) uniformly collapses to
0
as
\varrho\to 0
, since by continuity one has

\lim_{\varrho\to 0}\left(\sup\{|b^{\prime}_{j}-r_{j,j-1}|:a,b\in U^{\prime\prime},\|a^{-1}b\|\leq\varrho\}\right)=0,\qquad\forall j=2,\dots,n,

where again, the uniformity has to be understood also in the choice of the concatenation.
We can then find two continuous functions
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\colon(0,+\infty)\to(0,+\infty)
such that
\lim_{\varrho\to 0}\alpha_{1}(\varrho)=\lim_{\varrho\to 0}\alpha_{2}(\varrho)=0
and, combining (
95
) and (
92
), one has

(96)

\displaystyle\frac{|\varphi(b)-\varphi(a)-\omega(a_{0})\cdot(b^{1}-a^{1})|}{\|\widetilde{\varphi}(a)^{-1}a^{-1}b\,\widetilde{\varphi}(a)\|}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{j=2,\dots,m}\|\omega_{j}-\omega_{j}(a_{0})\|_{L^{\infty}(B(a_{0},\alpha_{1}(\varrho)))}

\displaystyle+f(\alpha_{2}(\varrho))\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}\frac{|b^{\prime}_{j}-r_{j,j-1}|^{1/\deg j}}{\|b^{\prime}\|},

for every sufficiently small
\varrho>0
and every
a\in B(a_{0},\varrho)
and
b\in U^{\prime\prime}
such that
\|a^{-1}b\|<\varrho
.
We claim that we are in a position to apply
Section

4.1
to the function
\varphi_{q}
and to the curves
E^{\varphi_{q}}_{j}
that connect 0 to
b^{\prime}
. Indeed, for
j=2,\dots,m
, the uniform bound on the Lipschitz constant of the map
\varphi_{q}\circ(E^{\varphi}_{j})_{q}
follows from (
94
) and the fact that
\omega
is continuous, while for
j\geq m+1
we use the fact that, since
f(\varrho)\to 0
as
\varrho\to 0
, the maps
\varphi_{q}\circ(E^{\varphi}_{j})_{q}
are in
h^{1/\deg j}(I_{j})
with a uniform bound on the Hölder constant. In particular, by (
75
), we get that for every
\varrho
sufficiently small, there exists
C^{\prime}>0
depending on the uniform bound of
\omega
on
U^{\prime}
such that for every
j=2,\dots,n

|r_{j,j-1}|^{1/{\rm deg}j}\leq C^{\prime}\|b^{\prime}\|,

and thus from (
96
) we get

\frac{|\varphi(b)-\varphi(a)-\omega(a_{0})\cdot(b^{1}-a^{1})|}{\|\widetilde{\varphi}(a)^{-1}a^{-1}b\,\widetilde{\varphi}(a)\|}\leq\sup_{j=2,\dots,m}\|\omega_{j}-\omega_{j}(a_{0})\|_{L^{\infty}(B(a_{0},\alpha_{1}(\varrho)))}+f(\alpha_{2}(\varrho))(n-m)(1+C^{\prime}).

By the continuity of each
\omega_{j}
, the proof follows by letting
\varrho\to 0
.
∎

For the forthcoming corollaries we recall for the reader’s benefit that we are going to use the notation in
Section

2.1
.

Corollary 4.6
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be two complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
, with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and
k
-dimensional and let
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
be an adapted basis of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
such that
\mathbb{W}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{k+1},\dots,X_{n}\})
and
\mathbb{L}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\})
. Let
\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}
be open and let
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\to\mathbb{L}
and
\omega\colon U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n-k}\to\mathbb{R}^{k\times(m-k)}
be two continuous functions. Assume that
\widetilde{\varphi}
is vertically broad* hölder and assume
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
holds in the broad* sense on
U
. Then the graph of
\widetilde{\varphi}
is a co-horizontal
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surface with tangents complemented by
\mathbb{L}
.

Proof.

It is enough to combine
Section

4.1
and
Section

2.3
.
∎

Corollary 4.7
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be two complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
, with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and
k
-dimensional and let
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
be an adapted basis of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
such that
\mathbb{W}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{k+1},\dots,X_{n}\})
and
\mathbb{L}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\})
. Let
\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}
be open and let
\widetilde{\varphi}\in\mathrm{ID}(\widetilde{U},\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
be a vertically broad* hölder map and assume
\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi
is continuous on
\widetilde{U}
. Then
\widetilde{\varphi}\in\mathrm{UID}(\widetilde{U},\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
.

Proof.

It is enough to combine
Section

3.4
and
Section

4.1
.
∎

Remark 4.8
.

We do not know whether, in arbitrary Carnot groups, one can drop the condition of vertically broad* hölder regularity in
Section

4.1
. This is the case for step-2 Carnot groups, with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and one-dimensional, see
Section

6.2
.

Corollary 4.9
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be two complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
, with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and
k
-dimensional and let
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
be an adapted basis of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
such that
\mathbb{W}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{k+1},\dots,X_{n}\})
and
\mathbb{L}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\})
. Let
\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}
be open and let
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\to\mathbb{L}
and
\omega\colon U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n-k}\to\mathbb{R}^{k\times(m-k)}
be two continuous functions. Assume
\widetilde{\varphi}
is vertically broad* hölder and assume
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
holds in the broad* sense on
U
. Then
\widetilde{\varphi}
is vertically broad hölder and
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
holds in the broad sense on
U
.

Proof.

It is enough to combine
Section

4.1
,
Section

3.3
and
Section

3.4
.
∎

4.2.
Area formula for codimension-one graphs in terms of intrinsic derivatives

We prove here that, if
\mathbb{L}
is horizontal and one-dimensional, and
\widetilde{\varphi}\in{\rm UID}(\widetilde{U},\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
, with
\widetilde{U}
open, then
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi
holds
in the sense of distributions
. For a precise definition of the distribution
D^{\varphi}\varphi
the interested reader may soon read the first lines of the proof of
Section

4.2
. We also provide an area formula for
{\rm graph}(\widetilde{\varphi})
in this case. For the case
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{H}^{n}
, this formula was already obtained in
[
ASCV06
, Proposition 2.22 & Remark 2.23]
. Recall that we are going to use the notation given in
Section

2.1
.

Proposition 4.10
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be two complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and one-dimensional, and let
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
be an adapted basis of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
such that
\mathbb{L}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{1}\})
and
\mathbb{W}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{2},\dots,X_{n}\})
. Let
\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}
be open and consider
\widetilde{\varphi}\in\mathrm{UID}(\widetilde{U},\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
. Then the following facts hold.

(a)

For every
j=2,\dots,m
, the distribution
D_{j}^{\varphi}\varphi\coloneqq D_{X_{j}}^{\varphi}\varphi
is well-defined on
U
.

(b)

For every
a\in U
, there exist
\delta>0
and a family of functions
\{\varphi_{\varepsilon}\in C^{1}(B(a,\delta)):\varepsilon\in(0,1)\}
such that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varphi_{\varepsilon}=\varphi\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}D_{j}^{\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\varphi_{\varepsilon}=\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi(X_{j})\quad\text{in $L^{\infty}(B(a,\delta))$},

for every
j=2,\dots,m
.

(c)

The equation

(97)

D_{j}^{\varphi}\varphi=\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi(X_{j})\eqqcolon\nabla^{\varphi}_{j}\varphi,

holds in the distributional sense on
U
, for every
j=2,\dots,m
. Here
\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi
is the intrinsic gradient of
\varphi
, see
Section

2.3
.

(d)

The subgraph of
\varphi
defined by
E_{\varphi}\coloneqq\{w\cdot\exp(tX_{1}):w\in U,t<\varphi(w)\}
has locally finite
\mathbb{G}
-perimeter
2
2

2

Here we take the usual definition of the horizontal perimeter with respect to the orthonormal basis
(X_{1},\dots,X_{m})
, see
[
FSSC03a
, Definition 2.18]
.
in
U\cdot\exp(\mathbb{R}X_{1})
and its
\mathbb{G}
-perimeter measure
|\partial E_{\varphi}|_{\mathbb{G}}
is given by

(98)

|\partial E_{\varphi}|_{\mathbb{G}}(V)=\int_{\Phi^{-1}(V)}\sqrt{1+|\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi|_{m-1}^{2}}\,\de\mathscr{L}^{n-1},

for every Borel set
V\subseteq U\cdot\exp{(\mathbb{R}X_{1})}
, where
\Phi\colon U\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the graph function of
\widetilde{\varphi}
composed with the exponential coordinates, and with a little abuse of notation we wrote
U\cdot\exp(\mathbb{R}X_{1})
meaning the set
\widetilde{U}\cdot\exp(\mathbb{R}X_{1})
embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
through exponential coordinates.

Moreover, the set
\mbox{graph}(\widetilde{\varphi})
has a unit normal given, up to a sign, by

(99)

\nu_{E_{\varphi}}=\left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi|_{m-1}^{2}}},\frac{\nabla_{2}^{\varphi}\varphi}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi|_{m-1}^{2}}},\dots,\frac{\nabla_{m}^{\varphi}\varphi}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi|_{m-1}^{2}}}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.

Proof.

Notice that the fact that
D^{\varphi}_{j}\varphi
is a well-defined distribution on
U
is a consequence of
Section

3.1
and the fact that
\mathbb{L}
is one-dimensional. Indeed, in coordinates, we get that the vector field
D^{\varphi}_{j}
is the sum of terms
g(x)\varphi^{h}\partial_{x_{i}}
, for some polynomial function
g
of the coordinates of
\mathbb{W}
, some integer
h\geq 0
, and some
i=2,\dots,n
. Thus in order to define the distribution
D^{\varphi}_{j}\varphi
, we only need to define
g(x)\varphi^{h}\partial_{x_{i}}\varphi\coloneqq g(x)\frac{1}{h+1}\partial_{x_{i}}\varphi^{h+1}
. Since
g(x)\frac{1}{h+1}\partial_{x_{i}}\varphi^{h+1}
is well-defined in the sense of distributions, because
\varphi
is continuous, (a) is proved.

We now show that equality (
97
) holds pointwise if
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
is of class
C^{1}
in
\widetilde{U}
. In case
\widetilde{\varphi}\in C^{1}(\widetilde{U})
, then, by
[
DD18
, Theorem 4.9]
, one has
\widetilde{\varphi}\in\mathrm{UID}(\widetilde{U},\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
.

We can assume without loss of generality that
e\in\widetilde{U}
and
\widetilde{\varphi}(e)=e
. Indeed, if we want to prove identity (
97
) in
a\in U
, we may consider
\widetilde{\varphi}_{p}
with
p\coloneqq\widetilde{\varphi}(a)^{-1}\cdot a^{-1}
, and use the invariance properties given by
Section

3.2
and
Section

2.3
, and then notice that
\widetilde{\varphi}_{p}(e)=e
.
By
Section

2.3
, since
\widetilde{\varphi}
is
{\rm UID}
, the set
{\rm graph}(\widetilde{\varphi})
is a
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-hypersurface and therefore there exist a neighborhood
\widetilde{V}
of
e
in
\mathbb{G}
and a function
f\in C^{1}_{\rm H}(\widetilde{V})
such that

(100)

\mathrm{graph}(\widetilde{\varphi})\cap\widetilde{V}=\{p\in\mathbb{G}:f(p)=0\}\cap\widetilde{V}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\nabla_{\mathbb{G}}f\neq 0\text{ on $\widetilde{V}$}.

Then, for every sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
and for every
j=2,\dots,n
, one has

f\Big(\exp(\varepsilon X_{j})\widetilde{\varphi}(\exp(\varepsilon X_{j}))\Big)=0.

Therefore, with a little abuse of notation, one can differentiate with respect to
\varepsilon
to get

(101)

\begin{split}0&=\frac{\de}{\de\varepsilon}_{|_{\varepsilon=0}}f\Big(\exp(\varepsilon X_{j})\widetilde{\varphi}(\exp(\varepsilon X_{j}))\Big)\\
&=\frac{\de}{\de\varepsilon}_{|_{\varepsilon=0}}f\Big(\widetilde{\varphi}(\exp(\varepsilon X_{j}))\Big)+\frac{\de}{\de\varepsilon}_{|_{\varepsilon=0}}f\Big(\exp(\varepsilon X_{j})\Big)\\
&=X_{1}f_{|_{e}}\left(\frac{\de}{\de\varepsilon}_{|_{\varepsilon=0}}\varphi(\varepsilon X_{j})\right)+X_{j}f_{|_{e}}\\
&=X_{1}f_{|_{e}}\left(\frac{\de}{\de\varepsilon}_{|_{\varepsilon=0}}(\varphi\circ\pi_{\mathbb{W}})(\varepsilon X_{j})\right)+X_{j}f|_{e}\\
&=(X_{1}f)_{|_{e}}D_{j}^{\varphi}\varphi|_{e}+(X_{j}f)_{|_{e}},\end{split}

where we used the fact that
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{W}\cdot\mathbb{L}
and exploited the fact that
\widetilde{\varphi}
takes values in
\mathbb{L}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{1}\})
. The last equality follows by using the definition of
D^{\varphi}_{j}
acting on
\varphi
, see (
26
). The claim is then obtained by (
101
) and (
25
).

To prove (b), we use some ideas of
[
FSSC03b
, Theorem 2.1]
to show that, for any
a\in U
, there exist
\delta>0
and a family of functions
\{\varphi_{\varepsilon}\in C^{1}(U):0<\varepsilon<1\}
, such that

\varphi_{\varepsilon}\to\varphi,\qquad{\rm and}\quad D_{j}^{\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\varphi_{\varepsilon}\to\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi(X_{j}),\quad\forall j=2,\dots,m,

uniformly in the Euclidean ball
B_{\rm e}(a,\delta)\Subset U
, as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

By
Section

2.3
, we can find a neighborhood
\widetilde{V}
of
a\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}(a)
and
f\in C^{1}_{H}(\widetilde{V})
satisfying (
100
). Let
\delta>0
be such that, setting
B\coloneqq B_{\rm e}(a,\delta)
, one has
\widetilde{B}\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{B})\subseteq\widetilde{V}
. Then, up to reducing
\delta
and
\widetilde{V}
and a regularization argument analogous to
[
FSSC03b
, Step 1 of Theorem 2.1]
, we can construct a family
\{f_{\varepsilon}\in C^{1}(\widetilde{V}):0<\varepsilon<1\}
such that

(102)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left(\sup_{\widetilde{V}}|X_{j}f_{\varepsilon}-X_{j}f|\right)=0,\qquad\forall j=1,\dots,m.

Since
\widetilde{\varphi}
takes values in
\exp({\rm span}\{X_{1}\})
, by
Section

2.3
, we can assume without loss of generality that
X_{1}f>0
on
\widetilde{V}
, since
X_{1}f\neq 0
and we are free to possibly exchange
f
with
-f
. By (
102
), we can find
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that
X_{1}f_{\varepsilon}>0
on
\widetilde{V}
, for every
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
. For any such
\varepsilon>0
, by the Euclidean implicit function theorem, we can find
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\varepsilon}
, defined on
\widetilde{B}
, such that

\mathrm{graph}(\widetilde{\varphi}_{\varepsilon})\cap\widetilde{B}\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{B})=\{p\in\mathbb{G}:f_{\varepsilon}(p)=0\}\cap\widetilde{B}\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{B}).

Moreover, since
f_{\varepsilon}
is smooth, then also
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\varepsilon}
is smooth. In particular, for every
b\in\widetilde{B}
, one has

f_{\varepsilon}(b\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}_{\varepsilon}(b))=0,\qquad\forall\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}).

From
[
FSSC03b
, Step 3 of Theorem 1.2]
we deduce that

(103)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left(\sup_{B}|\varphi_{\varepsilon}-\varphi|\right)=0.

Denote by
\Phi_{\varepsilon}\colon B\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
the graph function of
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\varepsilon}
composed with the exponential coordinates. Since
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\varepsilon}
is of class
C^{1}
, by using the pointwise version of (
97
) for
C^{1}
functions and (
25
), we deduce that

(104)

D^{\varphi_{\varepsilon}}_{j}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)=-\frac{X_{j}f_{\varepsilon}}{X_{1}f_{\varepsilon}}\circ\Phi_{\varepsilon}(x),\qquad\forall j=2,\dots,m,\quad\forall x\in B.

Then, by (
104
), (
102
), (
25
) and (
103
), we conclude that, for any
j=2,\dots,m
, the family
D^{\varphi_{\varepsilon}}_{j}\varphi_{\varepsilon}
converges uniformly on
B
to
-\frac{X_{j}f}{X_{1}f}\circ\Phi=\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi(X_{j})
, as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

The proof of (c) follows directly from (b) and the particular form of the distribution
D^{\varphi}_{j}\varphi
we discussed at the beginning of this proof. In fact from the convergence proved in (b), we know that, for any
a\in U
, there exists
\delta>0
such that
D_{j}^{\varphi}\varphi=\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi(X_{j})
on
B(a,\delta)
in the sense of distributions, for every
j=2,\dots,m
. It is then enough to consider a locally finite countable open sub-covering
\{B(a_{h},\delta_{h}):h\in\mathbb{N}\}
of
U
and build a partition of unity subordinate to it. The fact that
D^{\varphi}_{j}\varphi=\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi(X_{j})
holds in the sense of distributions on
U
is a consequence of the local identity, the linearity of distributions, and a standard argument using the partition of unity.

To prove (d), we first notice that, by
[
FSSC03b
, Theorem 2.1]
and
Section

2.3
, we know that, for every
p\in\mathrm{graph}(\widetilde{\varphi})
there exists a neighborhood
\widetilde{V}^{\prime}
of
p
and a function
f\in C_{H}^{1}(\widetilde{V}^{\prime})
, with
X_{1}f>0
on
\widetilde{V}^{\prime}
, representing
\mathrm{graph}(\widetilde{\varphi})
as non critical level set and such that

|\partial E_{\varphi}|_{\mathbb{G}}(V)=\int_{\Phi^{-1}(V)}\frac{|\nabla_{\mathbb{G}}f|}{X_{1}f}\circ\Phi\,\de\mathscr{L}^{n-1}

holds for every Borel set
V\subseteq V^{\prime}
. Now by (
25
), we can write

(105)

|\partial E|_{\mathbb{G}}(V)=\int_{\Phi^{-1}(V)}\sqrt{1+|\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi|^{2}_{m-1}}\de\mathscr{L}^{n-1},\quad\text{for every Borel set }\quad V\subseteq V^{\prime}.

Since the right hand side of (
105
) does not depend on the choice of
f
, by a covering argument we can extend it to every Borel subset
V
of
U\cdot\exp(\mathbb{R}X_{1})
.

The explicit expression of the unit normal in (
99
) comes from the fact that the graph of
\widetilde{\varphi}
is locally the zero-level set of
f
. Thus, the unit normal of
{\rm graph}(\widetilde{\varphi})
is in the direction of
\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}f
, and taking (
25
) into account, one has
\nabla_{j}^{\varphi}\varphi=-\frac{X_{j}f}{X_{1}f}\circ\Phi
for every
j=2,\dots,m
, and then we conclude by normalization.
∎

Remark 4.11
.

For what concerns the area formula in arbitrary Carnot groups, in
[
Mag17
, Theorem 1.2]
, the author proves that

(106)

P_{\mathbb{G}}(E)=\beta(d,\nu_{E})\mathscr{S}^{Q-1}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits\mathcal{F}E,

for any set
E
of finite perimeter with
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-rectifiable reduced boundary
\mathcal{F}E
. The density
\beta
is explicitly computed in
[
Mag17
, Theorem 3.2]
and depends on the metric
d
and on the normal
\nu_{E}
of
E
that is defined in the sense of Geometric Measure Theory. Moreover, in case the distance
d
is vertically symmetric,
\beta
is a constant that only depends on the group
\mathbb{G}
and on the metric
d
, see
[
Mag17
, Theorem 6.3]
. We finally notice that every Carnot group admits a metric
d
that is vertically symmetric, see
[
FSSC03a
, Theorem 5.1]
.
For a survey on the area formula in Carnot groups, we refer the reader to
[
SC16
, Section 4]
.

Notice that if
\widetilde{\varphi}
is in
{\rm UID}(\widetilde{U},\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
, the set
{\rm graph}(\widetilde{\varphi})
is a
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-hypersurface by
Section

2.3
. Then, by definition of
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-rectifiability and by
[
FSSC03b
, Theorem 2.1]
, the subgraph
E_{\varphi}
of
\widetilde{\varphi}
has a
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-rectifiable reduced boundary
\mathcal{F}E_{\varphi}={\rm graph}(\widetilde{\varphi})
. Thus we are in a position to apply
Section

4.2
and
[
Mag17
, Theorem 1.2]
, and in particular to compare (
98
) with (
106
) in order to obtain the explicit representation

(107)

\int_{\widetilde{V}\cap{\rm graph}(\widetilde{\varphi})}\beta(d,\nu_{E_{\varphi}})\de\mathscr{S}^{Q-1}=\int_{\Phi^{-1}(V)}\sqrt{1+|\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi|_{m-1}^{2}}\de\mathscr{L}^{n-1},

for every Borel set
\widetilde{V}\subseteq\widetilde{U}\cdot\exp(\mathbb{R}X_{1})
.

A general area formula for a
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-surface
\Sigma
, valid in an arbitrary Carnot groups, has been very recently obtained in
[
JNGV20
, Theorem 1.1]
. If we call
\alpha
the Hausdorff dimension of
\Sigma
, and we suppose
\Sigma={\rm graph}(\widetilde{\varphi})
, this formula allows for a representation of
\mathscr{S}^{\alpha}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits\Sigma
as an integral on
\widetilde{U}
of a properly defined area element with respect to
\mathscr{S}^{\alpha}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits\mathbb{W}
, see
[
JNGV20
, Lemma 3.2]
. According to the previous equation (
107
), we thus get that for an arbitrary Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
, and in case
\mathbb{L}
is one-dimensional, the area element of
[
JNGV20
, Theorem 1.1]
is, up to the function
\beta
, explicitly written in terms of the intrinsic gradient of
\widetilde{\varphi}
.

Eventually, by the recent work
[
CM20
]
, in particular
[
CM20
, Eq. (43) after Theorem 4.2]
, we get that on
\mathbb{H}^{n}
equipped with a vertically symmetric distance, in case
\mathbb{L}
is horizontal and
k
-dimensional, one can explicitly write the area element of
[
JNGV20
, Theorem 1.1]
in terms of the intrinsic gradient
\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi
.

Corollary 4.12
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be two complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
, with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and one-dimensional, and let
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
be an adapted basis of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
such that
\mathbb{W}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{2},\dots,X_{n}\})
and
\mathbb{L}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{1}\})
. Let
\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}
be open, and let
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\to\mathbb{L}
and
\omega\colon U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\to\mathbb{R}^{m-1}
be two continuous functions. Assume
\widetilde{\varphi}
is vertically broad* hölder and assume that
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
in the broad* sense on
U
. Then
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
in the sense of distributions and
\omega=\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi
.

Proof.

It is enough to combine
Section

4.1
and item (c) of
Section

4.2
.∎

4.3.
Relations between intrinsic differentiability and local approximability

Remark 4.13
.

Point (b) of
Section

4.2
can actually be generalized to the case
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and
k
-dimensional. The computation for the
k
-dimensional case is similar but one should pay attention to the fact that if
f\eqqcolon(f^{(1)},\dots,f^{(k)})\in C^{1}_{\rm H}(\widetilde{V};\mathbb{R}^{k})
is a vector valued map, its horizontal gradient is a
(k\times m)
-dimensional matrix (see
Section

2.3
). Using a regularization argument that is similar to
[
FSSC03b
, Step 1 of Theorem 2.1]
as in the proof of
Section

4.2
, we can find a family of functions
\{f_{\varepsilon}\in C^{1}(\widetilde{V};\mathbb{R}^{k}):0<\varepsilon<1\}
, such that each component
X_{j}f^{(i)}_{\varepsilon}
converges uniformly to
X_{j}f^{(i)}
for every
i=1,\dots,k
and
j=1,\dots,m
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, and such that, for every
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, the associated matrix
\nabla_{\mathbb{L}}f_{\varepsilon}
defined in
Section

2.3
has det
\nabla_{\mathbb{L}}f_{\varepsilon}\neq 0
on
\widetilde{V}
.

Then in order to prove point (b) of
Section

4.2
in this general case, we take the previous changes into account, the straightforward changes in (
101
), and we run the same computations of the proof of
Section

4.2
by using (
24
) instead of (
25
) at the end of the argument.

Remark 4.14
.

The statement (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) of
[
ASCV06
, Theorem 5.1]

claims that, in the Heisenberg groups
\mathbb{H}^{n}
, if
\mathbb{L}
is one-dimensional and
\varphi\in{\rm UID}(U,\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
, then there exists
\omega\in C(U;\mathbb{R}^{2n-1})
such that
\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
in the sense of distributions and there exists a family
\{\varphi_{\varepsilon}\in C^{1}(U):\varepsilon\in(0,1)\}
such that
\varphi_{\varepsilon}\to\varphi
and
\nabla^{\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\varphi_{\varepsilon}\to\omega
uniformly on any compact subset
K\subseteq U
.

However, the implication (5.17)
\Rightarrow
(5.19) in its proof is imprecise. In sight of this, one could replace point (ii) with a local version of it in which the approximating functions
\{\varphi_{\varepsilon}\}
depend on the point
a
and the convergence is uniform in a neighborhood
B(a,\delta)
, see
[
ASCV06
, Proposition 4.6]
. This does not affect the validity of the proofs, that only refer to
[
ASCV06
, Lemma 5.6]
, which holds true with the weakened approximation assumptions, since it has a local statement. Indeed, the proof of (ii)
\Rightarrow
(i) of
[
ASCV06
, Theorem 5.1]
just needs the local approximation. To the best of our knowledge, all the references to
[
ASCV06
, Theorem 5.1]
just require the local approximation: see point (3)
[
DD18
, Theorem 5.8]
, point (3) of
[
DD19
, Theorem 8.2]
,
[
Cor19
, Theorem 1.3]
,
[
Cor19
, Proposition 4.4]
,
[
BSC10b
, Theorem 2.7]
,
[
BSC10a
, Theorem 1.1 & Corollary 1.4]
,
[
BCSC14
, Theorem 2.7]
.

Anyway, the original formulation of (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) of
[
ASCV06
, Theorem 5.1]
can be fixed with the approximation argument exploited in the proof of
[
MV12
, Theorem 1.2]
and we expect this to hold true in all Carnot groups of step 2. We warmly thank Francesco Serra Cassano and Davide Vittone for precious suggestions.

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be two complementary subgroups in a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
, with
\mathbb{L}

k
-dimensional and horizontal. Let
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})

be an adapted basis of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}

such that
\mathbb{L}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\})
and

\mathbb{W}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{k+1},\dots,X_{n}\})
. Let
\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}
be an open set and let
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\to\mathbb{L}
be a continuous function.

Assume there exists
\omega\in C(U;\mathbb{R}^{k\times(m-k)})
such that, for every
a\in U
, there exist
r>0
, and a family of functions
\{\varphi_{\varepsilon}\in C^{1}(B(a,r);\mathbb{R}^{k}):0<\varepsilon<1\}
satisfying

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varphi_{\varepsilon}=\varphi\quad\text{in $L^{\infty}(B(a,r))$}\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}D^{{\varphi_{\varepsilon}}}{\varphi_{\varepsilon}}=\omega\quad\text{in $L^{\infty}(B(a,r);\mathbb{R}^{k\times(m-k)})$}.

Then
\varphi
is a broad* solution in
U
of the system
D^{{\varphi}}{\varphi}=\omega
.

Proof.

The proof closely follows the argument used in
[
ASCV06
, Lemma 5.6]
. For simplicity, we consider the case
k=1
. The case
k\geq 2
can be reached with the same proof and some typographical effort.

Let
a\in U
and fix
j\in\{2,\dots,m\}
and
\varepsilon>0
.
Since
\varphi_{\varepsilon}\in C^{1}(B(a,r))
, we can find
0<\delta_{2}(\varepsilon)<\delta_{1}<r
and a map
E^{\varphi_{\varepsilon}}_{j}\colon\overline{B(a,\delta_{2}(\varepsilon))}\times[-\delta_{2}(\varepsilon),\delta_{2}(\varepsilon)]\to\overline{B(a,\delta_{1})}
such that
E^{\varphi_{\varepsilon}}_{j}(b,\cdot)
is the
unique
solution of the Cauchy problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\gamma^{\prime}=D^{\varphi_{\varepsilon}}_{j}\circ\gamma,\\
\gamma(0)=b,\end{array}\right.

in the interval
[-\delta_{2}(\varepsilon),\delta_{2}(\varepsilon)]
, for every
b\in\overline{B(a,\delta_{2}(\varepsilon))}
.
By Peano’s estimate on the existence time for solutions of ordinary differential equations (see e.g.
[
Mus05
, Theorem 1]
) we can choose
\delta_{2}(\varepsilon)=C/\|P_{j}(x,\varphi_{\varepsilon})\|_{L^{\infty}(\overline{B(a,\delta_{1})})}
, with
C
only depending on
\delta_{1}
and with
P_{j}
a polynomial function of the coordinates
x
of
\mathbb{W}
and the components of
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
. This polynomial function depends only on the structure of
D^{\varphi}_{j}
, see (
34
). In particular, since
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
is converging uniformly on
B(a,\delta_{1})
, then
\delta_{2}(\varepsilon)
has a positive lower bound
M
independent on
\varepsilon
and we are going to verify the conditions of
Section

3.4
with
\delta_{2}\leq M
.

Since
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
are bounded on
B(a,\delta_{1})
uniformly in
\varepsilon>0
and
D^{\varphi_{\varepsilon}}_{j}
are vector fields with polynomial coefficients in
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
and, possibly, in some of the coordinates, the functions
E^{\varphi_{\varepsilon}}_{j}
are equi-Lipschitz with respect to
\varepsilon
on the compact set
\overline{B(a,\delta_{2})}\times[-\delta_{2},\delta_{2}]
. By Arzelá-Ascoli Theorem, we can therefore find an infinitesimal sequence
(\varepsilon_{h})
in
(0,1)
such that
E^{\varphi_{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{j}
converges to some continuous function
E_{j}^{\varphi}
uniformly on
\overline{B(a,\delta_{2})}\times[-\delta_{2},\delta_{2}]
. By definition of
E^{\varphi_{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{j}
, one has

E^{\varphi_{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{j}(b,t)=b+\int_{0}^{t}D^{\varphi_{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{j}\left(E^{\varphi_{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{j}(b,s)\right)\de\!s\qquad\text{and}

\varphi_{\varepsilon_{h}}\left(E_{j}^{\varphi_{\varepsilon_{h}}}(b,t)\right)-\varphi_{\varepsilon_{h}}\left(E_{j}^{\varphi_{\varepsilon_{h}}}(b,0)\right)=\int_{0}^{t}D^{\varphi_{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{j}\varphi_{\varepsilon_{h}}\left(E_{j}^{\varphi_{\varepsilon_{h}}}(b,s)\right)\de\!s,

for every
b\in\overline{B(a,\delta_{2})}
and every
t\in[-\delta_{2},\delta_{2}]
.

By letting
h\to\infty
and using that all the involved convergences are uniform, we get

E_{j}^{\varphi}(b,t)=b+\int_{0}^{t}D^{\varphi}_{j}\left({E_{j}^{\varphi}(b,s)}\right)\de\!s,\qquad\text{and}

\quad\varphi(E_{j}^{\varphi}(b,t))-\varphi(E_{j}^{\varphi}(b,0))=\int_{0}^{t}\omega_{j}(E_{j}^{\varphi}(b,s))\de\!s,

for every
b\in\overline{B(a,\delta_{2})}
and every
t\in[-\delta_{2},\delta_{2}]
, which are the conditions we were looking for to make
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
hold in the broad* sense.
∎

Corollary 4.16
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be two complementary subgroups in a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
, with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and k-dimensional. Let
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})

be an adapted basis of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}

such that
\mathbb{L}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\})
and

\mathbb{W}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{k+1},\dots,X_{n}\})
. Let
\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}
be open and let
\widetilde{\varphi}\in\mathrm{UID}(\widetilde{U},\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
.
Then, there exists
\omega\in C(U;\mathbb{R}^{k\times(m-k)})
such that
\varphi
is a broad* solution in
U
of the system
D^{{\varphi}}{\varphi}=\omega
. Moreover,
\omega=\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi
.

Proof.

It is enough to choose
\omega=\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi
, which is continuous taking
Section

2.3
into account, since
\widetilde{\varphi}\in\mathrm{UID}(\widetilde{U},\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
. The proof follows by combining item (b) of
Section

4.2
, which also holds in case
\mathbb{L}
is
k
-dimensional, see
Section

4.3
, together with
Section

4.3
.
∎

4.4.
Main theorem

Now we are in a position to give the following theorem, which is a generalization of
[
DD18
, Theorem 5.8]
to all Carnot groups.

Theorem 4.17
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be two complementary subgroups in a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
, with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and k-dimensional. Let
\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}
be open and let
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
be a continuous function. Fix a graded basis
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
such that
\mathbb{L}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\})
and
\mathbb{W}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{k+1},\dots,X_{n}\})
. Then, the following facts are equivalent.

(a)

\widetilde{\varphi}\in{\rm UID}(\widetilde{U},\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
;

(b)

\varphi
is vertically broad* hölder and there exists
\omega\in C(U;\mathbb{R}^{k\times(m-k)})
such that, for every
a\in U
, there exist
\delta>0
and a family of functions
\{\varphi_{\varepsilon}\in C^{1}(B(a,\delta);\mathbb{R}^{k}):\varepsilon\in(0,1)\}
such that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varphi_{\varepsilon}=\varphi\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}D_{j}^{\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\varphi_{\varepsilon}=\omega_{j}\quad\text{in $L^{\infty}(B(a,\delta);\mathbb{R}^{k})$},

for every
j=k+1,\dots,m
;

(c)

\varphi
is vertically broad hölder and there exists
\omega\in C(U;\mathbb{R}^{k\times(m-k)})
such that
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
in the broad sense.

(d)

\varphi
is vertically broad* hölder and there exists
\omega\in C(U;\mathbb{R}^{k\times(m-k)})
such that
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
in the broad* sense.

Moreover, if any of the previous holds, then
\omega=\nabla^{\varphi}\varphi
.

Proof.

(a)
\Rightarrow
(b) follows by combining
Section

3.3
, item (b) of
Section

4.2
in the
k
-dimensional case following the lines of
Section

4.3
. (b)
\Rightarrow
(d) follows from
Section

4.3
.
(d)
\Rightarrow
(a) follows from
Section

4.1
. (a)
\Rightarrow
(c) follows from
Section

3.3
,
Section

3.4
and the continuity of
\omega
follows from
Section

2.3
. The implication (c)
\Rightarrow
(d) is trivial.
∎

Remark 4.18
.

Notice that in
[
Koz15
, Example 4.5.1]
, in the setting of
Section

3.1
, the author constructs a function
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
such that
D^{\varphi}\varphi=-1
in the broad* sense but
\widetilde{\varphi}
is not vertically broad* hölder, see
Section

4.1
. Taking
Theorem

4.17
into account, this means that
\widetilde{\varphi}
cannot be UID. This also means that, in general, in
Theorem

4.17
, one cannot drop the assumption on the vertically broad* hölder regularity in arbitrary Carnot groups. We will show that this is possible for step 2 Carnot groups, in
Section

6
. For more examples related to this topic, we refer the reader to
[
Koz15
, Section 4.5]
.

5.
Some applications

Let us begin with an observation that will motivate the first part of this section. Consider the first Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{1}
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}\coloneqq{\rm span}\{X,Y\}\oplus{\rm span}\{Z\}
, and the only nontrivial relation
[X,Y]=Z
. By a direct application of the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula one gets
\exp X\exp Y\exp(-X)\exp(-Y)=\exp Z
.
By exploiting this formula, in
Section

5.1
below, we give an alternative proof, in the case of
\mathbb{H}^{n}
with
n\geq 2
, of
[
BSC10b
, Theorem 3.2]
. The argument we use is different because we prove that being a broad* solution with a continuous datum implies being
1/2
-little Hölder continuous along vertical coordinates (see also
Section

3.3
) that is actually simpler than proving 1/2-little Hölder continuity in all the coordinates as in
[
BSC10b
, Theorem 3.2]
. Nevertheless, this is sufficient for applying
Section

4.1
. Similarly, in
Section

5.1
, we obtain the same result of
[
BSC10b
, Theorem 1.2]
, by making use of
Section

5.1
and
Section

4.1
. We remark that our argument is different by the one used in
[
BSC10b
]
but, on the contrary, it does not work for
n=1
.

Recently, in
[
Cor19
, Proposition 4.10]
, the author has proved a generalization of
[
BSC10b
, Theorem 3.2]
that holds for arbitrary complemented subgroups in
\mathbb{H}^{n}
, and this statement is one of the key step in order to get the main theorem
[
Cor19
, Theorem 1.4]
. By using our reasoning, we are also able to recover
[
Cor19
, Proposition 4.10]
, and thus
[
Cor19
, Theorem 1.4]
, in the case
\mathbb{H}^{n}=\mathbb{W}\cdot\mathbb{L}
with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and
k
-dimensional, with
k<n
. However, our argument does not apply to the remaining case
k=n
, while the argument in the reference does.

Even if these results already appeared in the literature, we think it is worth writing them down with these different proofs. Indeed, the proof of
Section

5.1
provides a useful “toolkit” for the forthcoming theorems: in fact, a similar idea to the one exploited in
Section

5.1
will be used in
Theorem

6.6
to prove the analogous version of
Section

5.1
in the setting of free Carnot groups of step 2, when
\mathbb{L}
is one-dimensional. This will be the key step to obtain the analogous
Section

5.1
for Carnot groups of step 2 with
\mathbb{L}
one-dimensional. We refer the reader to the introduction of
Section

6
.

In
Section

5.2
below we give a class of nontrivial examples of
{\rm UID}
functions in the Engel group. We build them by making use of
Theorem

4.17
. This class of examples is inspired by
[
BV10
, Eq. (3.1)]
. Moreover, a slight modification of
[
BV10
, Eq. (3.1)]
, gives rise to functions whose intrinsic graphs are both of class
C^{1}_{\rm H}
and of class
C^{1}
, but they possess a characteristic point, see
Section

5.2
. We thank R. Serapioni for having discussed this example with us.

5.1.
A different proof of the propagation of broad* regularity in
\mathbb{H}^{n}
, with
n\geq 2

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be two complementary subgroups of
\mathbb{H}^{n}
, with
n\geq 2\,(
see
\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{example:Heisenberg})
such that
\mathbb{L}
is horizontal and
k
-dimensional with
k<n
, and let
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
be a continuous function, with
\widetilde{U}
open.

Then there exists
(X_{1},\dots,X_{2n+1})
an adapted basis of the Lie algebra such that
\mathbb{L}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\})
,
\mathbb{W}=\exp({\rm span}\{X_{k+1},\dots,X_{2n+1}\})
, and such that the only nonvanishing bracket relations are given by
[X_{i},X_{n+i}]=X_{2n+1}
for every
1\leq i\leq n
.

Moreover, if there exists a continuous function
\omega\colon U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2n+1-k}\to\mathbb{R}^{k\times(2n-k)}
such that
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
in the broad* sense, then
\widetilde{\varphi}
is vertically broad* holder. Namely, since we are in
\mathbb{H}^{n}
(see
Section

3.3
), for every
a_{0}\in U
there exists a neighborhood
U_{a_{0}}\Subset U
of
a_{0}
such that

(108)

\lim_{\varrho\to 0}\left(\sup\left\{\frac{|\varphi(\xi,z_{1})-\varphi(\xi,z_{2})|}{|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{1/2}}:(\xi,z_{1})\in U_{a_{0}},(\xi,z_{2})\in U_{a_{0}},0<|z_{1}-z_{2}|<\varrho\right\}\right)=0,

where we mean that
\xi\in U_{a_{0}}\cap\{z=0\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2n-k}
and
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
are the
(2n+1)^{th}
coordinates of
(\xi,z_{1})
and
(\xi,z_{2})
when seen as elements of
\mathbb{H}^{n}
.

Proof.

The existence of the basis as in the first part of the statement comes from
[
FSSC07
, Lemma 3.26]
. Fix
a_{0}\in U
and find
\delta>0
and sufficiently small neighborhoods
V^{\prime}_{a_{0}}\Subset V_{a_{0}}\Subset U
of
a_{0}
such that, for every
a\in V^{\prime}_{a_{0}}
, and every
j=k+1,\dots,2n
, we have the existence of integral curves
E^{\varphi}_{j}(a)\colon[-\delta,\delta]\to V_{a_{0}}
satisfying the conditions of
Section

3.4
. Denote by
\beta\colon[0,+\infty)\to[0,+\infty)
a modulus of uniform continuity for
\omega
on
\overline{V_{a_{0}}}
.

We fix a neighborhood
U_{a_{0}}\Subset V^{\prime}_{a_{0}}
of
a_{0}
,
\varrho>0
and points
(\xi,z_{1}),(\xi,z_{2})\in U_{a_{0}}
such that
|z_{1}-z_{2}|<\varrho
. The set
U_{a_{0}}
has to be chosen small enough: this will be clear during the proof. We are going to prove that, for every sufficiently small
\varrho
, we have

(109)

\frac{|\varphi(\xi,z_{1})-\varphi(\xi,z_{2})|}{|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{1/2}}\leq 2k\beta(\alpha(\varrho)),

for a continuous function
\alpha
with
\alpha(0)=0
that only depends on the norms of
\varphi
and
\omega
on
V_{a_{0}}
. From this fact (
108
) would follow, concluding the proof.

Recall that, by (
29
) and
since
k<n
, one has
D^{\varphi}_{k+1}=X_{k+1}
and
D^{\varphi}_{n+k+1}=X_{n+k+1}
. Assume without loss of generality that
z_{2}>z_{1}
, and set
t_{0}\coloneqq(z_{2}-z_{1})^{1/2}
. We exploit the relation
[D^{\varphi}_{k+1},D^{\varphi}_{n+k+1}]=X_{2n+1}
and the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula to conclude that we can join
(\xi,z_{1})
and
(\xi,z_{2})
by means of a concatenation of integral curves of
D^{\varphi}_{k+1}=X_{k+1}
and
D^{\varphi}_{n+k+1}=X_{n+k+1}
. In particular

(110)

\begin{split}a\coloneqq(\xi,z_{1})&\to_{E^{\varphi}_{k+1}(a)}\,a_{1}\coloneqq E^{\varphi}_{k+1}(a,t_{0})\to_{E^{\varphi}_{n+k+1}(a_{1})}\,a_{2}\coloneqq E^{\varphi}_{n+k+1}(a_{1},t_{0})\\
&\to_{E^{\varphi}_{k+1}(a_{2})}\,a_{3}\coloneqq E^{\varphi}_{k+1}(a_{2},-t_{0})\to_{E^{\varphi}_{n+k+1}(a_{3})}\,a_{4}\coloneqq(\xi,z_{2})=E^{\varphi}_{n+k+1}(a_{3},-t_{0}),\end{split}

and if
\varrho
is sufficiently small with respect to
\delta
(for example
\varrho<\delta^{2}
) we know that the integral lines in (
110
) are defined in
[-t_{0},t_{0}]
and all the points
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\in V^{\prime}_{a_{0}}
. Notice that it is precisely here that we have to take
U_{a_{0}}
small enough to guarantee that the points defined in (
110
) are in
V^{\prime}_{a_{0}}
. This can be done since we are taking the concatenation of integral curves living up to a time whose norm is bounded above by
t_{0}<\varrho^{1/2}
.

We set
\varphi=:(\varphi^{(1)},\dots,\varphi^{(k)})
in coordinates. From
Section

3.4
we get that, for every
a\in V^{\prime}_{a_{0}}
, we have

(111)

\frac{\de}{\de\!s}{|_{s=t}}\varphi^{(\ell)}(E^{\varphi}_{j}(a,s))=\omega_{\ell j}(E^{\varphi}_{j}(a,t)),\qquad\forall j=k+1,\dots,2n+1,\quad\forall\ell=1,\dots,k,\quad\forall t\in[-\delta,\delta].

Thus, by using the points defined in (
110
), Lagrange’s theorem and the triangle inequality, we get

(112)

\begin{split}\frac{|\varphi(\xi,z_{1})-\varphi(\xi,z_{2})|}{|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{1/2}}&=\frac{\left|\left(\varphi(a)-\varphi(a_{1})\right)+\left(\varphi(a_{1})-\varphi(a_{2})\right)+\left(\varphi(a_{2})-\varphi(a_{3})\right)+\left(\varphi(a_{3})-\varphi(a_{4})\right)\right|}{|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{1/2}}\\
&\leq\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\frac{1}{|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{1/2}}\cdot\Big|\left(\varphi^{(\ell)}(a)-\varphi^{(\ell)}(a_{1})\right)+\left(\varphi^{(\ell)}(a_{1})-\varphi^{(\ell)}(a_{2})\right)+\\
&+\left(\varphi^{(\ell)}(a_{2})-\varphi^{(\ell)}(a_{3})\right)+\left(\varphi^{(\ell)}(a_{3})-\varphi^{(\ell)}(a_{4})\right)\Big|\\
&=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\frac{|-t_{0}\omega_{\ell,k+1}(b_{1,\ell})-t_{0}\omega_{\ell,n+k+1}(b_{2,\ell})+t_{0}\omega_{\ell,k+1}(b_{3,\ell})+t_{0}\omega_{\ell,n+k+1}(b_{4,\ell})|}{t_{0}}\\
&\leq\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\left(|\omega_{\ell,k+1}(b_{3,\ell})-\omega_{\ell,k+1}(b_{1,\ell})|+|\omega_{\ell,n+k+1}(b_{4,\ell})-\omega_{\ell,n+k+1}(b_{2,\ell})|\right)\\
&\leq\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\left(\beta(|b_{3,\ell}-b_{1,\ell}|)+\beta(|b_{4,\ell}-b_{2,\ell}|)\right),\end{split}

where, for every
\ell=1,\dots,k
, the points
b_{1,\ell},b_{2,\ell},b_{3,\ell},b_{4,\ell}
are respectively chosen according to Lagrange’s Theorem, that can be applied in view of (
111
), on the images of the integral curves
E^{\varphi}_{k+1}(a)
,
E^{\varphi}_{n+k+1}(a_{1})
,
E^{\varphi}_{k+1}(a_{2})
,
E^{\varphi}_{n+k+1}(a_{3})
used in (
110
). By simple estimates relying on the triangle inequality, we get a constant
C_{0}>0
only depending on
V_{a_{0}}
such that for every
\ell=1,\dots,k
, the estimates
|b_{3,\ell}-b_{1,\ell}|\leq C_{0}t_{0}
, and
|b_{4,\ell}-b_{2,\ell}|\leq C_{0}t_{0}
hold. Since
t_{0}=|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{1/2}
, and
|z_{1}-z_{2}|<\varrho
, from (
112
) we thus get (
109
) with
\alpha(\varrho)\coloneqq C_{0}\varrho^{1/2}
.
∎

Proposition 5.2
.

In the setting of
Section

5.1
, it holds

D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega\quad\mbox{{\rm in the broad* sense}}\Rightarrow\widetilde{\varphi}\in{\rm UID}(\widetilde{U},\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L}).

Proof.

It is a direct consequence of
Section

5.1
and
Section

4.1
. Indeed, taking into account (
30
) of
Section

3.1
, the integral curves of
D^{\varphi}_{2n+1}
are vertical lines and, therefore, condition (
108
) of
Section

5.1
implies that
\varphi
is vertically broad* hölder.
∎

5.2.
Examples of uniformly intrinsically differentiable functions

We show a class of nontrivial examples of
{\rm UID}
functions in the Engel group, see
Section

3.1
.

Example 5.3
.

Consider the Engel group
\mathbb{E}
, with the splitting
\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{W}\cdot\mathbb{L}
described in
Section

3.1
. We show that the function

(113)

\varphi_{\alpha}(0,x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})\coloneqq x_{4}^{\alpha}\chi_{\{x_{4}\geq 0\}}(0,x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}),

produces
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\alpha}\in{\rm UID}(\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
for any
\alpha>1/3
. We first claim that
D^{\varphi_{\alpha}}_{X_{2}}\varphi_{\alpha}=\frac{\alpha}{2}x_{4}^{3\alpha-1}\chi_{\{x_{4}\geq 0\}}
in the broad* sense.
Indeed, for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, the functions

(\varphi_{\alpha})_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\varepsilon^{1/3}\chi_{\{x_{4}<0\}}+(x_{4}^{3\alpha}+\varepsilon)^{1/3}\chi_{\{x_{4}\geq 0\}}

are globally
C^{1}
and

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}(\varphi_{\alpha})_{\varepsilon}=\varphi_{\alpha}\quad\mbox{ in }L^{\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\quad\text{ and }\quad D^{(\varphi_{\alpha})_{\varepsilon}}_{X_{2}}(\varphi_{\alpha})_{\varepsilon}=\frac{\alpha}{2}x_{4}^{3\alpha-1}\chi_{\{x_{4}\geq 0\}},\quad\forall\varepsilon\in(0,1),

where the last equality comes from the particular form of
D_{X_{2}}^{\varphi}
in (
33
).
By applying
Section

4.3
, we get the claim.

We claim now that
\varphi
is vertically broad* holder, see
Section

4.1
. Indeed, since the integral curves of
D_{X_{4}}^{\varphi_{\alpha}}
are the vertical lines along direction
x_{4}
, see (
33
), and since
\alpha>1/3
, we get that
\varphi_{\alpha}
is locally uniformly
1/3
-little Hölder continuous along these curves. We are left to prove that, locally around any
a\coloneqq(0,x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
, condition (
89
) is satisfied for the integral curves of
D_{X_{3}}^{\varphi_{\alpha}}
, whose expression is in (
33
).

According to the sign of
x_{4}
we identify three families of integral curves of the vector field
D^{\varphi_{\alpha}}_{X_{3}}
starting from
a
. If
x_{4}<0
, the only integral curve of
D_{X_{3}}^{\varphi_{\alpha}}
starting from
a
is
\gamma(t)=(0,x_{2},x_{3}+t,x_{4})
, and it is well-defined for every time
t
. If
x_{4}=0
, we have that an integral curve of
D^{\varphi_{\alpha}}_{X_{3}}
, existing for all times
t
, starting from
a
is given by

\gamma(t)=\left(0,x_{2},x_{3}+t,(1-\alpha)^{1/(1-\alpha)}t^{1/(1-\alpha)}\chi_{\{t\geq 0\}}(t)\right),

while, if
x_{4}>0
, we have that an integral curve of
D^{\varphi_{\alpha}}_{X_{3}}
starting from
a
is given by

\gamma(t)=\left(0,x_{2},x_{3}+t,(1-\alpha)^{1/(1-\alpha)}\left(t+\frac{(x_{4})^{1-\alpha}}{1-\alpha}\right)^{1/(1-\alpha)}\right),

defined for
t\in\left(-\frac{x_{4}^{1-\alpha}}{1-\alpha},+\infty\right)
. By exploiting this explicit choice of integral curves, we notice that
\gamma(t)
, in the three cases, is constant or it is of order
t^{1/(1-\alpha)}
on the last component. Thus, we get that
\varphi_{\alpha}
is 1/2-little Hölder continuous along these integral curves, because
\alpha/(1-\alpha)>1/2
for any
\alpha>1/3
, and this happens locally uniformly. From this, we conclude that
\varphi_{\alpha}
is vertically broad* holder.
Now we conclude by applying (d)
\Rightarrow
(a) of
Theorem

4.17
.

Notice that, if
\alpha=1/3
, the function
\varphi_{\alpha}
is not UID in every neighborhood of the origin. Indeed, by
Theorem

4.17
, if
\varphi_{\alpha}
is UID, then it is vertically broad* hölder and so 1/3-little Hölder continuous along the integral curves of the vector field
D^{\varphi_{\alpha}}_{X_{4}}
. This means that
\varphi_{\alpha}
should be
1/3
-little Hölder continuous in the last coordinate, but this is not true when
\alpha=1/3
.

We remark here that, in the case of the Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{1}
, a slight modification of this types of examples gives rise to a
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-hypersurface, that is also
C^{1}
Euclidean, but it has 0 as a characteristic point, see
Section

5.2
.

Remark 5.4
.

Consider the first Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{1}
identified with
\mathbb{R}^{3}
by means of exponential coordinates, and consider the splitting
\mathbb{H}^{1}=\mathbb{W}\cdot\mathbb{L}
described in
Section

3.1
, with
\mathbb{L}
one-dimensional. Define
\varphi\colon\mathbb{W}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{L}\equiv\mathbb{R}
by setting
\varphi(0,x_{2},x_{3})\coloneqq{\rm sgn}(x_{3})|x_{3}|^{2/3}
.

Since
\varphi^{2}(0,x_{2},x_{3})=|x_{3}|^{4/3}\in C^{1}
, by
[
ASCV06
, Corollary 5.11]
, then
\widetilde{\varphi}\in{\rm UID}(\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
and, consequently, its graph is a
C^{1}_{\rm H}
-hypersurface. Moreover, in coordinates, one has

(114)

(0,x_{2},x_{3})\cdot(\varphi(0,x_{2},x_{3}),0,0)=\left({\rm sgn}(x_{3})|x_{3}|^{2/3},x_{2},x_{3}-\frac{1}{2}{\rm sgn}(x_{3})x_{2}|x_{3}|^{2/3}\right).

The surface
\Sigma
parametrized by (
114
) is the union of two surfaces
\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2}
given by

(115)

\begin{split}\Sigma_{1}&\coloneqq\left\{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:x_{1}\geq 0,x_{3}-x_{1}^{3/2}+\frac{1}{2}x_{1}x_{2}=0\right\},\\
\Sigma_{2}&\coloneqq\left\{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:x_{1}\leq 0,x_{3}+(-x_{1})^{3/2}+\frac{1}{2}x_{1}x_{2}=0\right\},\end{split}

that are glued along the
x_{2}
-axis
\{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:x_{1}=x_{3}=0\}
. We thus get that, for any
x\in{\rm int}(\Sigma_{1})
, one has
T_{x}\Sigma_{1}=\left(\frac{1}{2}x_{2}-\frac{3}{2}x_{1}^{1/2},\frac{1}{2}x_{1},1\right)^{\perp}
with respect to the standard Euclidean scalar product, while for any
x\in{\rm int}(\Sigma_{2})
, one has
T_{x}\Sigma_{2}=\left(\frac{1}{2}x_{2}-\frac{3}{2}(-x_{1})^{1/2},\frac{1}{2}x_{1},1\right)^{\perp}
with respect to the standard Euclidean scalar product. From these explicit expressions,
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
glue together in
C^{1}
regular way along the
x_{2}
-axis, and thus
\Sigma
is also
C^{1}
-Euclidean surface. Moreover, from the previous expressions, we get that
T_{0}\Sigma=(0,0,1)^{\perp}=\{x_{3}=0\}=V_{1}
and hence
0
is a characteristic point for
\Sigma
.

We remark here that a similar example appeared in
[
FSSC07
, Remark 3.8]
. In that case the intrinsic graph is
C^{1}_{\rm H}
regular but not
C^{1}
regular.

6.
Carnot groups of step 2

As already underlined in
Section

4.4
, a co-horizontal
C^{1}_{\mathrm{H}}
-surface cannot be always characterized only by its horizontal geometry. This is however possible inside Carnot groups of step 2. Indeed, in this section, we show that the assumption on the vertical broad* hölder regularity of
Theorem

4.17
can be dropped when
\mathbb{G}
is a Carnot group of step 2 and
\mathbb{L}
is a horizontal one-dimensional subgroup of
\mathbb{G}
. In particular, as main result of this section, we get
Theorem

6.17
, which is
Theorem

1.7
in the introduction.

We describe the strategy of the proof of
Theorem

6.17
. The key idea is to first show that the implication

(116)

D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega\;\mbox{broad*}\;\Rightarrow\;\varphi\;\mbox{vertically broad* h\"{o}lder}

holds in free Carnot groups of step 2, for a continuous
\omega
. This is done in two main steps. First, we explicitly write the intrinsic vector fields
D^{\varphi}
and we notice that the nonlinearity given by
\varphi
only shows up in one vertical coordinate for each vector field, see (
120
). Second, by using the structure of the vector fields
D^{\varphi}
, one can propagate the broad* regularity from the horizontal components of
\varphi
to the little Hölder regularity along vertical components by using a geometric trick: if
[X,Y]=Z
, and
\varphi
is
C^{1}
on the integral curves of
X
and
Y
, we expect
\varphi
to be
1/2
-little Hölder continuous on the integral curves of
Z
.

More precisely, the first step allows us to obtain the
1/2
-little Hölder regularity on the vertical coordinates affected by the nonlinearity by means of an adaptation of
[
ASCV06
, Theorem 5.8]
. The second step allows us to obtain the
1/2
-little Hölder regularity on all the remaining vertical coordinates. Notice that the efforts made to prove (d)
\Rightarrow
(a) in
Theorem

4.17
, asking for just the regularity along the integral curves of
D^{\varphi}
(i.e., the vertically broad* regularity), are here payed back from a crucial simplification of this proof. Indeed, to conclude the proof of
Theorem

6.17
in the case of free Carnot groups of step 2, namely
Theorem

6.6
, we just use (
116
) and apply (d)
\Rightarrow
(a) of
Theorem

4.17
. This can be done after having noticed that on Carnot groups of step 2 the vertically broad* hölder regularity reads as the locally 1/2-little Hölder continuity along vertical coordinates, see also
Section

3.3
.

Finally, to conclude the proof of the difficult implication (e)
\Rightarrow
(a) of
Theorem

6.17
, we use (
116
) together with the fact that, on a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
of step 2, the broad* condition lifts to the free Carnot group
\mathbb{F}
of step 2 and of the same rank of
\mathbb{G}
, see
Section

6.2
, while having a vertically broad* hölder property is naturally transferred from
\mathbb{F}
to
\mathbb{G}
, see
Section

6.2
. The resulting strategy presents some similarity to
[
LDPS19
]
.

We point out that (a)
\Leftrightarrow
(c) of
Theorem

6.17
is a generalization to all step 2 Carnot groups of
[
ASCV06
, Theorem 5.1]
and (a)
\Leftrightarrow
(e) is a generalization of
[
BSC10b
, Theorem 1.2]
. We refer the reader to the introduction for a more detailed discussion on the literature.

In the current section, without loss of generality, we will always work in coordinates and there will be no distinction between
\square
and
\widetilde{\square}
. See
Section

6.1
for details on the identifications.

6.1.
Regularity results for broad* solutions in free Carnot groups of step 2

Free-nilpotent Lie algebras can be defined as follows (see Definition 14.1.1 in
[
BLU07
]
).

Definition 6.1

(Free-nilpotent Lie algebras of step 2)
.

Let
m\geq 2
be integer. We say that
\mathfrak{f}_{m,2}
is the
free-nilpotent Lie algebra of step 2 with
m
generators

X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
if the following facts hold.

(i)

\mathfrak{f}_{m,2}
is a Lie algebra generated by the elements
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
(i.e.,
\mathfrak{f}_{m,2}
is the smallest Lie algebra containing
\{X_{1},\dots,X_{m}\}
);

(ii)

\mathfrak{f}_{m,2}
is nilpotent of step
2
(i.e., nested Lie brackets of length
3
are always
0
);

(iii)

for every nilpotent Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
of step
2
and for every map
\Psi\colon\{X_{1},\dots,X_{m}\}\to\mathfrak{g}
, there exists a unique homomorphism of Lie algebras
\overline{\Psi}:\mathfrak{f}_{m,2}\to\mathfrak{g}
that extends
\Psi
.

Definition 6.2

(Free Carnot groups of step 2)
.

A
free Carnot group of step 2
is a Carnot group whose Lie algebra is isomorphic to a
free-nilpotent Lie algebra
\mathfrak{f}_{m,2}
for some
m\geq 2
. In this case, the horizontal layer of
the free Carnot group is isomorphic to the linear span of the generators of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{f}_{m,2}
.

Remark 6.3

(Free Carnot groups of step 2 in exponential coordinates)
.

We give an explicit representation of free Carnot groups of step
2
. Fix an integer
m\geq 2
and denote by
n\coloneqq m+\frac{m(m-1)}{2}
. In
\mathbb{R}^{n}
denote the coordinates by
x_{j}
, for
1\leq j\leq m
, and by
y_{\ell s}
, for

1\leq s<\ell\leq m
. Let
\partial_{j}
and
\partial_{\ell s}
denote the standard basis vectors in this coordinate system.
We define
n
linearly independent vector fields on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
by setting:

(117)

\displaystyle X_{j}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\partial_{j}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j<\ell\leq m}x_{\ell}\partial_{\ell j}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{1\leq\ell<j}x_{\ell}\partial_{j\ell},\quad\mbox{ if }1\leq j\leq m,

\displaystyle Y_{\ell s}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\partial_{\ell s},\hphantom{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j<\ell\leq m}x_{\ell}\partial_{\ell j}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{1\leq\ell<j}x_{\ell}\partial_{j\ell}}\qquad\mbox{ if }1\leq s<\ell\leq m.

Let
\mathbb{F}\coloneqq(\mathbb{R}^{m+\frac{m(m-1)}{2}},\cdot)
be the coordinate representation of the step 2 Carnot group with
m
generators whose Lie algebra is generated by the vector fields in (
117
). Then
\mathbb{F}
is free and its Carnot structure is given by

V_{1}\coloneqq\mbox{span}\{X_{j}\,:\,1\leq j\leq m\}\quad\mbox{and}\quad V_{2}\coloneqq\mbox{span}\{Y_{\ell s}\,:\,1\leq s<\ell\leq m\}.

Moreover, for any
p
and
q\in\mathbb{F}
, the product
p\cdot q
is given by the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula, and yields

\displaystyle(p\cdot q)_{j}

\displaystyle=p_{j}+q_{j},\quad\quad\qquad\qquad\qquad\,\,\,\,\,\text{ if }1\leq j\leq m,

\displaystyle(p\cdot q)_{\ell s}

\displaystyle=p_{\ell s}+q_{\ell s}+\frac{1}{2}(p_{\ell}q_{s}-q_{\ell}p_{s}),\quad\text{ if }1\leq s<\ell\leq m.

It is easily verified that for
1\leq s<\ell\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq m
, one has

(118)

[X_{\ell},X_{s}]=Y_{\ell s}\quad\mbox{and}\quad[X_{j},Y_{\ell s}]=0.

Remark 6.4
.

Let
\mathbb{F}
be a free Carnot groups of step
2
and rank
m
.
To keep the notation simpler,
throughout this section, we identify without loss of generality
\mathbb{F}
with
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
n\coloneqq m+\frac{m(m+1)}{2}
by means of the coordinates described in
Section

6.1
. Moreover, given two complementary subgroups
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and one-dimensional, we identify them in the following way:

(119)

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:x_{2}=\dots=x_{n}=0\right\},

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:x_{1}=0\right\}.

Therefore, there are natural identifications between
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
and
\mathbb{W}
and between
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{L}
. We stress that, given an arbitrary free Carnot group of step 2 and complementary subgroups
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
, with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and one-dimensional, we can always choose coordinates satisfying (
119
) and such that the identifications of
Section

6.1
are satisfied.

Remark 6.5

(Projected vector fields on free Carnot groups of step 2)
.

Let
\mathbb{F}
be a free Carnot group of step
2
represented in coordinates as in
Section

6.1
and let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be complementary subgroups of
\mathbb{F}
as in (
119
). Given
V\subseteq\mathbb{W}
an open set, and given a continuous map
\psi\colon V\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
, according to
Section

3.1
, the projected vector fields are given by

(120)

\displaystyle D_{j}^{\psi}=\partial_{j}-\psi\partial_{j1}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j<\ell\leq m}x_{\ell}\partial_{\ell j}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{1<s<j}x_{s}\partial_{js}={X_{j}}_{|V}-\psi{Y_{j1}}_{|V},

\displaystyle\mbox{ for }j=2,\dots,m,

\displaystyle D^{\psi}_{\ell s}=\partial_{\ell s}={Y_{\ell s}}_{|\mathbb{W}},

\displaystyle\mbox{ for }1\leq s<\ell\leq m.

Then, for each
j=2,\dots,m
, every integral curve
\gamma_{j}\colon I\to\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
of
D^{\varphi}_{j}
has vertical components
y\coloneqq(y_{\ell s})_{1\leq s<\ell\leq m}\colon I\to\mathbb{R}^{\frac{m(m-1)}{2}}
satisfying the following equations

\displaystyle\dot{y}_{j1}(t)

\displaystyle=-\psi(x_{2},\dots,x_{j-1},x_{j}+t,x_{j+1},\dots,x_{m},y(t)),

\displaystyle\dot{y}_{\ell j}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}x_{\ell},\quad\quad\qquad\mbox{ if }j<\ell\leq m,

\displaystyle\dot{y}_{js}(t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}x_{s},\quad\,\qquad\mbox{ if }1<s<j,

\displaystyle\dot{y}_{\ell s}(t)

\displaystyle=0,\quad\qquad\quad\,\quad\mbox{ otherwise,}

where the horizontal components of
\gamma_{j}(0)
are
(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
.

We now prove that, given an open set
V\subseteq\mathbb{W}
and a continuous function
\psi\colon V\to\mathbb{L}
, a broad* solution of
D^{\psi}\psi=\omega
for some continuous
\omega
is also vertically broad* hölder (see
Section

4.1
). In particular, since the vector field
D^{\psi}_{\ell s}
satisfies
D^{\psi}_{\ell s}=\partial_{{\ell s}}
for every
1\leq s<\ell\leq m
(see (
120
)), then (
89
) is equivalent to the following condition: for every
a_{0}
in
V
, there exists a neighborhood
V^{\prime}
of
a_{0}
with
V^{\prime}\Subset V
such that for every
\ell
and
s
with
1\leq s<\ell\leq m
, one has

(121)

\lim_{\varrho\to 0}\left(\sup\left\{\frac{|\psi(\xi,\eta)-\psi(\xi,y)|}{|\eta_{\ell s}-y_{ls}|^{1/2}}\right\}\right)=0,

where the supremum is taken on all the couples
(\xi,\eta),(\xi,y)\in V^{\prime}
such that
\eta_{k\tau}=y_{k\tau}
for any
(k,\tau)\neq(\ell,s)
and
0<|\eta_{\ell s}-y_{\ell s}|\leq\varrho
.

In the first part of the proof of
Theorem

6.6
below, we use techniques that are similar to the ones exploited in
[
ASCV06
, Theorems 5.8 and 5.9]
in the context of Heisenberg groups. The third step of the proof is new.

Theorem 6.6
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be two complementary subgroups of the free Carnot group
\mathbb{F}
of step 2 with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and one-dimensional. Let
V\subseteq\mathbb{W}
be open and
\psi\colon{V}\to\mathbb{L}
be a continuous function and assume that
\psi
is a broad* solution of the system
D^{\psi}\psi=\omega
in
V
, for some
\omega\in C(V;\mathbb{R}^{m-1})
, with respect to the basis chosen in
Section

6.1
and
Section

6.1
.
Then
\psi
is vertically broad* hölder.

Proof.

Working in the coordinates described in
Section

6.1
and
Section

6.1
, we can assume without loss of generality that
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
are defined as in (
119
). If
m=2
, then
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{H}^{1}
, and therefore the statement would follow from
[
BSC10b
, Theorem 1.2]
.
We therefore assume that
m>2
.

We prove the following stronger fact, from which (
121
) follows. For each
a_{0}\in V
there are sufficiently small neighborhoods
I\Subset I^{\prime}\Subset V
of
a_{0}
such that, for every
1\leq s<\ell\leq m
, one can find a continuous and increasing function
\alpha_{\ell s}\colon(0,+\infty)\to[0,+\infty)
only depending on
I^{\prime}
,
\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}(I^{\prime})}
,
\|\omega\|_{L^{\infty}(I^{\prime})}
and on the modulus of continuity of
\omega
on
I^{\prime}
with the property that

(122)

\lim_{\varrho\to 0}\alpha_{\ell s}(\varrho)=0,

and

(123)

\begin{split}\frac{|\psi(\xi,\eta)-\psi(\xi,y)|}{|\eta_{\ell s}-y_{\ell s}|^{1/2}}\leq\alpha_{\ell s}(\varrho),\end{split}

for every
(\xi,\eta),(\xi,y)\in I
such that
\eta_{k\tau}=y_{k\tau}
for every
(k,\tau)\neq(\ell,s)
and
0<|y_{\ell s}-\eta_{\ell s}|\leq\varrho
.

Fix
a_{0}\in V
. Since
\psi
is a broad* solution of
D^{\psi}\psi=\omega
in
V
, there exist
0<\delta_{2}<\delta_{1}
and a family of maps

E_{j}^{\psi}\colon\overline{B(a_{0},\delta_{2})}\times[-\delta_{2},\delta_{2}]\to\overline{B(a_{0},\delta_{1})},

for
j=2,\dots,m
, such that the conditions of
Section

3.4
are satisfied.
Define
I^{\prime}\coloneqq\overline{B(a_{0},\delta_{1})}
and
I\coloneqq\overline{B(a_{0},\delta_{2})},
and set

M_{1}\coloneqq\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}(I^{\prime})}
. Let also
\beta
be an increasing modulus of uniform continuity of
\omega
on
I^{\prime}
. We are going to prove (
123
) with
\alpha_{\ell s}
defined by

(124)

\alpha_{\ell s}(\varrho)\coloneqq\begin{cases}3\delta(\varrho),&\mbox{ if }(\ell,s)=(j,1)\mbox{ and }j=2,\dots,m,\\
G_{\ell s}(\varrho),&\mbox{ otherwise,}\end{cases}

where
G_{\ell s}
will be determined later in (
138
) and

(125)

\delta(\varrho)\coloneqq\max\left\{\varrho^{1/4},\sqrt{\beta(C\varrho^{1/4})}\right\},

for some constant
C\coloneqq C(j)>0
such that

(126)

|\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}|+2(|\xi|+M_{1})|\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}|^{1/4}\leq C|\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}|^{1/4}

for any
(\xi,\eta),(\xi,y)\in I^{\prime}
with
\eta_{k\tau}=y_{k\tau}
for every couple
(k,\tau)\neq(j,1)
.

First step.
If
a=(x,y)\in I
, by using (
120
), we have that for any
2\leq j\leq m
and any
t\in[-\delta_{2},\delta_{2}]
, it holds

(127)

\displaystyle E_{j}^{\psi}(a,t)

\displaystyle=(x_{2},\dots,x_{j-1},x_{j}+t,x_{j+1},\dots,x_{m},y(t)),\qquad\text{where}

\displaystyle y_{\ell s}(t)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}y_{j1}-\int_{0}^{t}\psi(E_{j}^{\psi}(a,r))\,\de\!r,&\mbox{if }(\ell,s)=(j,1),\\
\frac{1}{2}tx_{\ell}+y_{\ell j},&\mbox{if }s=j,\text{ and }j<\ell\leq m,\\
-\frac{1}{2}tx_{s}+y_{js},&\mbox{if }\ell=j,\text{ and }1<s<j,\\
y_{\ell s},&\mbox{otherwise,}\end{cases}

and consequently, since
E^{\psi}_{j}
are the maps provided by
Section

3.4
,

t\mapsto y_{j1}(t)
is a solution of the Cauchy problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\ddot{y}_{j1}(t)=\displaystyle\frac{\de}{\de\!t}\Big[-\psi(E_{j}^{\psi}(a,t))\,\Big]=-\omega_{j}(E_{j}^{\psi}(a,t)),\qquad t\in[-\delta_{2},\delta_{2}],\\
\\
y_{j1}(0)=y_{j1},\\
\\
\dot{y}_{j1}(0)=-\psi(a).\end{array}\right.

As a consequence of (
127
) one gets

(128)

\displaystyle\max_{r\in[-|t|,|t|]}|\dot{y}(r)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left|\sum_{j<\ell\leq m}x_{\ell}\right|+\frac{1}{2}\left|\sum_{1<s<j}x_{s}\right|+\max_{r\in[-|t|,|t|]}\left|\psi(E_{j}^{\psi}(a,r))\right|\leq C_{1}(|x|+M_{1}),

for every
t\in[-\delta_{2},\delta_{2}]
, where the constant
C_{1}>0
only depends on the topological dimension of
\mathbb{F}
.

Second step.
Fix
j=2,\dots,m
and assume
a=(\xi,\eta),\hat{a}=(\xi,y)\in I
with
\eta_{k\tau}=y_{k\tau}
for all couples
(k,\tau)\neq(j,1)
. We will need to possibly shrink
I
in a way that will be clear throughout the proof.
We aim to show that

(129)

\frac{|\psi(a)-\psi(\hat{a})|}{|\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}|^{1/2}}\leq\alpha_{j1}(|\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}|),

where, according to (
124
),
\alpha_{j1}(\varrho)=3\delta(\varrho)
. This would imply (
123
) for
(\ell,s)=(j,1)
.

Set
\delta\coloneqq\delta(|\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}|)
and suppose (
129
) is not true, namely

(130)

\frac{|\psi(a)-\psi(\hat{a})|}{|\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}|^{1/2}}>3\delta.

Let
E_{j}^{\psi}(a,\cdot)
and
E_{j}^{\psi}(\hat{a},\cdot)
be the integral curves of
D^{\psi}_{j}
given by the broad* condition. By the first step they satisfy

E_{j}^{\psi}(a,t)=(\xi_{2},\dots,\xi_{j-1},\xi_{j}+t,\xi_{j+1},\dots,\xi_{m},\eta(t)),

and

E_{j}^{\psi}(\hat{a},t)=(\xi_{2},\dots,\xi_{j-1},\xi_{j}+t,\xi_{j+1},\dots,\xi_{m},y(t)).

We claim we can find
t^{*}\in[-\delta_{2},\delta_{2}]
such that
\eta_{j1}(t^{*})=y_{j1}(t^{*})
, with
\psi(E_{j}^{\psi}(a,t^{*}))\neq\psi(E_{j}^{\psi}(\hat{a},t^{*}))
. This will lead to a contradiction, by the fact that the equality
\eta_{j1}(t^{*})=y_{j1}(t^{*})
would also imply
\eta(t^{*})=y(t^{*})
.

Without loss of generality,
assume that the initial data satisfy
\eta_{j1}>y_{j1}
. By the first step of this proof, for every
t\in[-\delta_{2},\delta_{2}]
, one has

\displaystyle\eta_{j1}(t)-y_{j1}(t)-(\eta_{j1}-y_{j1})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\left[\dot{\eta}_{j1}(0)-\dot{y}_{j1}(0)+\int_{0}^{r^{\prime}}(\ddot{\eta}_{j1}(r)-\ddot{y}_{j1}(r))\,\de\!r\right]\de\!r^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-t(\psi(a)-\psi(\hat{a}))-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{r^{\prime}}\left(\omega_{j}(E_{j}^{\psi}(a,r))-\omega_{j}(E_{j}^{\psi}(\hat{a},r))\right)\,\de\!r\de\!r^{\prime}.

Using (
128
) and the fundamental theorem of Calculus, one gets a constant
C_{2}>0
only depending on
C_{1}
such that

(131)

\displaystyle|E_{j}^{\psi}(a,r)-E_{j}^{\psi}(\hat{a},r)|

\displaystyle\leq|\eta-y|+|r|\left(\max_{r\in[-|t|,|t|]}|\dot{\eta}(r)|+\max_{r\in[-|t|,|t|]}|\dot{y}(r)|\right)

\displaystyle\leq|\eta-y|+|t|\left(\max_{r\in[-|t|,|t|]}|\dot{\eta}(r)|+\max_{r\in[-|t|,|t|]}|\dot{y}(r)|\right)

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\left(|\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}|+2|t|(|\xi|+M_{1})\right),

for every
r\in[-|t|,|t|]
and
t\in[-\delta_{2},\delta_{2}]
. Hence, we obtain that

(132)

\displaystyle\eta_{j1}(t)-y_{j1}(t)-(\eta_{j1}-y_{j1})

\displaystyle\leq-t(\psi(a)-\psi(\hat{a}))+t^{2}\max_{r\in[-|t|,|t|]}\beta\big(|E_{j}^{\psi}(a,r)-E_{j}^{\psi}(\hat{a},r)|\big)

\displaystyle\leq-t(\psi(a)-\psi(\hat{a}))+t^{2}\beta\left(C_{2}(|\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}|+2|t|(|\xi|+M_{1}))\right),

for every
t\in[-\delta_{2},\delta_{2}]
. Up to redefining
\beta
, we can replace without loss of generality
\beta(C_{2}\varrho)
with
\beta(\varrho)
. Now, by (
130
) we know that

(133)

-|\psi(a)-\psi(\hat{a})|<-3\delta|\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}|^{1/2}.

Without loss of generality, up to restricting
I
, we can assume that

(134)

\delta_{2}\geq|y_{j1}-\eta_{j1}|^{1/4}\geq|y_{j1}-\eta_{j1}|^{1/2}/\delta,

where the second inequality directly follows from (
125
).
If
\psi(a)=\psi(\hat{a})
, then (
129
) would be trivial. We study two cases:
\psi(a)-\psi(\hat{a})<0
or
\psi(a)-\psi(\hat{a})>0
. If
\psi(a)-\psi(\hat{a})<0
, set
t_{0}\coloneqq-\frac{|\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}|^{1/2}}{\delta}
(otherwise we can choose
t_{0}\coloneqq\frac{|\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}|^{1/2}}{\delta}
) and evaluate
(
132
)
in
t=t_{0}
. Combining it with (
126
), (
133
), (
134
), the definition of
\delta
, and the fact that
\beta
is increasing, we obtain (in both cases)

(135)

\displaystyle\eta_{j1}(t_{0})-y_{j1}(t_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}+|\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}|^{1/2}\frac{-|\psi(a)-\psi(\hat{a})|}{\delta}+

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}|\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}|\beta\left(|\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}|+2(|\xi|+M_{1})\frac{|\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}|^{1/2}}{\delta}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}-3|\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}|+|\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}|\frac{\beta\left(C|\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}|^{1/4}\right)}{\delta^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}-3|\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}|+|\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}|\leq-|\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}|<0.

If
\psi(a)-\psi(\hat{a})>0
we can define

t^{*}\coloneqq\sup\{\,r\in[0,\delta_{2}]\,:\,\eta_{j1}(s)-y_{j1}(s)>0,\,\forall s\in[0,r]\},

since the set
\{\,r\in[0,\delta_{2}]\,:\,\eta_{j1}(s)-y_{j1}(s)>0,\,\forall s\in[0,r]\}
is not empty; indeed, recall that we assumed without loss of generality that
\eta_{j1}>y_{j1}
and therefore
\eta_{j1}(0)-y_{j1}(0)=\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}>0
. Moreover
0<t^{*}<t_{0}\leq\delta_{2}
, and recalling that
\eta_{k\tau}=y_{k\tau}
except for
(k,\tau)=(j,1)
, one has, by (
127
) that

(136)

\displaystyle\eta_{j1}(t^{*})

\displaystyle=y_{j1}(t^{*}),

\displaystyle\eta_{\ell j}(t^{*})

\displaystyle=y_{\ell j}(t^{*})=\frac{1}{2}t^{*}\xi_{\ell}+\eta_{\ell j},\quad\qquad\quad\mbox{ for }j<\ell\leq m,

\displaystyle\eta_{js}(t^{*})

\displaystyle=y_{js}(t^{*})=-\frac{1}{2}t^{*}\xi_{s}+\eta_{js},\,\quad\quad\quad\mbox{ for }1<s<j,

\displaystyle\eta_{\ell s}(t^{*})

\displaystyle=y_{\ell s}(t^{*})=\eta_{\ell s},\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\,\,\,\,\mbox{ otherwise.}

Hence, by definition of
E_{j}^{\psi}(a,\cdot)
and
E_{j}^{\psi}(\hat{a},\cdot)
in (
127
), one gets
E_{j}^{\psi}(a,t^{*})=E_{j}^{\psi}(\hat{a},t^{*})
and therefore

(137)

\displaystyle\psi(E_{j}^{\psi}(a,t^{*}))=\psi(E_{j}^{\psi}(\hat{a},t^{*})).

In the case
\psi(a)-\psi(\hat{a})<0
, we consider
t_{0}=-\frac{|\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}|^{1/2}}{\delta}
and define
t^{*}\coloneqq\inf\{r\in[-\delta_{2},0]:\eta_{j1}(s)-y_{j1}(s)>0,\,\forall s\in[r,0]\}
. Then
-\delta_{2}\leq t_{0}<t^{*}<0
and, also in this case, (
136
) and (
137
) are satisfied.

We now show that this leads to a contradiction. In case
\psi(a)-\psi(\hat{a})>0
, by using the properties of
E^{\psi}_{j}
, (
131
), (
133
), (
126
), the definition of
\beta
and the fact that
t^{*}<t_{0}
, we deduce

\displaystyle-(\psi(E_{j}^{\psi}(a,t^{*}))-

\displaystyle\psi(E_{j}^{\psi}(\hat{a},t^{*})))=-(\psi(a)-\psi(\hat{a}))-\int_{0}^{t^{*}}\left(\omega_{j}(E_{j}^{\psi}(a,r))-\omega_{j}(E_{j}^{\psi}(\hat{a},r))\right)\,\de\!r

\displaystyle\leq-3(\eta_{j1}-y_{j1})^{1/2}\delta+|t^{*}|\,\max_{r\in[0,t^{*}]}\beta\left(|E_{j}^{\psi}(a,r)-E_{j}^{\psi}(\hat{a},r)|\right)

\displaystyle\leq-3(\eta_{j1}-y_{j1})^{1/2}\delta+|t^{*}|\beta\left(|\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}|+2|t^{*}|(|\xi|+M_{1})\right)

\displaystyle\leq-3(\eta_{j1}-y_{j1})^{1/2}\delta+|t^{*}|\,\beta\left(|\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}|+2(|\xi|+M_{1})\frac{|\eta_{j1}-y_{j1}|^{1/2}}{\delta}\right)

\displaystyle\leq-3(\eta_{j1}-y_{j1})^{1/2}\delta+(\eta_{j1}-y_{j1})^{1/2}\delta\,\frac{\beta\left(C(\eta_{j1}-y_{j1})^{1/4}\right)}{\delta^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq(-3+1)(\eta_{j1}-y_{j1})^{1/2}\delta<0.

Similarly, if
\psi(a)-\psi(\hat{a})<0
, then

\displaystyle\psi(E_{j}^{\psi}(a,t^{*}))-\psi(E_{j}^{\psi}(\hat{a},t^{*}))

\displaystyle=\psi(a)-\psi(\hat{a})+\int_{0}^{t^{*}}\left(\omega_{j}(E_{j}^{\psi}(a,r))-\omega_{j}(E_{j}^{\psi}(\hat{a},r))\right)\de\!r

\displaystyle\leq-3(\eta_{j1}-y_{j1})^{1/2}\delta+|t^{*}|\,\max_{r\in[-t^{*},0]}\beta\left(|E_{j}^{\psi}(a,r)-E_{j}^{\psi}(\hat{a},r)|\right),

\displaystyle<0.

Hence, in both cases we have
\psi(E_{j}^{\psi}(a,t^{*}))\neq\psi(E_{j}^{\psi}(\hat{a},t^{*}))
that is in contradiction with (
137
), so
(
129
)
follows.

Third step.

Fix
\ell,s
with
1<s<\ell\leq m
, denote by
M_{2}\coloneqq\|\omega\|_{L^{\infty}(I^{\prime})}
and define

(138)

G_{\ell s}(\varrho)\coloneqq 2\sqrt{M_{2}}\alpha_{\ell 1}(4M_{2}\varrho)+2\sqrt{M_{2}}\alpha_{s1}(4M_{2}\varrho)+2\beta(C_{0}\varrho^{1/2}),

where
\alpha_{\ell 1}
and
\alpha_{s1}
are defined as in (
124
) for
j=\ell
and
j=s
, respectively,
\beta
is an increasing modulus of uniform continuity of
\omega
on
I^{\prime}
and
C_{0}>0
is a suitable constant, only depending on the supremum norm of
\omega
on
I^{\prime}
, that will be determined later.

We want to show that

(139)

\frac{|\psi(a)-\psi(\hat{a})|}{|\eta_{\ell s}-y_{\ell s}|^{1/2}}\leq G_{\ell s}(\varrho),

for every sufficiently small
\varrho>0
, every
a=(\xi,\eta),\hat{a}=(\xi,y)\in I
, such that
\eta_{k\tau}=y_{k\tau}
for every
(k,\tau)\neq(\ell,s)
and
0<|\eta_{\ell s}-y_{\ell s}|\leq\varrho
. Denote by
T_{\ell s}\coloneqq|\eta_{\ell s}-y_{\ell s}|^{1/2}
. We will need to possibly shrink
I
in a way that will be clear from the proof.

Rough idea of the proof.
We build a concatenation of integral curves of the vector fields
D^{\psi}_{\ell}
and
D^{\psi}_{s}
that joins
\hat{a}
