and a suitable point
a_{4}
that that can be connected to
a
with two vertical lines on which we can use the result of the second step of this proof. We start from
\hat{a}
and follow the integral curve of
D^{\psi}_{\ell}
for a time
T_{\ell s}
and we follow the integral curve of
D^{\psi}_{s}
for the same time
T_{\ell s}
; finally, we repeat the same procedure but for time
-T_{\ell s}
.
At the end of this process, we obtain a point with three different vertical components with respect to
\hat{a}
: two increments are given by the non linear terms
-\psi\partial_{\ell 1}
and
-\psi\partial_{s1}
coming respectively from
D^{\psi}_{\ell}
and from
D^{\psi}_{s}
and one increment is given by the commutator
[X_{\ell},X_{s}]
(which is
Y_{\ell s}
). In particular, whenever
\eta_{\ell s}-y_{\ell s}>0
, the
(\ell,s)
-component becomes equal to the
(\ell,s)
-component of
a
, that is
\eta_{\ell s}
. Vice versa, if
\eta_{\ell s}-y_{\ell s}<0
, one has to replace the times
\pm T_{\ell s}
with
\mp T_{\ell s}
.
In the end, we complete the proof by using the estimate of the second step of this proof applied to
a_{4}
and
a
. The desired estimates come by using Lagrange’s Theorem.

Assume
\eta_{\ell s}-y_{\ell s}>0
. Then we construct the following chain of points.

(140)

\begin{split}\hat{a}&\to_{E_{\ell}^{\psi}(\hat{a})}\,a_{1}\coloneqq E_{\ell}^{\psi}(\hat{a},T_{\ell s})\to_{E_{s}^{\psi}(a_{1})}\,a_{2}\coloneqq E_{s}^{\psi}(a_{1},T_{\ell s})\\
&\to_{E_{\ell}^{\psi}(a_{2})}\,a_{3}\coloneqq E_{\ell}^{\psi}(a_{2},-T_{\ell s})\to_{E_{s}^{\psi}(a_{3})}\,a_{4}\coloneqq E_{s}^{\psi}(a_{3},-T_{\ell s}),\\
\end{split}

where we recall that
E_{\ell}^{\psi}
and
E_{s}^{\psi}
are the integral curves of the vector fields
D^{\psi}_{\ell}
and
D^{\psi}_{s}
, respectively, given by the fact that
D^{\psi}\psi=\omega
in the broad* sense. In particular,
E_{\ell}^{\psi}
and
E_{s}^{\psi}
satisfy (
127
).

In case
\eta_{\ell s}-y_{\ell s}<0
we repeat the same construction by replacing
\pm T_{\ell s}
with
\mp T_{\ell s}
. In both cases, we have that
a_{4}\eqqcolon(\xi,y^{(a_{4})})
is such that
y^{(a_{4})}_{\ell s}=\eta_{\ell s}
. Indeed, if
\eta_{\ell s}-y_{\ell s}>0
, then

y^{(a_{4})}_{k\tau}=\begin{cases}\eta_{\ell s},&\mbox{if }(k,\tau)=(\ell,s),\\
\displaystyle\eta_{\ell 1}-\int_{0}^{T_{\ell s}}\psi(E_{\ell}^{\psi}(\hat{a},t))\de\!t+\int_{0}^{T_{\ell s}}\psi(E_{\ell}^{\psi}(a_{2},t))\de\!t,&\mbox{if }(k,\tau)=(\ell,1),\\
\displaystyle\eta_{s1}-\int_{0}^{T_{\ell s}}\psi(E_{s}^{\psi}(a_{1},t))\de\!t+\int_{0}^{T_{\ell s}}\psi(E_{s}^{\psi}(a_{3},t))\de\!t,&\mbox{if }(k,\tau)=(s,1),\\
y_{k\tau},&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}

We can assume that
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}\in I
. Indeed, this can be done because for a sufficiently small
\varrho
only depending on the supremum norms of
\varphi
and
\omega
on
I^{\prime}
, we can possibly reduce
I
to some
I_{0}
so that all the curves as in (
140
) starting in
I_{0}
, and living for times bounded above by
\varrho
, lie inside
I
.

Let
a_{5}=(\xi,y^{(a_{5})})
be a point that has the same components of
a
, except for position
(\ell,1)
for which
y^{(a_{5})}_{\ell 1}=y^{(a_{4})}_{\ell 1}
. As remarked above, we can assume without loss of generality that also
a_{5}\in I
. Moreover, we can estimate
|\psi(a)-\psi(\hat{a})|
as follows:

(141)

\displaystyle\left|\psi(a)-\psi(\hat{a})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\psi(a)-\psi(a_{5})\right|+\left|\psi(a_{5})-\psi(a_{4})\right|+\left|\psi(a_{4})-\psi(\hat{a})\right|.

We start by considering
|\psi(a)-\psi(a_{5})|
. Evaluating (
129
) for
j=\ell
, we get that

\displaystyle\left|\psi(a)-\psi(a_{5})\right|

\displaystyle\leq|\eta_{\ell 1}-y^{(a_{5})}_{\ell 1}|^{1/2}\alpha_{\ell 1}(|\eta_{\ell 1}-y^{(a_{5})}_{\ell 1}|),

and we also notice that

|\eta_{\ell 1}-y^{(a_{5})}_{\ell 1}|=\left|\int_{0}^{T_{\ell s}}\psi(E_{\ell}^{\psi}(\hat{a},t))\de\!t-\int_{0}^{T_{\ell s}}\psi(E_{\ell}^{\psi}(a_{2},t))\de\!t\right|.

Recalling that
M_{2}=\|\omega\|_{L^{\infty}(I^{\prime})}
, we aim to show that

(142)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{T_{\ell s}}\psi(E_{\ell}^{\psi}(\hat{a},t))\de\!t-\int_{0}^{T_{\ell s}}\psi(E_{\ell}^{\psi}(a_{2},t))\de\!t\right|\leq 4M_{2}T_{\ell s}^{2},

that would imply

(143)

\displaystyle\left|\psi(a)-\psi(a_{5})\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{M_{2}}T_{\ell s}\alpha_{\ell 1}(|\eta_{\ell 1}-y^{(a_{5})}_{\ell 1}|)\leq 2\sqrt{M_{2}}T_{\ell s}\alpha_{\ell 1}(4M_{2}T_{\ell s}^{2}).

We first observe that

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{T_{\ell s}}\psi(E_{\ell}^{\psi}(\hat{a},t))\de\!t-\int_{0}^{T_{\ell s}}\psi(E_{\ell}^{\psi}(a_{2},t))\de\!t\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{0}^{T_{\ell s}}\left(\psi(E_{\ell}^{\psi}(\hat{a},t))-\psi(\hat{a})\right)\de\!t-\int_{0}^{T_{\ell s}}\left(\psi(E_{\ell}^{\psi}(a_{2},t))-\psi(a_{2})\right)\de\!t+T_{\ell s}\left((\psi(\hat{a})-\psi(a_{2}))\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T_{\ell s}}\left|\psi(E_{\ell}^{\psi}(\hat{a},t))-\psi(\hat{a})\right|\de\!t+\int_{0}^{T_{\ell s}}\left|\psi(E_{\ell}^{\psi}(a_{2},t))-\psi(a_{2})\right|\de\!t+T_{\ell s}|\psi(\hat{a})-\psi(a_{2})|.

Recalling that for every
t
in the interval of definition of the curve
E^{\psi}_{\ell}
, one has

\frac{\de}{\de\!s}_{|s=t}\psi(E_{\ell}^{\psi}(A,s))=\omega_{\ell}(E_{\ell}^{\psi}(A,t))
for
A=\hat{a},a_{2}
, by exploiting the fundamental theorem of Calculus the previous estimate then yields

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{T_{\ell s}}\psi(E_{\ell}^{\psi}(\hat{a},t))\de\!t-\int_{0}^{T_{\ell s}}\psi(E_{\ell}^{\psi}(a_{2},t))\de\!t\right|\leq 2M_{2}T^{2}_{\ell s}+T_{\ell s}|\psi(\hat{a})-\psi(a_{2})|.

By Lagrange’s Theorem one also gets

\displaystyle|\psi(\hat{a})-\psi(a_{2})|\leq|\psi(\hat{a})-\psi(a_{1})|+|\psi(a_{1})-\psi(a_{2})|=T_{\ell s}|\omega_{\ell}(b^{*}_{0})|+T_{\ell s}|\omega_{s}(b^{*}_{1})|\leq 2M_{2}T_{\ell s},

where
b^{*}_{0}
and
b^{*}_{1}
are two points on
E_{\ell}^{\psi}(\hat{a},[0,T_{\ell s}])
and
E_{s}^{\psi}(a_{1},[0,T_{\ell s}])
, respectively. Hence, combining together the last two estimates, one obtains (
142
) and, consequently, also (
143
) holds.

Now we consider
|\psi(a_{5})-\psi(a_{4})|
. Since
|a_{5}-a_{4}|=|y^{(a_{5})}_{s1}-y^{(a_{4})}_{s1}|
, analogously to the previous case, one obtains

(144)

\left|\psi(a_{5})-\psi(a_{4})\right|\leq 2\sqrt{M_{2}}T_{\ell s}\alpha_{s1}(|y^{(a_{5})}_{s1}-y^{(a_{4})}_{s1}|)\leq 2\sqrt{M_{2}}T_{\ell s}\alpha_{s1}(4{M_{2}}T_{\ell s}^{2}),

where
\alpha_{s1}
is defined as in (
124
) and

|y^{(a_{5})}_{s1}-y^{(a_{4})}_{s1}|=\left|\int_{0}^{T_{\ell s}}\psi(E_{s}^{\psi}(a_{1},t))\de\!t-\int_{0}^{T_{\ell s}}\psi(E_{s}^{\psi}(a_{3},t))\de\!t\right|.

Eventually, we estimate
\psi(a_{4})-\psi(\hat{a})
in the following way:

\displaystyle\left|\psi(a_{4})-\psi(\hat{a})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|(\psi(a_{4})-\psi(a_{3}))+(\psi(a_{3})-\psi(a_{2}))+(\psi(a_{2})-\psi(a_{1}))+(\psi(a_{1})-\psi(\hat{a}))\right|

\displaystyle=|-T_{\ell s}\omega_{s}(a^{*}_{3})-T_{\ell s}\omega_{\ell}(a^{*}_{2})+T_{\ell s}\omega_{s}(a^{*}_{1})+T_{\ell s}\omega_{\ell}(a^{*}_{0})|

\displaystyle\leq T_{\ell s}(|\omega_{s}(a^{*}_{1})-\omega_{s}(a^{*}_{3})|+|\omega_{\ell}(a^{*}_{2})-\omega_{\ell}(a^{*}_{0})|)

\displaystyle\leq T_{\ell s}\left(\beta(|a^{*}_{1}-a^{*}_{3}|)+\beta(|a^{*}_{2}-a^{*}_{0}|)\right),

where
a^{*}_{0}\in E_{\ell}^{\psi}(\hat{a},[0,T_{\ell s}]),a^{*}_{1}\in E_{s}^{\psi}(a_{1},[0,T_{\ell s}]),a^{*}_{2}\in E_{\ell}^{\psi}(a_{2},[-T_{\ell s},0])
and
a^{*}_{3}\in E_{s}^{\psi}(a_{3},[-T_{\ell s},0])
are chosen to fulfill the conditions of Lagrange’s Theorem. By simple estimates relying on the triangle inequality, we get a constant
C_{0}>0
, only depending on the supremum norm of
\omega
on
I^{\prime}
, such that
|a^{*}_{1}-a^{*}_{3}|\leq C_{0}T_{\ell s}
, and
|a^{*}_{2}-a^{*}_{0}|\leq C_{0}T_{\ell s}
. Hence

(145)

\displaystyle\left|\psi(a_{4})-\psi(\hat{a})\right|

\displaystyle\leq T_{\ell s}\left(\beta(|a^{*}_{1}-a^{*}_{3}|)+\beta(|a^{*}_{2}-a^{*}_{0}|)\right)\leq 2T_{\ell s}\beta(C_{0}T_{\ell s}).

By combining (
141
) with (
143
), (
144
) and (
145
) and recalling that
T_{\ell s}=|\eta_{\ell s}-y_{\ell s}|^{1/2}
and
|\eta_{\ell s}-y_{\ell s}|<\varrho
, we thus get (
139
). Indeed, one has

\displaystyle\frac{\psi(a)-\psi(\hat{a})}{|\eta_{\ell s}-y_{\ell s}|^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\sqrt{M_{2}}T_{\ell s}\alpha_{\ell 1}(4M_{2}T_{\ell s}^{2})+2\sqrt{M_{2}}T_{\ell s}\alpha_{s1}(4M_{2}T_{\ell s}^{2})+2T_{\ell s}\beta(C_{0}T_{\ell s})}{|\eta_{\ell s}-y_{\ell s}|^{1/2}}

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{M_{2}}\Big(\alpha_{\ell 1}(4M_{2}|\eta_{\ell s}-y_{\ell s}|)+\alpha_{s1}(4M_{2}|\eta_{\ell s}-y_{\ell s}|)\Big)+2\beta(C_{0}|\eta_{\ell s}-y_{\ell s}|^{1/2})

\displaystyle\leq G_{\ell s}(\varrho).

Finally, since
G_{\ell s}
is defined as sum of continuous maps that are
0
at
0
, it follows that, if
\varrho\to 0
, then
G_{\ell s}(\varrho)\to 0
for which we get (
123
) also for all
(\ell,s)
with
s\neq 1
and the proof is complete.
∎

6.2.
Regularity results for broad* solutions in Carnot groups of step 2

In this section we see how to generalize
Theorem

6.6
, valid for free Carnot groups of step
2
, to any Carnot group of step 2. We adapt some techniques already exploited in
[
LDPS19
]
. More precisely, in
Section

6.2
we prove that the broad* condition lifts from
\mathbb{G}
to the free group
\mathbb{F}
with same step and rank of
\mathbb{G}
. In
Section

6.2
we show that the vertically broad* hölder regularity on
\mathbb{F}
implies the vertically broad* hölder regularity on
\mathbb{G}
. These two facts will put us in a position to prove
Theorem

6.17
by exploiting
Theorem

4.17
and
Theorem

6.6
.

We here introduce Carnot groups of step 2 and we refer the reader to
[
BLU07
, Chapter 3]
.
We denote with
m
the rank of
\mathbb{G}
and we identify
\mathbb{G}
with
(\mathbb{R}^{m+h},\cdot)
. If
q\in\mathbb{G}
, we write
q=(x,y)
meaning that
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{h}
. The group operation
\cdot
between two elements
q=(x,y)
and
q^{\prime}=(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
is given by

(146)

q\cdot q^{\prime}=\left(x+x^{\prime},y+y^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{B}x,x^{\prime}\rangle\right),

where
\langle\mathcal{B}x,x^{\prime}\rangle\coloneqq(\langle\mathcal{B}^{(1)}x,x^{\prime}\rangle,\dots,\langle\mathcal{B}^{(h)}x,x^{\prime}\rangle)
and
\mathcal{B}^{(i)}
are linearly independent and skew-symmetric matrices in
\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
, for
i=1,\dots,h
.

For any
i=1,\dots,h
and any
j,\ell=1,\dots,m
, denote by
(\mathcal{B}^{(i)})_{j\ell}\eqqcolon(b_{j\ell}^{(i)})
, and define
m+h
linearly independent left-invariant vector fields by setting

\displaystyle X^{\prime}_{j}(p)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\partial_{x_{j}}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{h}\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}b_{j\ell}^{(i)}x_{\ell}\,\partial_{y_{i}},\quad\mbox{ for }j=1,\dots,m,

\displaystyle Y^{\prime}_{i}(p)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\partial_{y_{i}},\,\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\mbox{ for }i=1,\dots,h.

The ordered set
(X^{\prime}_{1},\dots,X^{\prime}_{m},Y^{\prime}_{1},\dots,Y^{\prime}_{h})
is an adapted basis of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
of
\mathbb{G}
. Using the skew-symmetry of
\mathcal{B}
, it easy to see that

(147)

[X^{\prime}_{j},X^{\prime}_{\ell}]=\sum_{i=1}^{h}b_{j\ell}^{(i)}Y^{\prime}_{i},\quad\mbox{and}\quad[X^{\prime}_{j},Y^{\prime}_{i}]=0,\quad\forall j,\ell=1,\dots,m\;\text{ and }\;\forall i=1,\dots,h.

Remark 6.7
.

When
\mathbb{G}
is a free Carnot group of step
2
with coordinate representation defined as in
Section

6.1
, we denote the matrices of the beginning of
Section

6.2
with
\mathcal{B}^{(i)}\eqqcolon\mathcal{B}^{(\ell,s)}
with
1\leq s<\ell\leq m
. The composition law
(
146
)
also tells us that
\mathcal{B}^{(\ell,s)}
has entry
1
in position
(\ell,s)
,
-1
in position
(s,\ell)
and
0
elsewhere.

Since the space of skew-symmetric
m
-dimensional matrices has dimension
\frac{m(m-1)}{2}
, in any Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
of step 2, the dimensions
m
of the horizontal layer and
h
of the vertical layer are related by the inequality

h\leq\frac{m(m-1)}{2},

and
\mathbb{G}
is free if and only if
h=\frac{m(m-1)}{2}
.

From now on
\mathbb{G}
is a Carnot group of rank
m
and step
2
, with the coordinate representation previously discussed, and
\mathbb{F}
is the free Carnot group of step 2 and rank
m
with the coordinate representation as in
Section

6.1
and
Section

6.1
. We denote by
(X_{1},\dots,X_{m})
a basis of the first layer of the Lie algebra of
\mathbb{F}
. By definition of free Lie algebra, there exists a Lie group surjective homomorphism
\pi\colon\mathbb{F}\to\mathbb{G}
such that
\pi_{*}(X_{\ell})=X^{\prime}_{\ell}
for any
\ell=1,\dots,m
(see e.g.,
[
LDPS19
, Section 6]
).

If we consider on
\mathbb{F}
and
\mathbb{G}
the Carnot-Carathéodory metrics
d_{\mathbb{F}}
and
d_{\mathbb{G}}
respectively, the map
\pi
preserves the length of horizontal curves, so it is Lipschitz with

{\rm Lip}(\pi)=1
. The following lemma is well-known. We refer the reader to
[
LDPS19
, Lemma 6.1]
for a proof.

Lemma 6.8
.

For any
p\in\mathbb{F}
and any
q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{G}
, there exists
p^{\prime}\in\pi^{-1}(q^{\prime})
such that

d_{\mathbb{F}}(p,p^{\prime})=d_{\mathbb{G}}(\pi(p),q^{\prime}).

Recall that the dimension of
\mathbb{F}
is
n=m+\frac{m(m-1)}{2}
. From the definition of
\pi
, we notice that it preserves the horizontal coordinates, namely, for any
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, there exists
y^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{h}
such that

(148)

\pi(x,y)=(x,y^{*}).

We denote by
\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{G}}
and
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{G}}
two complementary subgroups of
\mathbb{G}
with
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{G}}
horizontal and one-dimensional. Similarly to
Section

6.1
, by means of exponential coordinates we identify them with
\mathbb{R}^{m+h-1}
and
\mathbb{R}
by setting

(149)

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{G}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(x_{1},0\dots,0)\,:\,x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}\},

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{G}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(0,x_{2},\dots,x_{m},y_{1},\dots,y_{h})\,:\,x_{i},y_{k}\in\mathbb{R}\mbox{ for }i=2,\dots,m,\,k=1,\dots h\}.

Remark 6.9
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a Carnot group of step 2 and
\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{G}}
,
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{G}}
be the complementary subgroups of
\mathbb{G}
defined as in (
149
). Then, according to
Section

3.1
the projected vector fields relative to a continuous
\varphi\colon U\subseteq\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{G}}\to\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{G}}
, with
U
open, are given by

(150)

\displaystyle D_{j}^{\varphi}

\displaystyle=\partial_{x_{j}}-\sum_{i=1}^{h}\left(b_{j1}^{(i)}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=2}^{m}x_{k}b_{jk}^{(i)}\right)\partial_{y_{i}}={X^{\prime}_{j}}_{|U}-\sum_{i=1}^{h}b_{j1}^{(i)}\varphi{Y^{\prime}_{i}}_{|U},\quad\mbox{ for }j=2,\dots,m,

\displaystyle D^{\varphi}_{i}

\displaystyle=\partial_{y_{i}}={Y^{\prime}_{i}}_{|U},\qquad\mbox{ for }i=1,\dots,h.

Now let
\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{F}}
and
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{F}}
be the complementary subgroups of
\mathbb{F}
defined as in (
119
). Then
\pi_{|_{\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{F}}}}\colon\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{F}}\to\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{G}}
is an isomorphism and more precisely, with our identification, we can assume it is the identity, see (
148
). Moreover, by (
148
), it follows that
\pi_{|_{\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{F}}}}\colon\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{F}}\to\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{G}}
is onto.

Since
\pi
is a Lie group homomorphism, its differential is a Lie algebra homomorphism. Hence, for any
1\leq s<\ell\leq m
, one also has

\pi_{*}(Y_{\ell s})=\pi_{*}([X_{\ell},X_{s}])=[\pi_{*}(X_{\ell}),\pi_{*}(X_{s})]=[X^{\prime}_{\ell},X^{\prime}_{s}]=\sum_{i=1}^{h}b_{\ell s}^{(i)}Y^{\prime}_{i},

where we used (
118
), (
147
) and
\pi_{*}(X_{j})=X^{\prime}_{j}
. We can therefore write the following formula

(151)

\begin{split}&\pi(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},y_{21},\dots,y_{m(m-1)})=(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},y^{*}_{1},\dots,y^{*}_{h}),\quad\text{where}\\
&y^{*}_{i}=\sum_{1\leq s<\ell\leq m}b_{\ell s}^{(i)}y_{\ell s},\quad\forall i=1,\dots,h.\\
\end{split}

Proposition 6.10
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a Carnot group of step 2 and let
\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{G}}
and
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{G}}
be two complementary subgroups of
\mathbb{G}
, with
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{G}}
horizontal and one-dimensional and choose coordinates such that (
149
) is satisfied. Let
\mathbb{F}
be the free Carnot group of step 2, rank
m
and let
\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{F}}
and
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{F}}
be the complementary subgroups of
\mathbb{F}
satisfying the identification (
119
).

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{G}}
be an open set and denote by
{V}\subseteq\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{F}}
the open set defined by
V\coloneqq\pi^{-1}(U)
. Let
{\varphi}\colon{U}\to\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{G}}
be a continuous map and let
\psi\colon{V}\to\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{F}}
be the map defined as

{\psi}\coloneqq\pi^{-1}\circ{\varphi}\circ\pi_{|_{V}}.

Assume there exists
\omega\in C(U;\mathbb{R}^{m-1})
such that
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
in the broad* sense.

Then
\psi
is a broad* solution in
V
of the system
D^{{\psi}}{\psi}=\omega\circ\pi
.

Proof.

Fix
j=2,\dots,m
. By (
120
), we have

(152)

\displaystyle D_{X_{j}}^{\psi}

\displaystyle=\partial_{x_{j}}-\psi\partial_{j1}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j<l\leq m}x_{\ell}\partial_{\ell j}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{1<s<j}x_{s}\partial_{js},

and by (
150
), it follows

(153)

\displaystyle D_{X^{\prime}_{j}}^{\varphi}

\displaystyle=\partial_{x_{j}}-\sum_{i=1}^{h}\left(b_{j1}^{(i)}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=2}^{m}x_{k}b_{jk}^{(i)}\right)\partial_{y_{i}}.

Let
a=(0,x_{2},\dots,x_{m},\eta_{21},\dots,\eta_{m(m-1)})\in V
, and denote with
b\coloneqq\pi(a)
the point in
\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{G}}
with coordinates
b=(0,x_{2},\dots,x_{m},y^{*}_{1},\dots,y^{*}_{h})
. Let
\delta>0
and let
\gamma_{j}\colon[-\delta,\delta]\to U
be an arbitrary integral curve of
D_{X^{\prime}_{j}}^{\varphi}
starting from
b
such that

(154)

\varphi(\gamma_{j}(t))-\varphi(b)=\int_{0}^{t}\omega_{j}(\gamma_{j}(s))\de\!s,\quad\forall t\in[-\delta,\delta].

Recall that by (
151
), there is an explicit relation between the coordinates of
a
and the coordinates of
b
. We are going to prove that we can lift
\gamma_{j}
to an integral curve
\zeta_{j}
of
D^{\psi}_{X_{j}}
starting from
a
, defined on
[-\delta,\delta]
and with values in
V
, that satisfies

\psi(\zeta_{j}(t))-\psi(a)=\int_{0}^{t}(\omega_{j}\circ\pi)(\zeta_{j}(s))\,\de\!s,\quad\forall t\in[-\delta,\delta].

Indeed, let
\zeta_{j}\colon[-\delta,\delta]\to\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{F}}
be defined as

(155)

\begin{split}\zeta_{j}(t)&=(0,x_{2},\dots,x_{j-1},x_{j}+t,x_{j+1},\dots,x_{m},\eta_{21}(t),\dots,\eta_{m(m-1)}(t)),\quad\text{where}\\
\eta_{\ell s}(t)&=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\eta_{j1}-\int_{0}^{t}\varphi(\gamma_{j}(r))\de\!r,&\mbox{for }(\ell,s)=(j,1),\\
\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}tx_{\ell}+\eta_{\ell j},&\mbox{for }s=j\,\text{ and }\,j<\ell\leq m,\\
\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}tx_{s}+\eta_{js},&\mbox{for }\ell=j\,\text{ and }\,1<s<j,\\
\eta_{\ell s},&\mbox{otherwise,}\end{cases}\end{split}

for
t\in[-\delta,\delta]
. By definition, one immediately gets
\zeta_{j}(0)=a
. We are left to prove the following facts.

(i)

\pi\circ\zeta_{j}=\gamma_{j}
;

(ii)

\zeta_{j}\colon[-\delta,\delta]\to V
is an integral curve of
D_{X_{j}}^{\psi}
;

(iii)

For every
t\in[-\delta,\delta]
one has

\psi(\zeta_{j}(t))-\psi(a)=\int_{0}^{t}(\omega_{j}\circ\pi)(\zeta_{j}(s))\de\!s.

(i). By (
153
), and the fact that
\gamma_{j}(0)=b=(0,x_{2},\dots,x_{m},y^{*}_{1},\dots,y^{*}_{h})
, we can explicitly write
\gamma_{j}
, exploiting the fact that it is an integral curve of
D^{\varphi}_{X_{j}^{\prime}}
, and get

(156)

\begin{split}\gamma_{j}(t)&=(0,x_{2},\dots,x_{j-1},x_{j}+t,x_{j+1},\dots,x_{m},y^{*}_{1}(t),\dots,y^{*}_{h}(t)),\quad\text{where}\\
y^{*}_{i}(t)&=y^{*}_{i}-\frac{1}{2}t\left(\sum_{k=2}^{m}x_{k}b_{jk}^{(i)}\right)-b_{j1}^{(i)}\int_{0}^{t}\varphi(\gamma_{j}(r))\de\!r,\qquad\forall i=1,\dots,h,\text{ and }\,\forall t\in[-\delta,\delta].\\
\end{split}

Using the explicit formula of
\pi
given in (
151
), we obtain that

(157)

\begin{split}&\pi(\zeta_{j}(t))=(0,x_{2},\dots,x_{j-1},x_{j}+t,x_{j+1},\dots,x_{m},\eta^{*}_{1}(t),\dots,\eta^{*}_{h}(t)),\quad\text{where}\\
&\eta^{*}_{i}(t)=\sum_{1\leq s<\ell\leq m}b_{\ell s}^{(i)}\eta_{\ell s}(t),\qquad\forall i=1,\dots,h,\text{ and }\,\forall t\in[-\delta,\delta].\\
\end{split}

In particular, by (
155
), we have that

\begin{split}\eta^{*}_{i}(t)&=b_{j1}^{(i)}\left(\eta_{j1}-\int_{0}^{t}\varphi(\gamma_{j}(r))\de\!r\right)+\sum_{j<\ell\leq m}b_{\ell j}^{(i)}\left(\frac{1}{2}tx_{\ell}+\eta_{\ell j}\right)\\
&\quad+\sum_{1<s<j}b_{js}^{(i)}\left(-\frac{1}{2}tx_{s}+\eta_{js}\right)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq s<\ell\leq m\\
s\neq j,\ell\neq j\end{subarray}}b_{\ell s}^{(i)}\eta_{\ell s},\end{split}

for every
i=1,\dots,h
and
t\in[-\delta,\delta]
. Using again (
151
), we also notice that
y^{*}_{i}=\sum_{1\leq s<\ell\leq m}b_{\ell s}^{(i)}\eta_{\ell s}
, and, by the skew-symmetry of
\mathcal{B}^{(i)}
, we deduce that

\begin{split}\eta^{*}_{i}(t)&=y^{*}_{i}-\frac{1}{2}t\left(\sum_{k=2}^{m}x_{k}b_{jk}^{(i)}\right)-b_{j1}^{(i)}\int_{0}^{t}\varphi(\gamma_{j}(r))\de\!r,\qquad\forall i=1,\dots,h\text{ and }\,\forall t\in[-\delta,\delta].\\
\end{split}

Comparing (
156
), (
157
) and the last equality, we get
\pi\circ\zeta_{j}=\gamma_{j}
, as desired.

(ii). We now check that
\zeta_{j}
is an integral curve of
D_{X_{j}}^{\psi}
. Recalling (
152
), and by the definition of the components of
\zeta_{j}
in (
155
), it suffices to check its
(j,1)
-coordinate, since for all the others is trivial. Observe that, from (i),
(\varphi\circ\gamma_{j})(t)=(\varphi\circ\pi\circ\zeta_{j})(t)=(\psi\circ\zeta_{j})(t),
for every
t\in[-\delta,\delta]
, and so

\eta_{j1}(t)=\eta_{j1}-\int_{0}^{t}\varphi(\gamma_{j}(r))\de\!r=\eta_{j1}-\int_{0}^{t}\psi(\zeta_{j}(r))\,\de\!r,\qquad\forall t\in[-\delta,\delta],

as desired. Notice that we have used that
\pi_{|_{\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{F}}}}
identifies
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{F}}
with
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{G}}
.

(iii). We first notice that, since
{U}\supseteq\gamma_{j}([-\delta,\delta])=(\pi\circ\zeta_{j})([-\delta,\delta])
and
V=\pi^{-1}(U)
, then also
\zeta_{j}([-\delta,\delta])\subseteq{V}
.

Using
\pi\circ\zeta_{j}=\gamma_{j}
and the fact that
\gamma_{j}
satisfies (
154
), we finally obtain

(158)

\psi(\zeta_{j}(t))-\psi(a)=\int_{0}^{t}(\omega_{j}\circ\pi)(\zeta_{j}(s))\de\!s,\qquad\forall t\in[-\delta,\delta].

We thus showed that every integral curve of
D^{\varphi}_{X^{\prime}_{j}}
satisfying (
154
) can be lifted to an integral curve of
D^{\psi}_{X_{j}}
satisfying (
158
), with a procedure that is patently local. Thus, by using
Section

3.4
, it follows that, if
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
holds in the broad* sense on
U
, then
D^{\psi}\psi=\omega\circ\pi
holds in the broad* sense on
V
.∎

Proposition 6.11
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a Carnot group of step 2 and let
\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{G}}
and
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{G}}
be two complementary subgroups of
\mathbb{G}
, with
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{G}}
horizontal and one-dimensional and choose coordinates such that (
149
) is satisfied. Let
\mathbb{F}
be the free Carnot group of step 2, rank
m
and let
\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{F}}
and
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{F}}
be the complementary subgroups of
\mathbb{F}
satisfying the identification (
119
). Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{G}}
be an open set and denote by
{V}\subseteq\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{F}}
the open set defined by
V\coloneqq\pi^{-1}(U)
. Let
{\varphi}\colon{U}\to\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{G}}
be a continuous map and let
\psi\colon{V}\to\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{F}}
be the map defined as

{\psi}\coloneqq\pi^{-1}\circ{\varphi}\circ\pi_{|_{V}}.

Then, if
{\psi}
is vertically broad* hölder, also
\varphi
is vertically broad* hölder.

Proof.

We observe that in the case we are dealing with, the vertically broad* hölder condition is equivalent to the locally 1/2-little Hölder continuity along vertical coordinates, see the discussion before the statement of
Theorem

6.6
, that holds verbatim for arbitrary Carnot groups of step 2. Fix
b_{0}\in U
and let
a_{0}\in\pi^{-1}(b_{0})
. Since
\psi
is vertically broad* hölder, there exist two neighborhoods
V^{\prime}
and
V^{\prime\prime}
of
a_{0}
with
V^{\prime}\Subset V^{\prime\prime}\Subset V
and an increasing map
\alpha\colon(0,+\infty)\to[0,+\infty)
only depending on
V^{\prime\prime}
, such that
\lim_{\varrho\to 0}\alpha(\varrho)=0
and

(159)

\frac{|\psi(\xi,\eta)-\psi(\xi,y)|}{|\eta_{\ell s}-y_{\ell s}|^{1/2}}\leq\alpha(\varrho)

for every
(\ell,s)
such that
1\leq s<\ell\leq m
, every sufficiently small
\varrho>0
and every
(\xi,\eta),(\xi,y)\in V^{\prime}
with
\eta_{k\tau}=y_{k\tau}
for every
(k,\tau)\neq(\ell,s)
and
0<|\eta_{\ell s}-y_{\ell s}|\leq\varrho
.

Set
U^{\prime}\coloneqq\pi(V^{\prime})
so that
b_{0}\in U^{\prime}
and clearly
U^{\prime}\Subset U.

We aim to prove that there exists an increasing function
\beta\colon(0,+\infty)\to[0,+\infty)
only depending on
U^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq\pi(V^{\prime\prime})
such that
\lim_{\varrho\to 0}\beta(\varrho)=0
and

(160)

\frac{|\varphi(\xi,\eta)-\varphi(\xi,y)|}{|\eta_{i}-y_{i}|^{1/2}}\leq\beta(\varrho),

for every
i=1,\dots,h
, every sufficiently small
\varrho>0
and every
(\xi,\eta),(\xi,y)\in U^{\prime}
with
y_{k}=y_{k}
for every
k\neq i
and
0<|\eta_{i}-y_{i}|\leq\varrho
.
Fix
i=1,\dots,h
and
\varrho>0
sufficiently small and consider
b=(\xi,\eta)

\hat{b}=(\xi,y)
in
U^{\prime}
such that
\eta_{k}=y_{k}
for all
k\neq i
and
0<|y_{i}-\eta_{i}|\leq\varrho
.

Applying
Section

6.2
to the points
b_{0}
and
b

we find
a=(\xi,\eta^{*})\in\pi^{-1}(b)
such that
d_{\mathbb{G}}(b_{0},b)=d_{\mathbb{F}}(a_{0},a)
and, since
\pi
is continuous, we can also assume, up to possibly reducing
U^{\prime}
, that
a\in V^{\prime}
.
Applying again
Section

6.2
to the points
b
and
\hat{b}
, we find
\hat{a}=(\xi,y^{*})\in\pi^{-1}(\hat{b})\cap V^{\prime}
such that
d_{\mathbb{G}}(b,\hat{b})=d_{\mathbb{F}}(a,\hat{a})
. Since the horizontal components of the points
b
and
\hat{b}
are equal and the norm induced by the distance
d_{\mathbb{G}}
is equivalent to the anisotropic norm on
\mathbb{G}
, we have that
d_{\mathbb{G}}(b,\hat{b})
is equivalent to
|\eta_{i}-y_{i}|^{1/2}
. Similarly, notice that
a,\hat{a}
have the same horizontal components and by the fact that the norm induced by
d_{\mathbb{F}}
is equivalent to the anisotropic norm on
\mathbb{F}
, it follows that
|\eta^{*}-y^{*}|^{1/2}
is equivalent to
d_{\mathbb{F}}(a,\hat{a})
. In particular, we can find a geometric constant
C_{1}>0
such that
|\eta^{*}-y^{*}|^{1/2}\leq C_{1}|\eta_{i}-y_{i}|^{1/2}
.

We can then make the following estimates:

\displaystyle\frac{|\varphi(\xi,\eta)-\varphi(\xi,y)|}{|\eta_{i}-y_{i}|^{1/2}}=\frac{|\psi(\xi,\eta^{*})-\psi(\xi,y^{*})|}{|\eta^{*}-y^{*}|^{1/2}}\frac{|\eta^{*}-y^{*}|^{1/2}}{|\eta_{i}-y_{i}|^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\left(\frac{|\psi(\xi,\eta^{*})-\psi(\xi,y^{*}_{21},\eta^{*}_{31},\dots,\eta^{*}_{m(m-1)})|}{|\eta^{*}_{21}-y^{*}_{21}|^{1/2}}+\dots\right.

\displaystyle\left.\qquad\qquad\dots+\frac{|\psi(\xi,y^{*}_{21},\dots,y^{*}_{m(m-2)},\eta^{*}_{m(m-1)})-\psi(\xi,y^{*})|}{|\eta^{*}_{m(m-1)}-y^{*}_{m(m-1)}|^{1/2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\left(\alpha(|\eta^{*}_{21}-y^{*}_{21}|)+\dots+\alpha(|\eta^{*}_{m(m-1)}-y^{*}_{m(m-1)}|)\right),

where we used (
159
) and assumed without loss of generality that all the considered points in the chain belong to
V^{\prime}
.
Observe that, for any
(\ell,s)
such that
1\leq s<l\leq m
, one has
|\eta^{*}_{\ell s}-y^{*}_{\ell s}|\leq|\eta^{*}-y^{*}|\leq C_{1}^{2}|\eta_{i}-y_{i}|\leq C_{1}^{2}\varrho
, and then we can define

\displaystyle\beta(\varrho)\coloneqq C_{1}\frac{m(m-1)}{2}\alpha(C_{1}^{2}\varrho).

The previous computations have shown that

\frac{|\varphi(\xi,\eta)-\varphi(\xi,y)|}{|\eta_{i}-y_{i}|^{1/2}}\leq\beta(\varrho),

and hence (
160
) holds, completing the proof.
∎

Theorem 6.12
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
of step 2 with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and one-dimensional and choose coordinates such that (
149
) is satisfied. Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{W}
be an open set and let
\varphi\colon U\to\mathbb{L}
and
\omega\colon U\to\mathbb{R}^{m-1}
be two continuous functions such that
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
in the broad* sense.
Then
\varphi
is vertically broad* hölder.

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{F}
be the free Carnot group of step 2, rank
m
and let
\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{F}}
and
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{F}}
be the complementary subgroups of
\mathbb{F}
satisfying the identification (
119
). By
Section

6.2
, we know that
\psi\coloneqq\pi^{-1}\circ\varphi\circ\pi
is a broad* solution of
D^{{\psi}}{\psi}=\omega\circ\pi
in
V=\pi^{-1}(U)
. Then, by
Theorem

6.6
,
\psi
is vertically broad* hölder and finally, by using
Section

6.2
, we obtain the thesis.
∎

We state here some corollaries of the previous results.

Corollary 6.13
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
of step 2 with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and one-dimensional and choose coordinates on
\mathbb{G}
such that (
149
) is satisfied. Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{W}
be an open set and let
\varphi\colon U\to\mathbb{L}
and
\omega\colon U\to\mathbb{R}^{m-1}
be two continuous functions such that
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
in the broad* sense on
U
.
Then
\varphi\in{\rm UID}(U,\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
.

Proof.

It is enough to combine
Section

4.1
and
Theorem

6.12
.
∎

Corollary 6.14
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
of step 2 with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and one-dimensional and choose coordinates on
\mathbb{G}
such that (
149
) is satisfied. Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{W}
be an open set and let
\varphi\colon U\to\mathbb{L}
and
\omega\colon U\to\mathbb{R}^{m-1}
be two continuous functions such that
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
in the broad* sense on
U
. Then, the intrinsic graph of
\varphi
is a surface of class
C^{1}_{\mathrm{H}}
.

Proof.

It is enough to combine
Section

4.1
and
Theorem

6.12
.
∎

Corollary 6.15
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
of step 2 with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and one-dimensional and choose coordinates on
\mathbb{G}
such that (
149
) is satisfied. Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{W}
be an open set, and let
\varphi\colon U\subseteq\mathbb{W}\to\mathbb{L}
and
\omega\colon U\to\mathbb{R}^{m-1}
be two continuous functions. Assume that
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
in the broad* sense on
U
. Then
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
in the sense of distributions on
U
.

Proof.

It is enough to combine
Theorem

6.12
and
Section

4.2
.
∎

Remark 6.16
.

The converse implication of
Section

6.2
is, up to now, only known for Heisenberg groups, see
[
BSC10a
]
. This implication in the general step-2 case will be a subject of further investigations by means of the techniques exploited in this section.

6.3.
Main theorem in Carnot groups of step 2

Now we are in a position to give the following theorem (stated in
Theorem

1.7
), which shows that the assumption on the vertically broad* hölder regularity in
Theorem

4.17
can be dropped if we are inside a Carnot group of step 2 and
\mathbb{L}
is one-dimensional. We use the same conventions as in
Theorem

4.17
, following the notation of
Section

2.1
.

Theorem 6.17
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{L}
be complementary subgroups of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
of step 2 with
\mathbb{L}
horizontal and one-dimensional. Let
\widetilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{W}
be an open set and let
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{U}\to\mathbb{L}
be a continuous function. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(a)

\widetilde{\varphi}\in{\rm UID}(\widetilde{U},\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
;

(b)

\widetilde{\varphi}\in{\rm ID}(\widetilde{U},\mathbb{W};\mathbb{L})
and
\de^{\varphi}\!\varphi
is continuous on
\widetilde{U}
;

(c)

there exists
\omega\in C(U;\mathbb{R}^{m-1})
such that, for every
a\in U
, there exist
\delta>0
and a family of functions
\{\varphi_{\varepsilon}\in C^{1}(B(a,\delta)):\varepsilon\in(0,1)\}
such that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varphi_{\varepsilon}=\varphi\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}D_{j}^{\varphi_{\varepsilon}}\varphi_{\varepsilon}=\omega_{j}\quad\text{in $L^{\infty}(B(a,\delta))$},

for every
j=2,\dots,m
;

(d)

there exists
\omega\in C(U;\mathbb{R}^{m-1})
such that
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
in the broad sense on
U
;

(e)

there exists
\omega\in C(U;\mathbb{R}^{m-1})
such that
D^{\varphi}\varphi=\omega
in the broad* sense on
U
with the choice of coordinates of (
149
).

Proof.

(a)
\Rightarrow
(b) is trivial, by item (b) of
Section

2.3
and (b)
\Rightarrow
(a) follows from
Section

3.4
,
Theorem

6.12
and
Section

4.1
.

(a)
\Rightarrow
(c) follows from (a)
\Rightarrow
(b) of
Theorem

4.17
and (c)
\Rightarrow
(a) follows by combining
Section

4.3
and
Section

6.2
.

(d)
\Rightarrow
(e) is trivial, by
Section

3.4
. (e)
\Rightarrow
(a) follows from
Section

6.2
. (a)
\Rightarrow
(d) follows from (a)
\Rightarrow
(c) of
Theorem

4.17
.
∎
